- **A slow embedding run no longer overwrites the vectors for a newer version of the file.** If a file changed while it was being embedded, the run for the old version could finish last and replace the new vectors with stale ones. The write now checks that the file is still at the version it embedded, and skips it otherwise.

## [2.9.29] - 2026-09-25

Upgrading reparses the index. The data directory fix below is in a file the parser fingerprint covers, so the parser stamp moves for every language and each file is read once more, although nothing a parse extracts has changed. Embeddings are untouched and keep serving throughout.

### Added

- **`hint-stats --session-id <id>` reports on one session.** The report covered every session on the machine at once, so reviewing a session meant reading figures pooled with every other. `--session-id latest`, or a session id, narrows emission counts, efficacy and bytes spent to that session. Suppression and manual marks apply to every session, so they are still shown machine-wide, and saved bytes are left out of the session view because the ledger they come from records no session.
- **`doctor` also checks the Copilot hooks installed in the current project.** It runs the project's `preToolUse` hook the way Copilot would and reports a failure with the command that repairs that scope: `token-goat install --copilot --local`, or `token-goat install --vscode` when only the VS Code install wrote those hooks. It runs that command only when token-goat on this machine wrote the hooks file, which it records outside the repository, so starting `doctor` inside a cloned repository never runs a command the repository chose.
- **Hook calls answer in about 70 ms instead of 110 to 135 ms.** Every hook call used to start Node and load token-goat's hook code before any of its own work ran, and that startup was about half of each call. The first hook call now also starts a resident hook server in the background, and later calls go to it. Measured on Windows, a `pre_tool_use` call went from 109-118 ms to 68-72 ms in Claude Code, 129-134 ms to 68 ms in Codex, 131 ms to 70 ms in Copilot CLI, and 129 ms to 70 ms in VS Code chat, with the same output each time. Up to three servers run per data directory, so parallel tool calls do not queue behind one another, and a call that finds every server busy or stopping, or whose payload is over 64 MiB, runs on its own as before. A server listens only on a named pipe or a Unix socket, answers only a caller holding the key in `hook-server.key`, and exits after 30 minutes idle, after an upgrade, or when turned off. On Linux and macOS, a data directory whose path is too long for a socket name puts the socket in a directory under the temp directory that only the current user can enter, and the server is not used if anyone else can reach that directory. A server whose socket file another server has replaced exits and leaves the new socket in place. `token-goat hook-server status` and `token-goat hook-server stop` inspect and stop them, including a server in the middle of a hook call, which still answers that call, and `doctor` reports on them. Set `server = false` under `[hooks]` in the global config, or `TOKEN_GOAT_HOOK_SERVER=0`, to turn it off. A project's `.token-goat.toml` cannot, because one server answers every project.
- **Read-only commands answer faster when an agent runs them.** When a command such as `section`, `symbol` or `read` writes to a pipe rather than a terminal, a running hook server answers it, so `token-goat section` took 60 ms instead of 122 ms. Output is the same either way. A command typed in a terminal, or one that reads standard input, still runs in its own process.

### Changed

- **The MCP tool list takes 25% less of each turn's context.** A client sends the whole list to the model on every turn. It was 17,314 bytes for 18 tools and is now 13,029. Each schema dropped its `$schema` line, which the MCP spec does not need, the `minLines` field gained real bounds in place of plus and minus 2^53, and the longest descriptions were cut to what a model acts on. The `projectRoot` note, repeated in 16 tools, is now one short sentence. Every tool takes the same arguments as before.
- **`token-goat capabilities` also lists what listens for other processes on this machine.** The hook server is the one entry, under its own heading. A test fails the build if it opens anything other than the pipe or socket it names.
- **A hook call no longer starts a second Node process to hold a lock.** Saving session state takes a file lock, and every hook call saves it. Holding that lock used to start a helper process that kept the lock file's timestamp fresh, so a slow holder was not mistaken for a crashed one and robbed of its lock. The lock now checks whether its holder is still running instead, and keeps it for a live holder for up to 60 seconds. A lock whose holder has exited is taken over as before.
- **Commands and hook calls do less work when they start and when they record stats.** The version and package name are built into the bundle, so no command reads package.json to learn them. The stats database no longer waits for a disk flush after each row: its write-ahead log keeps the file intact through a crash, and a power failure can lose only the last few rows. The hook server sends each answer before it writes the call's stats row, and takes the next call as soon as that row is written, however long the previous caller takes to read its answer.

### Removed

- **`hooks.watchdog_ms`.** Nothing read it. A config file that still sets it keeps loading.

### Fixed

- **`note set` and `note unset` keep the project's other notes when the notes file is briefly locked.** On Windows, a virus scanner or search indexer can hold a file for a moment right after it is written. A note command that read the file during that moment took it as empty and saved only the note it was changing, so every other note was gone. The read now retries through a short lock the way writes already did, and a lock that does not clear fails the command with the error instead of overwriting the file. The same retry means session start and the compaction manifest still carry the notes, and `note list` reports the error rather than "(no notes set)".
- **`note set` and `note unset` refuse to save over a line they cannot read.** A line in the notes file that is not in `key = "value"` form, such as a value a hand edit continued onto a second line, was skipped when the file was read, and the next `note set` or `note unset` saved the notes without it. Both commands now stop, name the line numbers, and leave the file as it was. `note list` and session start still show the notes around such a line.
- **`note set`, `note unset` and `note clear` change nothing when they cannot take the notes lock.** When the lock was still held after the wait, or could not be created at all, the command wrote the file anyway without it, so two note commands at the same moment could lose one of the notes. A lock that could not be created is now retried briefly, and a lock that is still not available fails the command with "no note was changed" and the file untouched.
- **A session keeps its record of files already read when the session file is briefly locked.** Every hook call saves the session's state by reading the file on disk, merging in its own view, and writing the result back. On Windows, when a virus scanner or search indexer held that file for a moment, the read came back empty and the save wrote one hook call's view over it, so every file read earlier in the session was forgotten: repeat-read hints and the compaction manifest went blank partway through the session. The read now retries through a short lock, and a file that still cannot be read is left as it is.
- **MCP reads confined to the project root work on Windows under every supported Node release.** With `mcp.confine_reads_to_project_root` on, which the restrictive security profile sets, `read`, `section`, `outline`, `grep` and the other file tools refused every file as "changed identity between validation and read" when the server ran on Node 22.16, 23, 24.0 or 24.1. The gate recorded each file's identity with a path `stat` and checked it against an `fstat` of the opened file, and on Windows those Node releases report a volume serial of 0 for the first and the real serial for the second. The gate now records the identity through an open handle too, so both sides are measured the same way. `pack` had the same comparison and skipped every file as "symlink points outside project root" on those releases; it is fixed the same way.
- **Copilot CLI and VS Code hooks keep working in a repository whose package.json says `"type": "module"`.** The hook script installed into `.github/hooks` was `token-goat-shim.js`, which Node loaded there as an ES module. Its first `require` threw, and because Copilot's `preToolUse` fails closed, every tool call in that repository was denied. The script is now `token-goat-shim.cjs`, which Node always loads as CommonJS. A two-line `token-goat-shim.js` stays beside it for sessions started before the upgrade that still run the old path, and it answers `{}` and exits 0 if the `.cjs` file is missing or fails to load. `doctor` warns about an install that still runs the old file. Run `token-goat install --copilot` (or `--vscode`) again to move it over.
- **Claude Code, Codex, Grok and Kimi hooks keep working under a package.json that says `"type": "module"`.** Their hook scripts had the same fault: installed as `token-goat-shim.js`, each was loaded as an ES module whenever a package.json above the harness config, such as one in the home directory, said `"type": "module"`, and every hook call failed. They are now installed as `token-goat-shim.cjs`, with a small `token-goat-shim.js` beside each that hands off to it for sessions started before the upgrade. `doctor` warns when a Claude Code or Codex install still runs the old file. Run `token-goat install` again, adding `--codex`, `--grok` or `--kimi` for those harnesses, to move them over.
- **A data directory made read-only stays read-only.** On Linux and macOS, the permission hardening token-goat runs on its data directory and on `TOKEN_GOAT_HOME` reset a directory it found at 0555 to 0700, giving the owner back the write permission that had been removed. The next writer then created the index's `-wal` and `-shm` files with the database's read-only mode, and every writer after that failed with `attempt to write a readonly database`, even once the directory was writable again. Hardening now clears only the group and other bits.
- **A hook call that never finishes no longer stalls every later hook call in the pi, opencode and OpenClaw hosts.** Those bridges run hook calls one at a time inside the host's own process, so one call that never settled held every call behind it; on the built bundle, the call queued after a stalled one was still waiting 130 s later. A call now waits at most 120 s for the one before it and then runs. The longest of 66,625 recorded hook calls took 31 s (p99 494 ms), and a slow call still gets its own answer when it finishes.
- **In the pi, opencode and OpenClaw hosts, a hook event that names no session no longer sees the previous session's reads.** Such an event kept whatever the last session had loaded, so a file that session had read was refused as already read. It now starts from an empty session.
- **In the pi, opencode and OpenClaw hosts, each session's hooks see that session's own id.** The id came from the first session the host served and stayed for every session after it, so context-pressure checks and transcript pairing in a later session read the first one's. The id now follows each event and is cleared for an event that carries none. A session id Claude Code sets itself is never replaced.
- **In the opencode and OpenClaw hosts, every hook call is answered for that host, not only the first.** token-goat copies the event's session id into Claude Code's session variable for hosts that never set it, and harness detection then read that variable as a sign of Claude Code. The plugins serve every call from one process, so each call after the first was answered in Claude Code's wire form, and an opencode compaction received no file manifest. Detection now ignores a session id token-goat set itself.
- **A task output read by a relative path is recognized.** `cat tasks/b0gc3pltn.output` or `tail -n 20 tasks/x.output` never matched, so an agent transcript read that way went through whole while the same read by absolute path was refused. A relative path, a Python `open()` read included, is now resolved against the directory the command runs in rather than the hook's own, and the recall command names the file by its absolute path.
- **A read wrapped in a subshell meets the same read gates as the bare read.** `( cat big.txt )` and `( FOO=1 cat big.txt )` passed every Bash read gate that `cat big.txt` meets. A single group wrapping the whole command is now looked through, a `cd` inside it included; `(a) && b`, `( a ); b` and `$( ... )` are left as written.
- **Read commands answer from an index they cannot write.** Under a read-only sandbox or a data directory that denies writes, `read`, `symbol`, `semantic`, `answer` and the other lookup commands failed after about 15 s per database open with `db: failed to enable WAL mode (unable to open database file)`. They now open the index read-only, say so once on stderr and exit 0. Stats are not recorded, and a file changed since indexing is served from the older index with a stale warning saying it cannot be updated this run, `--force-refresh` included. An index written by an older token-goat is refused rather than served, because upgrading it needs write access. Indexing and the worker still fail on such an index.
- **Files a stopped worker never embedded are embedded later, without being touched again.** The worker parses a file and then queues its embedding in memory, so stopping it with that queue full dropped the queue: a stop waits five seconds for embeddings in progress, and on Windows `worker stop` ends the process without waiting at all. Each dropped file stayed out of `semantic` until something edited it. On one machine that was 25,840 of a project's 27,051 files, all left by a single large reindex. While idle, the worker now queues every file whose embedding is behind the current settings, in batches of 50 that wait for the previous batch to finish. That includes files stamped while embeddings were turned off, and files skipped under an older `indexing.large_file_symbol_only_kb`. Nothing is queued while embeddings are off or their dependencies are missing. When they come back, the worker goes through every file again from the start, even if it had already been through them all, so turning embeddings on under a running worker catches up what it indexed while they were off, however briefly. A file that is not on disk is skipped rather than queued, because the queue removes a missing file from the index, and a file on a drive that is not mounted counts as missing. A batch that could not be written to the queue is tried again on the next idle cycle.
- **`doctor` names the real reason files have no embeddings.** Its low-coverage warning called `indexing.large_file_symbol_only_kb` "the usual reason" and prescribed `token-goat index --force`. In the project above, 202 files were over that limit and 25,840 were waiting for an embedding. The warning now counts the two separately. Files still waiting get "the worker embeds them while it is idle, or run 'token-goat index' in the project to embed them now". Files over the limit get the setting and its current value.
- **`doctor`'s oversized-index warning measures what each table takes up.** It summed the lengths of four text columns, which cannot see an index or a fixed-size row. On a 4.9 GB index it reported refs as 89 MB (2%) and called 3.9 GB unmeasurable, while refs and its indexes held 2.7 GB. Each table is now measured in the pages it and its indexes occupy, so that index reads "refs 2678 MB (55%)" and "symbols and their search index 1680 MB (34%)". The warning names the command that shrinks those two, `token-goat project exclude "<root>"` for the largest project followed by `token-goat reclaim-index`, and the `indexing.max_db_size_mb` value that would stop the warning if the size is expected.
- **A damaged index fails at once instead of after 15 seconds.** Opening a database that is not SQLite, or whose file is corrupt, retried the switch to WAL mode for 15 seconds before failing, so each open took 15 s and `doctor` took 45 s to report one damaged index. Both errors now fail on the first attempt.
- **`worker start` refuses a data directory it cannot write, before starting anything.** The worker keeps its pid file, queue and index in that directory. It used to start the daemon first and fail when the pid file could not be written, leaving `worker start` hung and a daemon running that `worker stop` could not find. It now exits 1 with the directory and the reason. `doctor` reports the worker as failing for the same cause instead of only "not running".
- **`doctor` says why it could not check for duplicate MCP processes, and no longer reports a failed check as clean.** When PowerShell did not answer, the message always blamed PowerShell, whether the query had timed out, `powershell.exe` was missing, or the query had exited with an error. An error exit with nothing printed was read as an empty process list and reported as no duplicates found. The message now gives the actual reason and a command to list Node processes by hand, and an error exit is reported as a check that could not run. After a timeout it also suggests running `doctor` again once the machine is less busy.
- **A Read of part of a large tool result goes through.** Claude Code saves any Bash result over 20,000 bytes to a file and tells the model to Read it. Every such file is over the size at which token-goat refuses a whole-file Read, and a Read of a few lines was refused the same way. The command named in its place pages by head, tail or pattern, so lines cut from the preview could not be reached by line number. A Read with `offset` and `limit` covering under 20KB now goes through, and the refusal for a whole-file Read says so. A window wider than that is refused with its own size ("Lines 1-500 span 29KB"). The refused whole-file Read also counted as a read of the file, so the bounded Read that followed it was refused as a re-read of a file the model had never seen. A refused Read no longer counts.
- **A `sed` or `awk` line range that covers the whole file is refused like `cat`.** `cat SKILL.md` was refused with a pointer to `token-goat section` and `outline`, but `awk 'NR>=1 && NR<=324' SKILL.md` read the same 324-line, 54KB skill with no word. A range gets a hint only when the surgical read that replaces it costs less, and for a range over every line that read is the whole file too, so no hint was ever given. A range from line 1 to the last line, or past it, now gets the same answer as `cat`, naming the range and the file's line count. A refused range is not counted as served, and lines an earlier read did serve still get the recall line.
- **The heading list for a large skill says how to reach the headings it leaves out.** When a skill has more than 40 headings, the list shown instead of the body stops at 40 and sent the model to `token-goat skill-body` for the rest, which loads the whole body the list was standing in for. It now says the others load by name like the ones shown, and names the `token-goat outline` command that lists all of them.
- **A "did you mean" list prints one suggestion per line.** When `skill-section`, `note-add --symbol` or `insert-section --after` could not find a name, the suggestions came out as one line with a literal `\n` before each one, and so did the "Try: token-goat outline" line after a missing section. Each line now prints on a line of its own. A line break inside a heading or name still prints as `\n`, since those come from the file.
- **Read commands are no longer credited with savings no alternative could have made.** `symbol NAME` and `semantic` were credited the size of every file that matched, as if each would have been read whole, and one `symbol main` lookup was booked at 1.92M tokens. They are now credited the largest matched file, the one read a search stands in for. `refs`, `changed` and the semantic fallback over cached output were credited the full output of the shell command each replaces, though output past 20,000 bytes reaches the model as a 2KB preview, and one `refs` call was booked at 2.5M tokens. They are now priced at what the harness would have delivered, as every other shell saving already was. `stats --methodology` now states what each read command is measured against. Rows already in the ledger keep the values they were recorded with.
- **`doctor` no longer reports installed hooks as missing when token-goat is launched by another path to the same file.** A hook entry records the bundle path of whichever process ran `install`, and `doctor` compared that path by spelling. With the global npm install reached through a link, as `npm link` leaves it, a `doctor` launched through the npm shim reported all eight wired events as missing and told the user to reinstall, while the same `doctor` launched from the checkout reported none. Two spellings of one existing file now count as the same hook, in both the exec and the string form, and `install` no longer rewrites an entry that already runs the same bundle. A path to a different file still reads as stale.
- **In the pi, opencode and OpenClaw hosts, `stats --hooks` and `doctor` time each hook call from when it started.** They timed it from when the host process started, so in a long session every hook call looked as slow as the session was old.
- **`install --help` and `uninstall --help` say what a harness flag covers.** Each harness flag, such as `--codex` or `--copilot`, was described as installing its harness "also", but a harness flag installs only that harness and leaves the Claude Code integration untouched, so `install --codex --copilot` could leave an outdated Claude Code hook script in place. The help now says so. Run a bare `token-goat install` as well to update Claude Code.
- **An edited file is reindexed even when the edit reaches the queue while the worker is claiming it.** Each edit adds the file to a queue that the worker claims by renaming it. An edit written just as that rename happened landed in the claimed copy after the worker's last read of it and was deleted with it, so the file stayed stale in every read command until something edited it again. An edit that misses the live queue is now written to it again.
- **A plain search pattern is no longer refused as too slow on a busy machine.** Before running a search pattern, token-goat times it on short test inputs and refuses one whose time climbs steeply, so a pattern cannot stall the process. A moment where the machine gave the CPU to something else could make a plain word look slow, so `token-goat grep e src` could fail with exit code 1 and `pdf-locate` could print nothing. A pattern with nothing that repeats or branches is now accepted without timing, since its cost cannot climb, and a pattern that does look slow is timed again before it is refused.

## [2.9.28] - 2026-09-24

### Added

- **A failed tool call is now classified and counted, per tool and per model.** Claude Code routes a failed Read or a non-zero Bash exit to `PostToolUseFailure`, never to `PostToolUse`, and `install` did not register that event before, so the repeat-failure brake never ran there. `install` now writes it, `doctor` warns when a user-scope install lacks it, and the bridge status row lists it. `session-audit --tool-errors [--json]` reports calls, errors and unknown errors per tool and per model, with the top unknown error prefixes; across 3,692 local transcripts it names all but 1,537 of 7,938 errors.
- **A run of reads or searches sent one turn at a time now gets a hint to batch them, and a run of empty searches gets a hint to search by meaning instead.** When three or more reads or searches in a row each arrive a full turn after the previous result, one line asks for independent reads to go out together in one message. When three searches in a row find nothing, one line points at `token-goat answer` or `token-goat semantic`. Each hint speaks at most once per streak and twice per session, only on a pass or context answer, never on a deny or rewrite. `hint-stats` gains four categories for this: `read_batch` and `search_brake`, which are scored on the pattern of calls that follows, plus `grep_dedup_hint`/`glob_dedup_hint`, which count spend but carry no verdict.
- **`token-goat semantic` takes several queries in one call, and warns when its best match is weak.** `token-goat semantic "a" "b" "c"` runs each query through the same search and prints one block per query in argument order (JSON: an array of per-query payloads, each carrying its query). Three queries in one call took 1.7 s against 2.4 s as three separate calls. `--preflight` combined with extra queries is now rejected, since it never searches. When the closest match that survives the relevance floor is farther than 0.85, a notice on stderr names the distance and points at a keyword route or a rephrase; JSON carries it as `lowConfidence`. Calibrated on this repository's live index: 38 real questions scored 0.565 to 0.800, 12 off-topic queries scored 0.863 to 1.043, so the cut flags every off-topic query tested and no real one. The configured `semantic.max_distance` floor is unchanged.

### Changed

- **A deny or read hint now opens with a command that runs as printed, naming a real symbol or heading instead of a placeholder.** The most frequent denies named a literal placeholder (`section "file::SectionHeading"`, `config-get "file" KEY_NAME`, `read "file::SymbolName"`) that exits 1 when pasted as written. A new resolver fills the name from the file itself: headings or content the hook already holds, then the index while it still matches the file on disk, then a bounded read of the file's start, falling back to the old placeholder only when nothing usable is found. A JSON or YAML key now routes to `json-query`/`yaml-query` instead of `config-get`. A deny already received for the same call since the last compaction now comes back as its command plus one line, instead of the full explanation again (283 of 2,764 measured denies were verbatim repeats); a deny that delivers content, such as an inlined skill slice, is never shortened.

### Fixed

- **A Bash read that starts with an environment-variable assignment no longer slips past the read gates.** `FOO=1 cat src/cli.ts`, `LANG=C sed -n '1,80p' x` and similar leading assignments used to dodge the read hints and dedup that the bare command hits, since every read extractor anchored at the start of the command. Execution is unchanged: the assignment still runs. An inline compression opt-out hidden behind such a prefix is now refused the same way a bare one already was.
- **A background command's output that prints JSON is read like any other output.** A task `.output` file was taken for an agent transcript whenever its first character was `{`, so a command that printed JSON, such as `codex exec --json` or a report script, had its own output refused and pointed at a transcript reader. The file now has to open the way a transcript does. On the machine this was measured on, 42 of 62 brace-first task outputs were transcripts, all opening with the same key, and the other 20 were command output.
- **The note that an identical search already ran now fires on Claude Code's own Grep and Glob.** Their results carry structured counts (`numFiles`, `numLines`, `numMatches`) and no text to count, so every one was recorded as finding nothing and the note never appeared. Only shell searches got it before.
- **`session-audit --tool-errors` no longer counts a deny that delivers the content as an error.** For a large skill, token-goat answers with its compact slice or heading tree inside the deny, and a Read can be served a compact copy the same way; those calls did their job. They are now labeled `tg_content_delivered`, left out of each tool's error count and rate, and totalled on a line of their own. Over 7 days on one machine, Skill went from 235 errors in 269 calls to 62, with 173 delivered.
- **A batch or search hint is scored on the agent and the prompt that saw it.** Subagents share their parent's session id, so a subagent's calls could settle the parent's pending hint; rows are now matched on the agent. A hint still open when a new prompt arrives is closed as unscored, instead of being judged by calls made for a different task.
- **The pi, opencode and OpenClaw bridges keep each session's state to itself.** They run hooks inside the host's long-lived process, where a session with nothing saved yet started from the previous session's reads, and two hooks running at once could overwrite each other's state mid-call, dropping a read or counting it twice. A session with nothing on disk now starts empty, and hook calls within one process run one at a time.

## [2.9.27] - 2026-09-23

Upgrading reindexes PowerShell files. The change is in the PowerShell parser, so its parser fingerprint moves and PowerShell files are reparsed on the next run. Embeddings and other languages are untouched.

### Added

- **`json-outline` and `yaml-outline` take `--filter TEXT`, and a query can name a key that holds a dot.** A flat registry with hundreds of numeric keys used to print every one of them, so finding one entry meant reading the whole outline. `--filter` keeps the keys containing the text and says how many matched: `json-outline registry.json --filter 11862` prints ten keys and `(10 of 93 keys contain "11862")`. `json-query` and `yaml-query` read a quoted bracket segment as one literal key, so `"['a.b'].c"` reads `c` under the key `a.b`, where `a.b.c` walks three levels.
- **Notes saved with `token-goat note set` come back on their own.** They print at every session start and in the compaction manifest, just after the edited files and before the files read, so a finding written down before a compaction outlives it. Codex does not run a session start hook, so there the manifest is how notes return. The session start reminder names the command.
- **`waste --copilot` and `audit` name the MCP servers a session never called, with the command that turns each one off.** Copilot records which server answered every MCP tool call, so the report now counts calls per server. For a server with none it prints `copilot mcp disable <name>`, which Copilot keeps in its settings until `copilot mcp enable <name>` undoes it; `--disable-mcp-server <name>` skips a server for one run. When the log records no tool calls at all, the report says it cannot tell which servers went unused rather than guessing.

### Changed

- **A file read through inline Python, Node or PowerShell runs with its output capped instead of being refused.** A command like `python -c "import json; print(json.load(open('r.json'))['118615'])"` used to be denied outright. In the Claude Code and Codex transcripts on the development machine from 2026-08-08 to 2026-09-23, that refusal fired 217 times. About 203 of those commands printed one key, a count, or a slice of a single file, the next call was another interpreter variant 31% of the time, and one session hit 91 refusals in a row. The command now runs under a 2,000-token cap with no other filtering: a projection comes back whole, and a whole-file dump comes back cut, followed by a recall id and the narrower command to run next. The refusal stays where the wrapper cannot run: compression turned off, a pipeline or chain, a command sent to the background, a script longer than the command line allows, and VS Code.
- **A shell read of a JSON or YAML file points at `json-outline` and `json-query` (or the YAML pair) instead of `config-get`.** `config-get` reads flat keys and has no spelling for a nested value or a key holding a dot, while the outline runs as written and lists every top-level key with its type and size.
- **The instructions token-goat installs tell the agent what to do after a refusal.** A read turned away by a `[tg]` refusal and retried with a shifted range or a different read tool is now listed among the failure shapes, next to the command that replaces it: the one the refusal names.

### Security

- **The set of packages allowed to run in your process is now pinned and checked on every build.** Supply-chain malware has moved past install scripts: the current pattern is a package that installs cleanly, passes a static scan, and puts its payload inside a method the program is certain to call once it starts working. Blocking install hooks does nothing about that, and neither does scanning an install that looks normal. What the attack still needs is a package in the tree, so that is what is bounded here. Exactly one package is required at runtime, `jsonc-parser`, bundled into the shipped artifact; every other production dependency is optional and loaded only when the feature that needs it is used. A dependency arriving through a routine version bump now fails the build and names itself instead of quietly beginning to execute in every install. `SECURITY.md` states the guarantee and the guard holds that statement to the tree it describes, so it cannot go stale into something untrue.
- **Every package the shipped bundle can load is now reviewed by name, read from the artifact rather than from the lockfile.** The lockfile pins what an install puts on disk, and that is not the same question as what actually gets loaded: the bundle can resolve a module the host application supplies out of its own process, or a development-only package behind a guarded import, and none of that moves a byte in `package-lock.json`. A review that reads only the lockfile passes while the import surface grows. The new guard reads the built artifact instead, so a new run-time import is a build failure that prints the name. Its own limits are written down rather than glossed: a specifier assembled from a variable has no literal to find, which makes this a floor on the import surface and never a ceiling, and is why the lockfile check is the other half rather than a duplicate.
- **A package declared as both optional and development was missing from the list of everything able to run in your process.** npm collapses that pair down to a plain development flag in the lockfile, which reads as "never reaches a user" for a package an install really does fetch — and the guard claiming to enumerate that set was reading the flag and nothing else. It now reads the manifest's own declarations as a second, independent opinion and takes the union, with a test that fails if the two ever disagree again. One package was missing; it is named now.
- **Installing Token-Goat runs nothing, and that is now a checked property rather than a habit.** The package declares no `preinstall`, `install`, or `postinstall` script, so there is no install-time hook to abuse — but nothing had ever asserted it, and the moment to be told that changed is when it changes, not during an incident. Any lifecycle script npm would run by itself must now be listed with what it is for.
- **Dependency signatures are verified in CI and before any commit that moves the lockfile.** `npm audit signatures` checks every resolved package against the registry's signature for that exact version, so a tarball altered after publication fails rather than installs. It is deliberately not offered as an answer to the attack above: a package published maliciously by its own maintainer carries a perfectly valid signature. It closes the tampering half, and the guard on the package set closes the other.
- **Releases are built and published by two separate jobs, so the code that runs during a build cannot reach the registry credential.** `npm ci` resolves several hundred development packages here and the test suite executes all of them, and while the build and the publish shared a single job, every one of them ran on the same filesystem as the token — free to rewrite the artifact before it shipped, or to add a publish-time hook that npm would then run with the credential in scope. The building job now holds no secret at all, the publishing job installs nothing and runs no scripts, and the only thing crossing between them is the built bundle. This does not make a release untamperable and is not claimed to: the build is a bundler running dependency code, so the artifact is the residual risk. It is a far smaller one than a writable checkout sharing a job with a publish token.

### Fixed

- **A long command output that Claude Code saved to a file no longer blocks the next read of the same lines.** Past 20,000 bytes Claude Code writes a command's output to a file and shows the model only the first 2 KB. token-goat still counted all of it as already seen, so a later read covering the same lines came back as `already served verbatim in this session; withheld here`, pointing at lines the model never saw. Output saved this way is no longer counted as seen. The line range a `sed -n` or `awk` read recorded is taken back too, and a `cat` of the whole file no longer counts as a read of it, so a later `Read` of those lines is not refused as already read.
- **`TOKEN_GOAT_BASH_COMPRESS=0` written in front of a command turns compression off for all of it.** The hooks only read their own environment, so the prefix never reached them, and `TOKEN_GOAT_BASH_COMPRESS=0 sed -n '1,80p' notes.md | cut -c1-200` was still compressed and capped. The prefix now counts on any stage of a pipeline or chain, after `export`, `env` (with or without its own flags), or `time`, inside a `( ... )` subshell or a `for` loop body, and as PowerShell's `$env:TOKEN_GOAT_BASH_COMPRESS = 0`. The same text inside a heredoc body is file content and does not count.
- **Comment folding is now a script that proves it only touched comments.** The house rule is that a comment goes on one line, and the hand-written fold used to apply it swallowed the `*/` closing a JSDoc block in two guard files, which commented out the code beneath it. Nothing failed loudly: the files still parsed, and what surfaced was an unused-import lint error several lines away. `scripts/merge-comments.mjs` replaces it, and before writing anything it strips comments from the file with esbuild, folds, strips again, and refuses the file unless the two renderings are byte-identical. It carries a self-test, `tests/merge_comments_script.test.ts` runs that self-test in the suite, and a `--check` case pins the guard sources as already folded, so an edit that re-wraps one fails in the suite rather than landing.
- **Terminal output is compressed under Codex CLI, Copilot CLI and PowerShell on Windows, where nothing was compressed at all before.** Those harnesses run their shell commands through PowerShell rather than the Git-Bash the wrapper assumed, and the only safe answer available was to give up: the hook recognised the harness, returned without rewriting, and every command on those three ran at full width. The wrapper now hands the command to PowerShell itself, passed as base64 through an environment variable that the launched script clears before it runs anything, so a backslash in a path, a quote, a redirection or an angle bracket arrives as written and no child process inherits the command text. Two cases are left alone rather than risked: a payload whose base64 does not decode back to the exact original, and a wrapped command long enough to approach Windows' 32,767-character limit on a command line. Both run uncompressed, which is what they did yesterday.
- **`Invoke-Pester` is recognised as a PowerShell command.** The PowerShell filter is what turns a wall of verbose, debug and progress records into the failures underneath them, and it decides whether to run by looking at the program being invoked. It knew `pwsh` and `powershell` and nothing else, so the ordinary way to run a PowerShell test suite went through unfiltered. `Invoke-Pester` and `pester` are now on that list. Capitalisation was never the obstacle: the name is lowercased before the comparison and always has been.
- **A helper function declared inside a Pester block can be looked up by name.** PowerShell functions were only collected at the outermost brace level, and a test suite puts most of its helpers inside a `BeforeAll`, `Describe` or `Context`, so those functions were absent from the index: `token-goat symbol` reported them as not found, and a surgical read of one was not possible in the file where it mattered most. They are indexed now, each recorded under the block that encloses it, and the lifecycle blocks themselves (`BeforeAll`, `BeforeEach`, `AfterAll`, `AfterEach`) are indexed as setup symbols so the setup a test depends on is reachable the same way.
- **A semantic search for a bare name that finds nothing now says whether that name is an indexed symbol.** `semantic` matches passages by meaning and by keyword, and `symbol` looks up an exact name, so a search for something like `parseHeader` can come back empty from the first while the second would have answered it outright. An empty result for a single identifier reads as "not here", which is the wrong conclusion and the expensive one, because the next move is usually a wide search. The empty result now checks the symbol table and says which case this is: that the name is indexed, with the command to fetch it, or that it is not, with the same command offered as the next thing to try.
- **Three more search modes whose lines are the answer are passed through instead of being summarised into invented numbers.** A search that lists files or counts them per file emits no match lines, and the summariser reads every line as one match on the text before its first colon. `-l` and `-c` were released whole for that reason; their siblings were missed. `rg --count-matches` reports a per-file total of matches rather than matching lines, so each `path:18` line counted as a single match for `path`: a search of this project's own source printed `372 matches across 372 file(s)` with every file shown as one match, where the real total was 4466. `rg --files-without-match` and `rg --files` emit a bare path per line, and a 394-file listing became `394 matches across 1 file(s)` attributed entirely to the search root in the first case and `394 matches across 0 file(s)` with every line unattributed in the second. All three now release their output untouched.
- **A `kubectl get` that asks for a document is no longer truncated as if it were a table.** `kubectl get` prints a table by default, and truncating one is the point: the rows that survive stay readable and a marker says how many did not. Nothing looked at the requested output format, so the same truncation was applied to `-o json` and `-o yaml`, where it leaves a fragment cut in the middle of an object. A 121-line `-o yaml` listing came back as thirteen lines, under a marker suggesting `--selector` as the remedy, which does not make a document parseable. Documents in every spelling of the flag, including the template formats, are now released whole. `wide`, `name` and `custom-columns` stay line-oriented and are still truncated, because a truncated one of those is still valid and the marker still discloses the loss. A document larger than the filter's overall output cap is still shortened by that cap, which is a separate bound and prints the recall command for the full text.
- **`token-goat help <command>` reports success instead of failing after printing the help you asked for.** Every one of them did this: the help text arrived on stdout, and then `token-goat: (outputHelp)` arrived on stderr and the command exited 1. The action asks for its answer by re-running `<command> --help` internally, which signals completion by throwing rather than by exiting so that nothing can cut output short mid-write, and the layer that turns a thrown error into a message could not tell that signal apart from a real one. Any script that checked the exit code, and any agent reading stderr, was told the lookup failed -- and this is the spelling the compact help's own closing tip recommends. `--help`, a bare `help` and `<command> --help` were never affected. Asking about a name that does not exist now says so and exits 1, where before it printed the entire top-level command list, which reads like an answer.
- **The command list no longer advertises four commands that do not exist, print two of them twice, or hide three that do.** `token-goat help` is the surface a caller consults to find out what this CLI can do, and it was a hand-written list that nothing compared against the commands actually registered. It named `snapshot-snapshot`, `opencode-*`, `start` and `stop`, none of which exist, so following the list got you `unknown command`; it printed `compress` and `compress-text` twice each in one group; and it left out `audit`, `history` and `locate`, which meant the only way to find one of those was to already know it was there. Both halves of the list are now derived from their producers and checked against each other, so a command added or renamed without touching the list fails the build rather than reaching users as a wrong answer.
- **A `-L` search is still compressed when ripgrep runs it.** grep reads `-L` as the inverse file listing, but ripgrep reads it as `--follow`, so the short spelling belongs to grep alone and the long `--files-without-match` is shared. Treating the two alike would have released an ordinary symlink-following search whole and dropped the compression it exists to perform.
- **An inline interpreter read no longer counts as a full read of the file.** The session read cache recorded the file as read, so a later `Read` of it could be turned away as "already read this session" when the model had only seen one key or a capped slice.
- **Output cut by `compress --max-tokens` alone can now be recalled.** The full output was stored only when a filter changed something, so a run that the token cap cut and nothing else dropped the rest with no way to get it back.
- **Comment folding leaves tool directives on their own line.** `eslint-disable-next-line`, `@ts-expect-error`, `/// <reference>`, `#region` and the coverage and formatter ignore comments are instructions to a tool. Folded onto the comment above them they became prose the tool never read, which is how a folded `no-control-regex` directive stopped suppressing its rule.
- **The check that every package the bundle loads has been reviewed no longer rejects Node's own `node:sqlite` on Node 22.** It told built-in modules apart using Node's list of them, which on Node 22 leaves out the modules that exist only under the `node:` prefix, so the database driver's import failed the check on every CI runner while passing locally on Node 24.
- **Paging through a file in line ranges is refused once, and for source code the refusal names the code at the lines you asked for.** After three slices of one file, every further slice was denied for the rest of the session, and the refusal listed functions from the top of the file whatever lines were requested. A Copilot session looking for lines 140 to 180 of a Python file was refused 18 times in a row, each retry a slightly different window. The refusal now fires once per file until the next compaction, and on a source file it names the function holding those lines, as `token-goat read "file::name"`. A later slice is let through with the same pointer attached as a note.
- **`doctor` warns when an installed hook shim is older than the token-goat running it.** Each harness's shim is written by `install` and rewritten only by the next one, so upgrading without reinstalling left the old shim in place. It still launched and answered, so `doctor` passed it, but none of the shim's own fixes reached that harness. On one machine the Copilot shim was 153 lines behind and dropped every note sent before a tool call, and the Claude Code shim was 92 lines behind. `doctor` now compares the Claude Code, Codex and Copilot shims with the ones this build would write and names the `install` command that refreshes a stale one.

## [2.9.26] - 2026-09-23

No reindex is needed.

### Fixed

- **A `--json` search is passed through instead of being summarised into a count that was never measured.** `rg --json` emits one JSON event per line, and none of them is a match line. The summariser reads each line as a match on the text before its first colon, and a JSON line's first colon sits inside `{"type"`, which holds no dot or slash, so every line fell into the unattributed bucket. A 403-line search of a single file was replaced by `grep: 403 matches across 0 file(s)` and one line noting 403 unattributed lines, which is the filter reporting that it had parsed nothing and shipping the result anyway. The count was the number of events rather than matches, the file count was wrong, and the output the caller asked for by name was gone. `--vimgrep` and `-o` were checked the same way and both attribute correctly, so neither changed.
- **A `node -e` one-liner that reads a JSON file with `require()` and writes the same file back is no longer denied.** An in-place edit is not a read into context, and denying one only moves the same script into a file where it runs unchecked, so that shape was already exempt. The exemption was keyed on how the read was spelled rather than on the write, and sat inside the `readFileSync` branch alone: the ordinary version-bump one-liner was denied, while the byte-identical edit spelled `readFileSync` was allowed, and the denial told the caller that `fs.readFileSync()` bypasses read hooks for a command that never calls it. Reading one file and writing a different one is still treated as a read, unchanged.
- **A PowerShell `[IO.File]` read-modify-write of one file is no longer denied.** That extractor had no write check of any kind, so replacing a file's text through `WriteAllText` was blocked as a read, and the substitute the message offered cannot perform an edit. It now bails on a write back to the same path, matching the rule the Node extractor already applied. Reading one file and writing another is still treated as a read.

## [2.9.25] - 2026-09-23

Upgrading reparses the index. The change is in the indexer, so the parser stamp moves for every language and each file is read once more. Embeddings are untouched and keep serving throughout: nothing already stored is re-embedded, and no existing index loses chunks, because no file in a normal project comes close to the ceiling this restores.

### Fixed

- **The per-file chunk ceiling counts the chunks that were stored, not an estimate taken before they were cut.** `indexing.max_chunks_per_file` was checked against a character-budget cut taken just before embedding. The embedder then threw that cut away and re-cut the same content with the model's own tokenizer, which flushes on a token budget as well as a character one and so divides the file more finely. The ceiling was therefore applied to a count several times smaller than the one it was meant to bound: a chunk may run to 8000 characters on the character cut but is held to 510 tokens on the token one, which measured over 6117 stored chunks is roughly 810 to 1775 characters, so a file could clear a ceiling of 600 and store several thousand rows. Embeddable documents skipped the check entirely and had no ceiling at all. The ceiling is now re-applied to the rows in the index after embedding, for documents and source alike, and a file that breaches it has its chunks removed and is marked so the background sweep agrees. That sweep already counted stored rows, so the two now measure the same thing and cannot reach opposite verdicts on one file.

## [2.9.24] - 2026-09-23

### Fixed

- **The oversized-index warning reports each category as a share of the database file, and says how much of the file it did not measure.** The warning states the file's size and then introduces its breakdown with "where it went", but each share was worked out against the total of the categories it had measured rather than against the file. Those categories cover the text columns and the embedding vectors only, so on a database whose size is mostly indexes and fixed-width rows they account for a small part of it and the shares quietly rescaled to fill that part instead. A 1633 MB index whose symbol bodies held 167 MB reported them as 76% of it, which reads as about 1.2 GB recoverable by the rebuild named in the same sentence. The shares are now taken against the file, so that case reads 10%, and a remainder worth mentioning is named outright rather than left to be inferred from shares that no longer add up to everything.
- **The lock file records the same version as the package manifest.** Releasing bumped the manifest by hand, and nothing in that path updated the version the lock file carries, so the two drifted apart across two releases while every check stayed green. A new check compares them.

## [2.9.23] - 2026-09-23

### Fixed

- **A file the index never managed to parse is read fresh from disk instead of being reported as up to date.** The index's file table had one row per indexed file, and three separate checks read a row with no content hash as a record written before content hashing existed, accepting it as current. No version of token-goat has ever written such a row from a parse: every insert back to the first commit records the hash alongside the timestamp. The only thing that ever produced one was the read-retry counter the table carried until 2.9.22, which added a row for a path it had merely failed to open. On any index created before that release, a file that hit a single lock or permission error was therefore treated as current from then on, with no symbols to serve, no reparse, and no warning, and nothing short of editing the file could recover it. Those rows are now recognised as what they are, so the first surgical read of such a file parses it once and replaces the row. The stale-file warning stays silent for them, since a file that is missing from the index is a different thing to say than a file the index holds an older copy of.

## [2.9.22] - 2026-09-23

Upgrading reparses the index. The line-number arithmetic described below is shared by every adapter, so the parser stamp moves for all of them and each file is read once more. Embeddings are untouched and keep serving throughout.

### Fixed

- **The Salesforce front-end and single-file-component adapters read a template once, not once per match.** Both worked out a match's line number by slicing the document from character 0 and counting newlines, and its text by splitting the whole document, so each cost the entire file once for every symbol and reference found in it. On a file a human wrote that is invisible; on a generated LWC template, where every tag lands on line 1, it is quadratic. A 1.2 MB template took 1,323 ms to index and a 290 KB one took 84 ms: four times the work for twice the input. They take 28 ms and 7 ms now, twice the work for twice the input. Both adapters had grown their own copy of the same two helpers, so the arithmetic now lives once in the shared line-index helpers every other adapter already uses.
- **The edit-failure hook places every match without re-reading the file for each one.** When an edit fails because its `old_string` matched more than once, the hook reports how many times and where. It found each match's line by the same slice-and-count, so the work grew with the square of the match count: a one-character `old_string` occurring 100,000 times in a 2 MB file took 1.7 seconds, and the hook accepts files up to 10 MB. The harness waits on that before it can tell you the edit failed.
- **A file that failed to be read is no longer recorded as indexed.** When the worker could not read a file -- a lock, a directory in the way, a permission error -- it counted the failure so it could retry a few times and then stop. It kept that count in the index's file table, which meant creating a row there for a file that had never been indexed at all. A row in that table is what three separate places read as "this file is in the index", so a file that hit one transient lock was treated as indexed from then on and lost the on-demand repair that would otherwise have picked it up on the next read. The counters now live in their own small table, and a row in the file table means what it says again. `token-goat project prune` reclaims a counter whose file is no longer on disk, and removing a file from the index takes its counter with it. A counter for a file that still exists but cannot be read is left alone, however many times it has failed, since clearing that would hand a locked file a fresh retry budget on every sweep.

## [2.9.21] - 2026-09-23

Upgrading reparses the index. The stored reference rows described below are the ones that change, so the parser stamp moves for every language and each file is read once more. Embeddings are untouched and keep serving throughout.

### Fixed

- **`token-goat project prune` reclaims file rows whose file was deleted under a root that still exists.** That is the largest category of dead rows, and the only thing that ever reclaimed it was the worker daemon's own sweep, on a twenty-four hour cadence: the one-off command never ran that sweep, so it answered "Nothing to do" against an index that had plenty to do. It runs the same sweep the daemon does rather than restating its rules, so the two cannot drift apart, and `--dry-run` previews it without writing. A root that would lose an anomalously large share of its rows in one pass is still skipped rather than pruned, and now says so.
- **A reference's stored context is bounded to a window around the reference.** A context is the source line the reference sits on, which is short in a file a human wrote and the whole document in one a machine generated: a FlexiPage, a serialized Flow, a minified bundle and a one-line JSON document all put everything on line 1. Every reference on that line stored a copy of the entire file, so the bytes written grew with the square of the input — a 779 KB FlexiPage carrying 10,000 component references wrote 7,789,990,000 characters of context and took 58 seconds to index. It takes 0.8 seconds now, and the time is linear in the file. The window is centred on the reference's own column rather than cut from the head of the line, so the row still shows what it is a reference to.

## [2.9.20] - 2026-09-23

Upgrading reparses and re-embeds the whole index. Both digests moved across the work folded into this release: three new languages changed what `detectLanguage` answers for files that used to come back as unknown, and the extraction and chunking sources the embedding stamp is drawn from moved with them, so the first touch of each file after upgrading re-reads it. The database schema is unchanged at version 16, so 2.9.19 can still open an index this version wrote -- going back just costs the same reparse and re-embed in the other direction.

### Added

- **`token-goat audit` emits a Maintainer Feedback Card without a session transcript to point it at.** It was a skill procedure you ran by hand, which meant the numbers in a card were only as good as whoever assembled them. It is a command now, and it finds its own subject: `audit` and `waste` both locate the active or most recent Copilot CLI session under `~/.copilot/session-state/` by matching project roots and reading process lock files, so neither needs a path argument in the ordinary case.
- **`token-goat section` can slice a long document from the top, and `token-goat conflicts` can show context around a marker.** `section` gained `--max-lines <n>` and `--head <n>` for documents that are one linear run of prose rather than a heading tree, with the truncation stated in the text and carried in the JSON. `conflicts` gained `-C, --context <n>`, defaulting to 3, so a conflict arrives with the lines around it and their numbers instead of just the marker block.
- **`indexing.max_db_size_mb` and `indexing.auto_reclaim_embeddings` are configurable.** The size at which `doctor` starts warning about the index was a fixed number in the source; it defaults to 1500 now and can be set per project. `doctor --repair` reclaims embeddings when the second key is on. Both are locked in project configs alongside the rest of the indexing block.
- **A session start that follows a compaction now restores what the session already knew.** Claude Code tells a hook when a session start is the one after a compaction, and token-goat did nothing with that: the resume packet existed but only ever ran when someone typed `token-goat resume` by hand. It is now built and delivered automatically on that one session source, appended to the routing reminder rather than replacing it. The other four sources (startup, resume, clear, fork) still do no extra work, so a cold start costs what it always did. Turn it off with `compact_assist.enabled = false`.
- **`token-goat resume` names the web pages the session fetched.** A fetched page is the one kind of session state that cannot be recovered by looking at the working tree: the URL is gone from the model's context and nothing on disk names it. The packet now lists the last eight, each with the id that recalls the body through `token-goat web-output`.
- **`token-goat session-audit` measures its own estimate against the billing figures printed beside it.** The report has always shown billed tokens in one section and a characters-per-token estimate in the next, and never subtracted them. A new `Estimator calibration` section prints the model-visible bytes, the estimate over them, the billed first-write tokens, the signed error between the two, and the bytes-per-token the corpus implies. It compares against uncached input plus cache writes, never uncached input alone, because in a cached session almost every first write is billed as a cache write. Read the error as a bound rather than a correction factor: cache expiry and the system prompt both inflate the billed side and neither can be separated out from a transcript, which the section says in its own output.
- **A byte is no longer priced as a fixed number of tokens.** Source and prose run about four bytes to the token; a base64 or hex payload runs about one and a half. Estimates now recognise a dense payload and price it accordingly, which matters most for the overflow guard: under the flat rate it guessed a blob at a third of its real cost and fired after the context it was protecting was already spent. Ordinary text is priced exactly as before, so nothing already credited changes value.
- Added native configuration language symbol extractors for Nginx (`nginx.conf`, `.nginx`), Caddyfile (`Caddyfile`, `.caddy`), and Apache HTTP Server (`httpd.conf`, `apache2.conf`, `.htaccess`). Anonymous server, location, upstream, virtual host, directory, and proxy blocks can now be addressed directly with `token-goat read "file::symbol"`.
- Added `--max-lines <n>` and `--head <n>` options to `token-goat section` to slice section content from the top, avoiding large payload dumps on long documents under single headings.
- Added `-C, --context <n>` support to `token-goat conflicts` (defaulting to 3 lines), showing surrounding file context around merge conflict markers to eliminate whole-file reviews during git merges.
- Added configurable index database size warning threshold (`indexing.max_db_size_mb`, default 1500 MB) and automated vector cleanup (`indexing.auto_reclaim_embeddings`, default false) in `token-goat doctor` and `doctor --repair`. When the shared index database exceeds the configured limit, doctor highlights the dominant tables and can safely reclaim embedding vectors and compact the database during repair.
- **`semantic` has a relevance floor you can set, and it says when the floor is what emptied the results**: the command dropped a vector match only past a bound nothing reaches in practice, so a question the project has no answer for still came back with a full page of blocks that print exactly like real matches. `semantic.max_distance` is the worst distance still counted as a match. It ships at the same value as the old bound, which means it filters nothing until you set it, and that default is a measurement rather than an omission: against this repository's own index, twelve real questions had best matches from 0.635 to 0.813 and eleven nonsense ones from 0.820 to 1.017, but a two-file project in this repository's test suite matches its own target at 0.934. The two ranges overlap once corpus size changes, so no single number separates them everywhere, and a number picked wrong removes real answers without saying so. Lower it once you have looked at your own project's distances. When the floor does empty the vector half, the command now says so with the distance that came closest: `Matching on meaning found nothing within 0.5 (closest was 0.598)`, so the number to change is in front of you. A repository's own `.token-goat.toml` cannot set this key, for the same reason it cannot set the index skip lists: a checked-in file setting it near the minimum would take that repository's code out of the vector half while keyword search went on answering as though nothing were held back.

### Fixed

- **Indexing a large Salesforce metadata file is no longer quadratic in its own size.** Every reference the adapter recorded worked out its line number by copying and re-measuring everything before it in the file, and then took its context line by scanning forward to the next newline -- which, on a generated FlexiPage or a serialized Flow written as one long line, meant scanning to the end of the document. A FlexiPage with 10,000 component references took 2.8 seconds and copied several gigabytes of string to produce a few hundred kilobytes of index. Both steps now read from the line index the adapter already builds, which takes the same file to 12 milliseconds and makes the cost grow with the file rather than with its square.

- **`waste`, `audit` and `session-audit` no longer fail outright on a long session.** Both transcript parsers read the file line by line, but each began by loading the whole thing into one string first, which put a ceiling on them that has nothing to do with available memory: a string cannot exceed about 512 MB, and the read throws at that point rather than returning what it got. Run against a real 570 MB session transcript, the command stopped with `Cannot create a string longer than 0x1fffffe8 characters` and produced no report at all -- and the sessions big enough to reach that are the ones an audit is most worth running on. Both now stream the file.

- **Copilot session auto-discovery works on macOS, and on Windows paths written in short form.** `audit` and `waste` find a session by comparing the project root they resolved against the working directory Copilot recorded in `workspace.yaml`, and that comparison was the one place in the codebase resolving paths by hand instead of through the shared canonicalizer. macOS exposes `/var` as `/private/var`, so the two spellings of the same directory never matched and discovery fell through to the Claude transcript instead -- silently, since falling through produces a valid report about the wrong session. Both sides now go through the canonicalizer every other project-root producer uses, which also settles Windows drive-letter case and 8.3 short segments.

- **A project you indexed but never edited could never have its dead rows reclaimed.** The sweep that removes index rows for files that have vanished from disk only scans project roots that have been registered, and the only thing registering one was the edit hook -- so the sweepable set was "roots you have edited" rather than "roots you have indexed". On a real index, 233 of 235 dead rows sat outside every registered root and a quarter of all indexed files belonged to a root that had never been registered, while `token-goat project prune` reported nothing to do. Indexing now registers the root too, including when every file in the project turns out to be already current.
- **`refs --top`, `--exclude-tests` and `--grep` chose from the alphabetically first twenty thousand references rather than from all of them.** All three narrow the resolved set in JavaScript after the query returns, so the SQL cap decided which rows the filter could ever see, and `queryRefs` orders by path then line -- alphabetically, not by count. "Top files by reference count" therefore meant the top files among whichever sorted first. On the index this was found against, `expect` holds 143,666 references, 7.2 times the window; `refs expect --top 8` named a leading file of 695 references while the real leader held 1,571 and did not appear at all, and `push --exclude-tests` could not reach 2,459 of 17,484 genuine non-test references at any `--limit`. Raising the number would only move the project size at which that recurs, so these three paths query unbounded and the cap is gone. `refs` now reports an exact total on those routes instead of a floor.
- **A ranged read past a file's five-hundredth symbol was offered the file's very first symbol.** The read hint drew candidates from one 500-row window and then looked inside it for symbols overlapping the requested lines. Symbols come back ordered by starting line, so past the five-hundredth that window only ever held the top of the file: a read further down overlapped nothing, fell through to the unfiltered window, and named something thousands of lines away. Pointing somewhere else is worse than the bare placeholder the hint was built to replace. 76 of the 13,900 files in this machine's index hold more than 500 symbols, the largest 10,342. The overlap scan pages now and stops at the first page beginning past the range, since no later symbol can reach back into it, so a file that fits in one page costs no extra query.
- **Three unusable names at the top of a file left a hint with no name to offer.** The hint asked the index for three symbols and only then dropped the names it cannot safely render -- adjacent labels carrying a spoken marker, headings whose emoji hold a zero-width joiner -- so the cap decided which names the drop could ever consider, and the hint fell back to its generic placeholder while the file went on holding perfectly good names. The sampler beside it had the same shape from the other side, stopping at the three its caller displays so the caller's filter ran against a list already truncated to the display count. Both over-fetch and cut afterwards now.
- **A bounded `Get-Content -TotalCount`/`-First`/`-Head`/`-Tail` read was denied as a whole-file dump.** The PowerShell recognizer did not read those parameters, so a deliberately bounded read was intercepted as if it had asked for everything. It recognises them now and lets the read through, emitting the path hint as advice and recording the file in the session cache rather than blocking.
- **Every deny against a file whose name carries a capital letter was booked as abandoned.** A denied path's basename is stored lower-cased, and the two Bash-side classifiers compared it against the raw command line, so `CLAUDE.md`, `README.md` and `MEMORY.md` -- the names conventionally capitalised, and the ones the census is read for most -- never matched. Retry and edit classification were never affected, since both normalise each side before comparing. Every fixture in the deny-outcome tests used a lower-case path, which is how it stayed green.
- **Trimmed plan detail counted as nothing trimmed.** The census pattern for omission markers required a literal ` more ` after its digits, which the cloud filter's `[token-goat: N plan detail lines omitted]` does not carry, so every plan-detail trim was indistinguishable from a filter that never fired. The other seven spellings matched, which is what made the gap invisible: the count was never empty, only short. A new guard renders every marker template in the filters against the pattern, so the next spelling fails the suite instead of going uncounted.
- **Two deny wordings had never been classified since the branches were written.** Each records a stat name an existing template already claims -- the ranged-reread deny shares one with the served-read deny, the sequential-paging deny with the count deny -- so neither looked unclassified, while no template's pattern matched the string its branch actually prints. Both are classified now, and the table's own doc block records that a shared stat name is not evidence of classification, since that is precisely what hid these two.
- **A file the unembeddable sweep pruned could never be reconsidered.** The sweep deleted the file's chunk rows and left its original embedding stamp in place, which the freshness gate answers on its terminal equality clause forever. Raising `indexing.max_chunks_per_file`, or dropping an extension from the non-text asset list, re-opened the decision for every file the live gate had stamped and for none of the files this sweep pruned -- which were left holding zero chunks, invisible to semantic search, and unreachable by any later configuration. The sweep now restamps each pruned file with the marker the live gate would have given it, so the file stays settled while its condition holds and is re-examined the moment that condition moves.
- **Vectors whose chunk row was already gone were never reclaimed.** Both writers that delete a file's embeddings will clear the chunk row even when the matching vector delete fails or cannot run, which is the right call -- a leaked chunk row is worse, since that is what every scoped query reads -- but nothing collected the leftover afterwards. Every other sweep takes its population from chunk or file rows, which for an orphan are by definition already gone, so only a full rebuild ever cleared them: on this machine's index, 6,685 vectors against 212,089 chunks, 3.1%, with row ids spanning both the historical and the live range, so an ongoing leak rather than one migration. Unreclaimed they inflate the vector-table size `doctor` reports without it being able to attribute the space, and every nearest-neighbour pass spends candidate slots on rows that join to nothing. `token-goat project prune` collects them, after the chunk sweep so anything that pass orphans is picked up in the same run.
- **`find`, `locate` and `symbol` searched only the first twenty thousand symbols of a project.** All three match names in JavaScript rather than in SQL, so the database had already chosen which rows they would see before the name test ever ran. Twenty thousand reads as a generous margin and is not one: of the projects indexed on the machine this was found on, three are larger, and the largest holds 234,675 symbols, so a search there ran over the alphabetically first eight and a half percent of it. A name past that point came back as absent -- and since all three fall back to near-name matching when nothing matched, the result was not silence but a confident list of unrelated files for a symbol that is in the index. They now walk the whole project, keeping only the names and paths they are going to print, so memory follows the project's vocabulary rather than its symbol count. The near-name suggestion offered after a failed `symbol` lookup is ranked over every name for the same reason.
- **A very long line was reported as three times shorter than it was.** Two passes cap line width -- one on the way in, at four thousand characters, to keep the filter regexes off a runaway line, and one on the way out at a thousand. The second one only ever saw the line the first had already shortened, so its notice counted from there: a ten-thousand-character line came out saying three thousand characters were elided when nine thousand were gone. The first pass's own figure is now carried into the second.
- **A tool run through `npx`, `uvx`, `pnpm dlx` or `bunx` with any flag in front of it was not recognised.** The prefix stripper stopped at the first token after the launcher's name, so `npx --yes --package eslint eslint .` was read as running a tool called `--yes` and fell through to the generic filter. It now skips the launcher's own options, including the ones that take a value, and stops at the first real binary -- and at `--`, which every one of these launchers treats as the end of its own options.
- **`yarn install` was compressed as if it were whatever a script called `install` happened to run.** A bare `yarn <name>` runs yarn's own built-in when one exists by that name, and a great many projects have a `scripts.install` (a `husky install`, a native rebuild) that yarn never reaches on `yarn install`. It resolved that script anyway and picked its filter. The 47 built-in subcommand names are now left alone; `yarn run install` still resolves the script, because that spelling really does run it.
- **`token-goat compress` could not resolve package-manager scripts at all.** Deciding which filter to use needs the directory holding the `package.json` that `yarn lint` or `npm test` resolves against, and nothing supplied one on the command-line path, so every wrapped run fell back to the generic filter. It now uses the directory the command is about to run in.
- **A command that wrote to stderr on its way into a pipe lost its compression.** The `&` in a redirection like `>&2` or `&>log` was read as the operator that puts a job in the background, so `rg -n pat src >&2 | tail -20` was treated as two unrelated commands and fell back to the generic filter, while the same command without the redirect was compressed by the one written for search output — 99% against 65% on a real run here. One spelling, `2>&1`, was already handled by deleting it from the command beforehand; the rest now need no such handling, because a redirection's `&` is recognised for what it is.
- **Nerd Font glyphs were deleted from output, and counted in the widths that exist to ignore them.** One function did two jobs: clean text for the model, and measure how wide text is on screen. Private Use Area glyphs get removed for the second job because terminals disagree about how wide they are, and it skipped that removal whenever the input carried no colour code. So a glyph in a filename came out of a piped `token-goat read` a character short, while a glyph in an uncoloured table cell was measured as if it took two columns and pushed the row out of line. The measuring job now lives in the width helper alone, and everything that cleans text for the model leaves glyphs where it found them.
- **`curl` printing rows of numbers had its whole body deleted.** The pattern that recognises a transfer-meter frame is a row of numeric columns, which is also what plenty of real output looks like, and it was applied to every line whether or not curl had printed a meter at all. So `curl -s` against a numeric body returned nothing but a note saying some progress lines were dropped: total data loss, described as something else. A meter frame now only counts as one after curl has printed the meter header that introduces it.
- **The compaction manifest listed the pages your subagents fetched nowhere.** It merged each subagent's files into the summary it hands the model, but read the web-URL list from the main thread alone, so a research subagent's pages were absent from the one artifact whose job is to carry that context across a compaction.
