A lost URL row costs more than a lost file row, because the cache id that would have recalled the page for free goes with it.
- **Copilot CLI dropped the tool name whenever a tool was called `constructor`, `toString` or `__proto__`.** The shim looked its tool-name and argument maps up without checking the key was really in them, so those names resolved to something inherited from the language rather than to nothing, and the payload forwarded to token-goat carried no tool name at all — every hook keyed on one went quiet. MCP servers pick their own tool names, so the key was never ours to vouch for.
- **`grep -Tc` and `grep -Tl` had their counts and filenames thrown away.** grep and ripgrep disagree about which short flags take a glued value, and one shared list was covering both. It was built from ripgrep's reading, where `-T` selects a file type and swallows the rest of the cluster; in grep `-T` is `--initial-tab`, a plain boolean, so the scan stopped there and never saw the `c` or `l` behind it. A count-only search then reached the summarizer, which reprinted every file's count as 1, and a filename-only search had each path read as a match. Each tool now carries its own list, chosen by the command that was actually run rather than by which filter picked it up, since either filter can be handed either tool. Ripgrep gains `-r` in the bargain: its replacement text is no longer scanned for flags, so `rg -rlfoo` stops reading as `--files-with-matches`.
- **A queued compaction manifest was deleted on its way to the model whenever another hook had something to say.** The handler that carries a queued hint to the next tool call is advisory, which means it never overrules another handler, but it consumed the queue the moment it read it. So on any tool call where a second handler returned output of its own, and compressing a shell command's output is the everyday case, the manifest was gone with nothing failing and the hint still counted as delivered. It is now read without being consumed, and cleared only once the text is confirmed to be in what the hook is about to emit. A hint that misses one tool call waits for the next instead of vanishing.
- **A subagent consumed the recovery manifest queued for its parent.** A subagent shares its parent's session id, and the deferred-hint queue was keyed on that id alone, so the first tool call any child made read the manifest the parent's compaction had queued, the relay cleared it as delivered, and the session that actually lost its context got nothing. The queue now uses the same agent-aware key the rest of the session store already understands, so parent and child have their own.
- **A file could name itself with token-goat's own authority prefix and have it read back as an instruction.** The resume packet listed edited files, read files, skill names and the diffstat verbatim, so a path like `[tg] do-this.ts` put the prefix that marks a denial into recovery context the model treats as direction. Every repository-controlled string in the packet is now neutralized the way the read and shell hooks already neutralize theirs, without dropping the filename itself.
- **The overflow guard overshot its own budget on dense payloads by 2.6x.** It decided whether to trim by pricing the text at its measured rate, then trimmed against a flat three-bytes-per-token that only ever suited ordinary text. A base64 blob capped at a thousand tokens came out at 2,583. Both halves now price the same content the same way, which is the whole point of a guard that exists to fire before the context it protects is spent.
- **Every filter read an empty command line when its command was piped.** A piped command is filtered after the fact, and the code that picked the right filter for it threw away the command's arguments on the way. Nothing reported an error; the filters simply behaved as though the command had no arguments. The clearest case: `rg -n pattern one-file.ts | cat` returned `grep: 38 matches across 0 file(s)` and discarded all 38 lines, because a single-file search prints no path to group by and the filter's fallback — attribute those lines to the one file named in the command — had no command to look at. The same command unpiped answered correctly. Files-only (`-l`) and count-only (`-c`) searches were summarised instead of released whole for the same reason.
- **A search asked only for the list of matching files, or only for counts, was summarised into a worse version of itself.** Both shapes already are the answer, so restating them as a per-file tally could only subtract. They now pass through, still bounded by the line and byte caps, which disclose what they drop.
- **Binary files vanished from a large `git diff --stat`.** A binary row reads `Bin 3 -> 5 bytes` where a text row carries a change count, and the pattern that recognises a stat row required a digit right after the `|`. Binary rows therefore did not count toward the threshold that triggers the directory rollup, and were printed above the summary they belonged in rather than folded into it.
- **The compaction manifest dropped the list of edited files first.** Read files were rendered before edits and the manifest has a character budget, so past roughly 28 reads — an ordinary session — the entire edited-files section was cut off. The model summarising the conversation was told what the session had looked at but not what it had changed. Edits are rendered first now: a dropped read costs a re-read, a dropped edit is a fact nothing else records.
- **The session's file list evicted edited files before read ones.** Past 500 files the oldest were dropped by last-read time, and a file that was edited but never read carries no read time at all, so it sorted below everything and went first — losing exactly the entries the compaction manifest and the resume packet are built from.
- **The compaction canary measured paths the model was never shown.** It sampled 64 file paths from the session and checked how many survived into the summary, but the manifest prints at most 40 rows per section and then trims the whole thing to a character budget. Paths past that point could not survive, so retained context was counted as lost. The manifest now hands back the paths it printed as it prints them, so the two cannot disagree — and a file whose name contains a space is no longer cut off at the space, nor is the `...and 3 more` line that ends a long section mistaken for a file.
- **A hint the relay rewrote on its way out was re-sent on every later tool call.** Delivery was proven by looking for the queued text in the emitted output, and the relay sanitises that output after the hint is written and before the check runs. A hint naming a path with a shell metacharacter in it therefore never matched itself, was never cleared, and was re-emitted for the rest of the session while counting as delivered each time.
- **Nginx `http`, `events`, `stream` and a plain `server` block were invisible to the indexer.** The pattern that finds a block required an argument before the keyword, and those four take none, so a stock `nginx.conf` indexed its upstreams and locations and nothing else. `token-goat read "nginx.conf::server"` returned nothing on the file the feature was announced for.
- **A Caddyfile placeholder desynced the Caddy block tracker.** Blocks were classified once per line but closed once per brace, so a line like `redir https://{host}{uri}` pushed one entry and closed two, ending the enclosing site block early and dropping every block below it. Classification now happens per brace, which is where the imbalance was.
- **A tool named after a built-in object property was not treated as a name.** Three lookups read their maps with a plain index, so a payload naming its tool `constructor` came back holding a function rather than a string, and an input key of the same name was rewritten to `function Object() { [native code] }`. All three now ask whether the map really has that key.
- **Copilot CLI received none of the hints that fire before a tool runs.** The read-dedup nudges, the surgical-read advisories on shell commands, the notices on a write path: all of them were computed, counted as sent, and thrown away by the bridge, which returned an empty response for them. The reason recorded for that was that Copilot's pre-tool hook has no field to carry a hint. It has one, and has had one in every version this bridge was ever checked against — the shipped SDK type declares it, and the shipping runtime reads it from the same response object as the two fields that already worked. Hints now arrive, alongside a rewritten argument rather than instead of it. The one thing still withheld is the shrunk-image payload, which is a data URL rather than a sentence and would cost the model more than the picture it replaced; that continues to arrive as a rewritten path.
- **A Copilot tool result delivered as a plain string lost the hint attached to it.** The bridge appends a hint to the result body, and read the body from one of two object fields. Copilot's own result type is either that object or a bare string, and the string form left nothing to append to.
- **Qwen Code's invalid-settings error now says what is wrong with the file.** Its three sibling installers quote the parser's own message; this one dropped it and left the reader to find the bad line themselves.
- **Every tool hook on Codex was dead, and nothing said so.** Codex matches a hook against the tool name it puts on the wire, and for shell commands that name is `Bash`: it renames its own `exec` tool before matching. token-goat wired the internal name, so no pre-tool or post-tool hook fired at all on a real Codex run: no read tracking, no command output caching, no denials, and no delivery for the compaction manifest that had just been queued. A run that should have recorded eight of each recorded none. Fixed by matching the name Codex actually sends, read out of a captured hook payload rather than out of token-goat's own notes, which is where the wrong name came from.
- **Codex was being treated as Claude Code whenever it was started from a Claude Code terminal.** Nothing Codex sets identifies its own hook processes, so token-goat guessed from the environment, and two of the variables it checked first are handed down by whatever shell launched Codex. The Codex hook script now states which harness it is before doing anything else, the way the Kimi and pi bridges already did. A wrong answer here quietly sent every Codex session down another harness's wire format.
- **Codex CLI gets the compaction manifest it was silently losing.** Codex fires the pre-compaction hook and then discards whatever the hook returns, so token-goat built a manifest on every Codex compaction and handed it to nothing. It is now queued at that moment and carried by the next tool call, the same delivery path Copilot CLI already uses. Measured rather than assumed: a forced auto-compaction produced six pre-compaction events, and a shim returning one marker from both the pre-compaction and post-tool hooks put the post-tool copy in the transcript twice and the pre-compaction copy zero times.
- **A harness with no summary to search no longer reads as a harness that lost every path.** The check that watches whether a compaction summary kept the manifest scored an absent summary field as nothing-survived, so a harness that never offered the channel would have looked like a dead one, and five in a row is what makes `token-goat doctor` report a break. An absent summary now records as nothing sampled, which is already the inconclusive case. A summary that arrives empty still counts, because that is a real measurement.
- **Copilot CLI dropped every hint that arrived alongside a compressed tool result.** The bridge took one branch or the other, so when a result carried both a smaller body and a hint, the hint went to a field Copilot's own code discards. Both now travel together in the body Copilot reads. The test that covered this was green throughout: it asserted on the discarded field, which proved the shim emitted the hint and nothing about anyone receiving it.
- **The compaction manifest is delivered on harnesses that throw the hook's answer away.** Copilot awaits the pre-compaction hook and never assigns its result, so the manifest was built and discarded. It is now queued at that moment and carried by the next tool call, using the delivery path that already existed for exactly this case.
- **The check that says whether a compaction summary kept the manifest no longer counts paths the manifest never printed.** It sampled the session's touched files directly while the manifest prints a filtered set, so every incidental path (a `node_modules` entry, a build artefact) went into the denominator and could never be found in the summary. The ratio drifted down for a reason that had nothing to do with the channel it watches, which is the one false alarm a check like this must not raise. Both sides now read from the same selection.
- **`token-goat hint-stats` prints the unit on `spent=`.** The number is a character count and was named correctly everywhere except the line that shows it. `docs/cli.md` also showed a `net=` field the command stopped emitting.
- `Caddyfile`, the filename every Caddy install ships and the one this feature was named for, was not detected at all. Basenames are matched in lower case, and the Caddy row spelled the name in its display case, so the key could never be hit and the file came back as an unknown type while `caddy.conf` beside it worked.
- Nginx files were detected but never offered the symbol read they had just been given. The row that says a language resolves named definitions was left on the data defaults, so the bash hook kept suggesting a line range for `nginx.conf` and diff-on-reread skipped it, even though `read "nginx.conf::server"` worked the whole time.
- The embedding model ran at half its configured thread count for any caller that supplied a partial config, because the fallback constant said 2 while the shipped `worker.embed_threads` default it claims to mirror says 4.
- PowerShell reads with bounded head parameters (`Get-Content -TotalCount`, `-First`, `-Head`) are recognized as partial reads rather than whole-file dumps, avoiding false denies and unnecessary retry turns.

### Changed

- **`semantic.max_distance` is bounded at 1.2 rather than 2.** Above 1.2 the setting was inert: the search keeps its own threshold during the scan and the configured value is applied afterwards as a floor, so a wider figure was accepted, validated, persisted, and discarded inside the scan before the floor could ever see it. The validator clamps rather than rejects, so a stored value above the new ceiling becomes 1.2 and behaves exactly as it already did.
- **`find --json`'s `truncated` flag now means only that `--limit` dropped files.** It used to also mean the scan had run out of window, and there is no window any more. The matching `Results may be incomplete` line is gone with it.
- **One fold for tool names, instead of three.** The same lowercase-and-strip-separators rule existed in three files, and the copy in the hook registry had lost the type guard the other two carry, so an untrusted tool name that the other two answered for threw there instead. It is one leaf module now.
- **One table of Copilot CLI tool names, instead of two identical ones.** The bridge and the hook normalizer each held their own copy, byte-identical and with nothing checking that they stayed that way.
- **The Codex bridge is recorded as run against a Codex binary rather than read from one.** It had been marked as sourced from the declared schema with no run on record. It has now been driven on codex-cli 0.155.0: real pre-tool and post-tool payloads, a pre-tool denial the model obeyed by switching to a `token-goat` command, and post-tool context reaching the model. The whole chain was then re-run once the two fixes above were in: a forced auto-compaction session recorded eight compactions and put the recovery manifest into its own transcript twelve times. `token-goat bridges-status` shows the new note.
- **Two Codex events were listed as absent from the harness, and are not.** The table said Codex has no post-compaction event and no session-start equivalent. Both appear in codex-cli 0.155.0's own hook-event list. They stay unwired, now for the reason that applies to everything else in that table: neither channel has been measured, and Codex's post-compaction input carries no summary field for the check to read. A wrong reason is worse than a listed gap, because it reads as a decision.
- **VS Code's missing compaction hook is confirmed two releases on.** The event table it reads out of a Copilot hooks file was re-read in the 1.138.0 bundle and still lists the same eight keys with neither compaction key, so on that harness the manifest is unavailable rather than unwired: the trigger itself never arrives, and there is nothing to queue.
- **One compaction manifest builder instead of two.** Two existed and built different text from different code: the hook emitted one, and `token-goat compact-hint` showed the other, so a user checking what the next compaction would carry was shown a manifest the summarizer would never see, and its token count sized the wrong text. The builder the hook uses is now the only one, it lives in its own module, and `compact-hint` reports it. The second builder's goal-inference and per-directory grouping were not carried over: an inferred goal is a guess presented to the summarizer as fact, and grouping obscures the per-file read counts the manifest exists to preserve. Its noise-path filtering was kept.
- **Nine `compact_assist` configuration keys that configured nothing were removed** (`triggers`, `min_events`, `max_manifest_tokens`, `compact_skip_ttl_secs`, `noise_floor_tokens`, `edited_dir_group_threshold`, `max_section_lines`, `wide_session_threshold`, `orchestrator_commit_threshold`). None were read on the hook path. A key that configures nothing is worse than a missing key, because it reads as a decision someone made. Setting one is now a configuration error rather than a silent no-op; `compact_assist` keeps `enabled`, `auto_trigger_multiplier`, `max_manifest_chars`, `summary_budget_chars`, and `harness`.
- **`docs/architecture.md` no longer describes two compaction features that do not exist.** It promised a sealed `MUST_PRESERVE` block and a token-budgeted manifest delivered as a system message. The manifest that ships has no sealed block, is capped in characters, and reaches the summarizer as custom instructions. The paragraph now says which parts are history and points at `CLAUDE.arch.md` for what ships.
- Extended the SQL extractor with DuckDB macros (`CREATE MACRO`) and secrets (`CREATE SECRET`), PostgreSQL extensions, domains, aggregates, policies, rules, and operators, plus Oracle synonyms, database links, clusters, and contexts.
- **`semantic` prints the number it ranked on**: the list is ordered by a fused score that combines the vector pass and the keyword pass, but each result showed only its vector distance, and results the keyword pass alone found showed nothing at all. So a result at distance 0.850 could sit above one at 0.776, correctly, and read as a sorting bug. Each result now carries its position in the list, and marks whether the keyword pass voted for it (`+keyword`) or was the only pass that found it (`keyword`). A result only the vector pass found prints exactly as it did before. With `--json`, each item carries `rank`, the fused score, and which of the two passes produced it.
- **A hint refused on price leaves a record**: `token-goat hint-stats` counts what each kind of hint saved against what it was shown for, and a hint that was built and then withheld because the saving was too small to be worth the words recorded nothing at all. Those refusals were invisible, so the table could not tell a hint nobody wanted from one nobody ever saw. Each is now recorded under the same identifier the shown hints use, and appears in the `undisplayed` column.
- **`symbol` puts a real definition ahead of a line that merely imports the name**: asking for a name that a file both imports and defines elsewhere could answer with the import line, which shows where the name came from rather than what it is. Import bindings now sort behind real definitions when you search by name. Listing a file's symbols is unchanged, since there the import line is part of what you asked for.

## [2.9.19] - 2026-09-21

**Upgrading migrates the index database, and the migration is one way.** The hint ledger gained columns, so the schema stamp moves from 14 to 16 the first time this version opens the database. The migration runs by itself and keeps the existing rows. Going back to an older token-goat afterwards is what does not work: an older build sees a stamp it does not recognise, refuses to open the file rather than guess at it, and says to update token-goat or delete the index and let it rebuild. Deleting it is safe, since the index is rebuilt from your own files.

### Added

- **`token-goat hint-stats` separates a hint the agent never saw from one it ignored**: the table scored every emitted hint the same way, so a hint the harness dropped before it reached the model counted against the hint's efficacy exactly like one the model read and disregarded. Two things are recorded now. A hint is marked observable only when the channel it went out on can carry it to the model, and displayed only when it actually arrived. The table gains a `displayed` column, and the rows that cannot be scored on presence carry a footnote saying so.

- **`token-goat answer "<question>"` routes a plain-English question to the command that already answers it, or refuses with a reason**: six intents are recognised — where a symbol is, who calls it, what tests cover it, what a file exports, what a file imports, and what breaks if a symbol changes. The subject is looked up in the index before anything runs, so the router picks both the right command and the right argument form: `answer "what tests cover foldPath"` resolves `foldPath` to `src/path_containment.ts` and runs `test-for` against the file, where passing the symbol to `test-for` directly fails. A bare basename resolves when exactly one file in the project carries it, and reports the candidates when several do. Every lookup and every delegate is scoped to the current project root, so a name shared with another checkout on the same machine never answers about that checkout. There is no model call, no inference, and no fallback to a fuzzy match: a question that matches no intent, or whose subject is in neither the `symbols` nor the `files` table, prints `cannot answer deterministically: <why>; try: <command>` and exits 1. Questions of judgement, intent, or runtime behaviour refuse even when they name a real symbol, because an answer the agent trusts and stops checking costs more than a refusal it can act on. Every answer is preceded by `via: token-goat <command> <argument>`, so the delegated command can be verified or re-run with its own flags. Export and import questions resolve the subject as a file and only as a file: they are module-level properties, so a bare word is matched against the project's file list by name or by extensionless stem (`config` finds `src/config.ts`), several matches report the candidates, and a subject that names only a symbol refuses and points at the file-level command. Measured over the 32 distinct subjects a real session used with this shape, resolving them symbol-first answered about an unrelated file in 12 of the 14 cases that resolved at all. A subject is never resolved into a vendored, generated, or tool-metadata tree, which is how `where is worker` came to answer about a bundled PDF library. `X imports` is recognised as the sibling of `X exports`, `Read`, `Locate`, `Verify`, `Measure`, `View`, `Trace` and `Identify` are stripped alongside the imperative verbs already handled, and the what-breaks intent now matches the phrasings its own refusal message advertises, including `what depends on X` and `what breaks if I change X`. Every answer is bounded at 20 rows, and the `via:` line names every flag needed to reproduce exactly the window it printed — including the `-p` that scopes `symbol` to this project, which the router always applied and the pointer never mentioned. Unbounded, `answer "who calls normalizePath"` emitted 24,274 bytes against the 21,091 of `src/paths.ts` — the file the answer exists to replace — and now emits 996, with the delegate saying on stderr how many callers it withheld. The delegate's own output is filtered the same way its subject already was, so a vendored definition no longer appears in the answer either.

- **`token-goat read "file:142"` resolves a line number to the region around it**: a line number is what you actually hold when a grep hit, a stack frame, or a diff hunk points at code, and until now the only specs `read` accepted were `file::symbol` and the raw line window `file@142-160`, so a line number meant falling back to `sed -n '142,160p'` and paging blind. `file:142` now returns the smallest symbol enclosing line 142; if the line sits above the first symbol it returns the file preamble, and if it falls between two symbols it returns that gap, running to end of file when nothing follows. `file:142-160` returns every region the span overlaps, in file order, with a region nested inside another coalesced into its container. Every block names what it is and its true line span — `# sample.ts:12 -> function betaDogfood  lines 11-13 of 15 (~19 tok)` — so a narrowed answer can never be mistaken for a complete one. A line past end of file, or a file with no indexed symbols, is refused with that reason rather than answered with the nearest slice. Windows absolute paths are handled: the spec splits on its last colon and is only read as a line spec when what follows is entirely digits, so `C:/proj/file.ts:142` keeps its drive letter, and `file::symbol`, `file::142-160` and `file@142-160` are all unchanged.

- **`token-goat symbol --exclude-vendored`**: hides definitions that live under a dependency or build directory (`node_modules`, `dist`, `site-packages`, and the rest of the set the indexer itself skips). Opt-in; default output is unchanged. Older index generations still hold such rows, and they sort to the front of a page, so a bare name search could answer with a bundled library's definition ahead of the project's own.

### Changed

- **A line-range hint proves it saves bytes before it spends any**: the hint that offers a surgical read in place of a line window never compared the two sizes. On this repository's own changelog the replacement it proposed was 43% larger than the window it objected to, so following the advice cost more than ignoring it. Both sizes are already known at the point the hint is built, so the saving is now computed and put through the same byte floor every other hint answers to. A hint that cannot show a saving is not emitted, and one that can names the specific heading or symbol rather than the file.

- **A hint builder names its own file instead of having one read back out of its text**: the file a hint referred to was recovered by matching the prose the hint had just written, which broke whenever the wording changed and silently attributed the hint to nothing. Each builder now carries the path alongside the message.

- **A fix to one language's parser no longer reindexes every file in every project**: the stamp recorded against each indexed file was a single digest covering all extraction logic, so a change to the Dart adapter invalidated a project that contains no Dart at all. Over the last 90 days, 26 of 79 parser changes touched only one or two languages, and on one real index those languages accounted for 161 of 17,855 files while every one of those changes reparsed all 17,855. Each file is now stamped with a digest for its own language, so an adapter fix reparses that language and leaves the rest of the index alone. Changes to shared extraction logic still invalidate everything, which is deliberate: reparsing a file that did not need it costs seconds, while skipping one that did leaves wrong symbols in the index indefinitely.

- **A fix to one document extractor no longer re-embeds every file on the machine**: the embedding half of the stamp was a single digest over all 25 sources that decide chunk text, so editing `pdf_extract.ts`, which can only change how a PDF's bytes become chunk text, marked every TypeScript, Python and Markdown file stale. On one real index that is 243,238 chunks across 17,876 files, roughly 45 minutes of inference, to rebuild vectors for 29 PDFs. The stamp now carries a global digest plus one per extraction kind (pdf, docx, pptx, xlsx, markdown), and only the kinds whose digest moved are re-embedded. Measured end to end on a 123-file project holding four kinds: a pdf-source change re-embeds 2 files instead of 123, and an xlsx-source change 1. Everything that can change which chunker a file goes through stays in the global digest, so a classification change still invalidates every kind at once.

**Upgrading to this version reindexes once, and re-embeds once behind it.** Every existing row carries the old single parse stamp, so the first run after the upgrade treats the whole index as stale and reparses it. The re-embed comes with it. An earlier draft of this release carried neither cost past the reparse, because a database stamped by the previous release was recognised as already agreeing with the new per-kind stamps. That recognition was tied to this release's own chunking digest, so that it would lapse the moment a release genuinely changed what a chunk is cut on, and the path-spelling fix below is exactly that: chunk boundaries are cut on the symbols a file has, and on the machines that fix repairs a file had none to offer. Stored vectors keep serving while it runs, so `semantic` answers throughout rather than going dark. On a large index it is roughly 45 minutes of work in the background, one time. After it, a release that touches a single language adapter only reparses files in that language, and one that touches a single document extractor only re-embeds that format's files.

### Fixed

- **A file is indexed under the name reads look it up by**: the index stored whatever spelling the caller handed it, while every read resolves a name through one shared normaliser first. That normaliser rewrites two kinds of directory name the writer left alone: a Windows short name such as `C:/Users/RUNNER~1`, and the `/var` link macOS puts in front of its temp directory. Where a project or temp path arrived through either, the file indexed and then could not be found again, so `symbol`, `read`, `refs` and `section` all answered as though it had never been indexed. The writer now mints the stored name with the same normaliser the readers use.

- **`brief`, `refs` and `section` no longer name a comma form that fails**: given two specs, each printed advice to join them with a comma. All three join a list of `file::name` specs and none of them join a line spec, so half the advice named a command that exits with an error. Each now offers the joined form only for the shapes it really serves, and otherwise says to run the command once per spec. This is the same fix `read` received, applied to the three commands that do not share its dispatch.

- **One loose secret match no longer silences every fold for a whole file**: the three fold gates on the read hook asked the redaction pass whether a file held a secret and declined on any match. Declining hands the file to the model in full and unredacted, so a wrong match bought no safety and cost that file its skeleton, its outline and its body fold together. Measured across first-party source of 12 kB or more, 9.4% of files were silenced this way, among them a documentation comment reading `PasswordException:` and an ordinary type annotation. The redaction pass is deliberately tuned to over-match, because over-redacting something on its way to disk is free. The gates now ask a stricter question that skips only that one catch-all pattern and keeps every prefix-anchored pattern, any custom patterns, and strict mode. Nothing written to disk changes.

- **A line spec with a stray colon is declined instead of being read as a path nobody typed**: `token-goat read "src/parser.ts:1:2"` split on the last colon and went looking for `src/parser.ts:1`, reporting a file the caller never named. A Windows drive letter is still a colon that belongs in a path, so `C:142` stays a drive-relative path rather than line 142. Both now report the spec as typed.

- **The note about extra arguments names a command that runs**: passing two specs to `read` printed advice to join them with a comma, which then failed, because `read` joins symbol specs and not line specs. The note now tests the joined form against the same dispatch `read` itself uses, and offers it only when it works. Otherwise it says to run the command once per spec.

- **A refusal to serve a whole file prints a command that runs**: the block on reading a large document or config file is a hard one, so the sentence it hands back is the only next move available, and that sentence named a placeholder. `token-goat section "CHANGELOG.md::SectionHeading"` exits with `Section 'SectionHeading' not found`, which spends the next turn on syntax rather than on the file. The message now fills in the first heading or key the index really holds for that file. A file that is missing, unindexed, stale, or holds no name worth quoting keeps the old wording, and nothing is newly denied.

- **A `file:N` read reports a stale index the same way every other read does**: the line-region path repaired a stale index but skipped the check that follows it. The repair is allowed to fail safely and leave the old rows in place, and that trailing check is what turns the failure into a visible warning. Without it a line read could resolve against stale spans and say nothing.

- **`semantic` no longer competes against text decoded out of JPEGs, fonts and archives**: `token-goat index` enumerates files with `git ls-files`, which returns every tracked file whatever is in it, and the embedding step had no check between its document-extraction branch — PDF, DOCX, PPTX and XLSX, all of which do hold extractable text — and a plain UTF-8 read of the file's bytes. A JPEG fell through to that read, which does not fail; it produces mojibake, and the chunker then cut the mojibake into hundreds of tiny control-character chunks, each carrying a 384-dimensional vector that ranked against real source for every query. A census of one real machine-wide index found 333 JPEGs holding 39,477 such chunks, about 118 per image, and another 22,450 across `.webp`, `.png`, `.avif` and `.ttf`. Files with an extension that names an encoded format — images, fonts, archives, audio, video and compiled artefacts — are now indexed for symbols and never embedded, and say so in their stamp. Documents are untouched: the formats something can read text out of still embed that text.

- **One generated data file can no longer take over a quarter of the semantic index**: the only ceiling on what a single file contributed was its size in kilobytes, which does not separate generated data from source, because chunk cuts snap to structure rather than to bytes. A few hundred kilobytes of keyword snapshot — thousands of one-line keys, each its own cut — became thousands of near-identical chunks, while a source file of the same size became a few hundred meaningful ones. On the same index the 32 worst files, nearly all comfortably under the size ceiling, held 22% of every chunk on the machine. A file is now indexed for symbols only once it would contribute more than `indexing.max_chunks_per_file` chunks, default 600: the largest hand-written source file in that whole index produced 387, and the smallest generated offender 513. Raise the setting to take files back into `semantic`; the ones it admits are re-embedded rather than left skipped.

**Upgrading reclaims the chunks both gates would now reject.** Every one of those rows was written by a build that allowed it and carries a valid content stamp, so the freshness check reads them as current and would skip them forever. The first `token-goat index` or background drain on this version sweeps them out of each index it opens, deleting only files the current gates reject and only once per version. It reads no file content and runs no model inference, so it costs seconds rather than the full re-embed that invalidating the embedding stamp would have forced.

- **`token-goat callers` says when it showed you only part of the answer**: text output stopped dead at its bound — 500 rows by default, or exactly `--limit N` — with no header and no marker, so a clipped page was indistinguishable from a symbol that genuinely has that many callers. `callers normalizePath` against this repo printed 500 of 801 and said nothing. It now prints `Showing the first N of M callers (raise --limit to see the rest).` on stderr, naming the real total, so stdout stays a pure row list and piping is unchanged. `--json` already carried this as its `truncated` flag and is unaffected.

- **`token-goat reconcile` drops index rows for deleted files in a project git cannot enumerate**: the deletion pass was skipped entirely whenever the tracked-file list came back empty, which is every non-git root, so rows for files long gone from disk stayed in the index forever. The pass stats each indexed row, so a failed enumeration can no longer be mistaken for a deletion and live files are left alone; the disclosure line for that state now says which comparisons were skipped rather than claiming none ran.

- **Only a missing file counts as a deleted one during `token-goat reconcile`**: the deletion pass stats every indexed row git did not list, and any failure of that stat was read as "the file is gone". A permission-denied path, or an index row on a network or removable drive that is not currently reachable, would have queued that root's entire index for removal in one sweep. `ENOENT` and `ENOTDIR` mean deleted; every other error means the row's fate is unknown, and the row is left alone.

- **A `bash-output <id>` pointer keeps resolving to the output it was handed for**: ids for cached Bash output now fold the output into the command hash, so two runs of one command with different output land in two entries and an elision notice from earlier in the session still recalls the text it described. The plain command hash stays resolvable and points at the newest entry, so cross-run delta folding and `token-goat waste` are unaffected; `token-goat bash-history` lists one row per stored output.

- **Editing a file keeps the already-read evidence for the parts of it the edit did not touch**: a Write or Edit cleared that file's entire served-body index, so the next read of it shipped whole. In one edit-read-edit loop over a changelog, fourteen repeated reads of the same opening block cost roughly 274,000 tokens. The index is matched on the served bytes and never on line position, so a line the edit rewrote stops matching by itself while the untouched lines around it still come back withheld behind a recall pointer. The `sed` line-range ledger, matched on numbers an edit does move, is still cleared on every edit.

- **`token-goat stats` shows the top files of the current session again**: the section was read from a directory nothing has ever written to, so it was absent from every run and looked like "nothing worth reporting". It now reads the same session store the hooks write to, and prints the most-read files of the latest session.

- **token-goat follows `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` everywhere Claude Code does**: every path to Claude Code's config home was pinned to `~/.claude`, so a user who relocates that home got an install which reported success and then did nothing. The hooks shim, the user `settings.json` wiring, the CLAUDE.md routing block and the skill all landed in a tree Claude Code never reads. The read side was equally dead: the skill cache, the agent roster the spawn hook advises from, transcript discovery for `waste` and the Bash hook, the bootstrap audit, and the CLAUDE.md and MEMORY.md walks all looked in the wrong place. Setting the variable now moves all of them together, matching how Claude Code resolves its own config home. With the variable unset, `~/.claude` is used exactly as before.

- **The MCP tools read the skills and transcripts of a relocated Claude Code config home**: the root allowlist that lets all 17 MCP tools inspect trees outside a workspace named `~/.claude/skills` and `~/.claude/projects` literally, so a user who sets `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` had every tool refuse the only copy of those trees that exists. Both entries now hang off the same config-home accessor the rest of token-goat uses, and they replace the home-relative pair rather than sit beside it, since an allowlist should not admit a directory Claude Code no longer uses. Only the named children are admitted, never the config home itself, so pointing the variable at a drive root grants that drive's `skills` and `projects` and nothing more. With the variable unset the allowlist is byte-for-byte what it was.

## [2.9.18] - 2026-09-19

### Fixed

- **Scratch files written by shell commands under the OS temp dir no longer enter the index**: only edits made through the Edit and Write tools skipped the temp dir, so a file written by a shell redirect, `tee`, `sed -i` or a git command became a permanent index row. One real index held 3,186 of them. Every enqueue path now skips the temp dir, and `token-goat project prune` removes rows already indexed.

- **`token-goat doctor` says which command will shrink an oversized index, or that none will**: the over-1GB warning always recommended a reclaim, which on a 2,250 MB index freed 1.3 MB. It now reads the free-page count and the number of indexed temp-dir files and points to `reclaim-index`, to `project prune`, or to neither when the size is live index data.

- **`doctor`'s unmapped-tool list shows each tool once**: a tool seen by both the pre and post hooks was listed twice.

- **Compressed command output no longer starts with "[early-exit: normalisation alone sufficient]"**: the line named the filter's internal branch and appeared on 773 outputs across 159 recorded sessions without telling the model anything it could use.

- **`doctor` no longer reports the compaction manifest channel as dead while it works**: summaries name files relative to the project root, and the check only counted paths repeated in full, so every compaction scored zero. Relative paths now count.

## [2.9.17] - 2026-09-18

### Added

- **`token-goat locate <spec>` finds a symbol or landmark and prints its exact line span**: it takes `name` or `file::name`, falls back to the closest names by edit distance when nothing matches exactly, narrows to one file with `--file`, caps results with `--limit`, and emits the shared envelope with `--json`.

### Fixed

- **The large-output hint keeps its `bash-output` pointer when the command contains quotes**: the hint used to quote the whole command in its suggestion, and any command holding a double quote, `$`, a backtick or a newline had the entire suggestion replaced with "command omitted", pointer included. In recorded sessions 60 of 75 of these hints arrived with nothing to act on. The hint now leads with the recall pointer, offers `compress -c` only for a command it can quote, and stays silent on token-goat's own commands, whose output is already the narrow form.

- **A `node -e` script that reads a file and writes the same file back is no longer denied**: an in-place edit is not a read into context, and denying it only pushed the identical script into a file that then ran unchecked. 7 of 29 `node -e` denies across 300 recorded sessions were this shape. A script that writes a different file than the one it read is still denied.

- **After an upgrade, the session-start note no longer says every file changed outside the session**: files reindexed only because the parser or chunker changed are unchanged on disk, but the note counted them as edits, so one session opened with "1484 changed" when two files had. They now get their own clause ("unchanged on disk but indexed by an older version of token-goat"), and the changed/new/removed breakdown counts only real drift.

## [2.9.16] - 2026-09-18

### Fixed

- **Semantic search now covers the whole of every chunk it stores, so a match in the second half of a long function is found**: chunks were capped at 8,000 characters, but the embedding model reads 512 wordpieces, about 1,700 characters of code, and silently drops the rest, so the vector stored for a longer chunk described only its head while the chunk's line range claimed all of it. On this repository's own index, 17.3% of chunks were over that limit and 49.5% of the chunked text was never embedded; a query for the TIFF page-loop guard in `probeBufferMeta` ranked the chunk holding it 10,442nd of 18,102 by vector distance, and now ranks it first. Chunks are now cut with the model's own tokenizer so none is longer than what the model reads, and a single line too long for one chunk, such as minified code, is split across several. The same repository now indexes to 27,296 chunks instead of 18,102, a 12% larger database, and a full index takes about twice as long, because that text is now actually embedded. Upgrading keeps the existing vectors answering searches and re-embeds each file as reconcile or `token-goat index` reaches it.

- **`semantic` reports the best-matching part of a long function instead of the worst one**: results are grouped by the symbol that encloses them, and when several chunks of one function matched, each later and weaker chunk replaced the one before it, so the row showed the least relevant range at the least favorable rank. A query whose best chunk was the third-closest in the whole index dropped out of the top 25; it now leads the list.

- **Semantic search keeps answering through an upgrade that changes how files are chunked**: when only the code that decides chunk text and boundaries has moved, the model and runtime are the same and stored vectors still compare correctly with new queries, so they are kept and every embedded file is marked for re-embedding. Before, every stored vector on the machine was deleted at once, and `semantic` came back empty in every project until each one was reindexed by hand. The session-start drift sweep and `token-goat reconcile` queue those files for the background worker, which rebuilds each file's chunks in place, and `token-goat index` rebuilds them all. A change of model, model revision or inference runtime still discards the stored vectors, since they no longer share a space with new queries. This release changes the chunking digest, so upgrading re-embeds in the background.

- **A photo shot on a phone or DSLR held vertically reaches the model upright instead of sheared into two striped copies of itself**: a JPEG carrying an EXIF orientation tag stores its pixels in landscape and leaves a viewer to rotate them, and the header probe reported that rotated, display-order size -- correct for `image-meta`, which answers what a viewer shows -- while the shrink path handed the same numbers to the resizer as if they described the decoded buffer, so every row of a 3000x2000 frame was read at a 2000-pixel stride. Orientations 5-8 arrived as a stride-mismatch shear; 3 and 4 survived the stride but arrived upside down, since the re-encode writes no EXIF block for a viewer to act on. The decoders now hand back their own dimensions and the frame is physically rotated before the resize, so what the model sees is what a viewer sees. Rotating a photo upright costs it some of its compression: an orientation-6 landscape photo re-encodes roughly 45% larger than the sheared output it replaces. Entries already in the shrink cache are re-encoded rather than served, since the cache key now carries an engine revision.

- **An animated GIF reaches the model as a moving picture instead of one correct frame followed by black**: the GIF decoder allocated a fresh zeroed canvas per frame, but a GIF frame paints only its own sub-rectangle and skips its transparent pixels, so every frame after the first in a delta-optimised GIF -- what `gifsicle`, ImageMagick's `-layers Optimize`, and every screen recorder emit -- arrived with only its small changed patch on black. A 6-frame 2400x600 capture was delivered with frames 1 through 5 at 41.6% black, and the notice still read "65% smaller", because black compresses to nearly nothing: the percentage was true of the bytes and false of the picture. Frames are now composited onto a running canvas, honouring disposal 2 (clear the rectangle) and 3 (restore what was under it), and a transparent input keeps a reserved palette index instead of flattening to black. A delta-optimised GIF will now often decline to shrink, because a composited full-frame re-encode is larger than the delta original it came from; it passes through at full size and full fidelity rather than arriving small and broken.

- **A delta-optimised animated GIF is shrunk instead of passing through untouched**: the decoder composites every frame to a full canvas, and the encoder then wrote each one back as a full-canvas frame, throwing away the delta encoding the source almost certainly had -- a 757 KB, 14-frame 1100x1100 GIF from ImageMagick `-layers Optimize` re-encoded to 5.3 MB, seven times its input, so the size guard declined it and the original went to the model whole. That is every real animated GIF: `gifsicle`, ImageMagick, and every screen recorder emit sub-rectangle frames. Each frame after the first is now diffed against the previous output frame and written as the bounding box of what changed, with disposal 1 so the next frame still inherits the region it leaves in place, against one global colour table rather than 768 bytes of duplicate palette per frame; the same file now shrinks to 407 KB with every frame decoding to the same picture as before. A frame identical to the one before it is written as a 1x1 rectangle rather than dropped, so its delay is still honoured. A pixel that goes opaque to transparent cannot be expressed by a frame that leaves the previous one in place, so the frame before it is widened to cover the pixels going transparent and disposed with 2, and the frame after repaints what that clear wiped. An animation whose delta re-encode still lands above the original is declined exactly as before. The encoder itself now loads only when an animated GIF actually arrives, so it is no longer part of what the hook parses on every tool call.

- **A JPEG is bounded by a limit this engine states rather than by whatever jpeg-js defaults to**: every other decoder calls `assertDecodableSize` before allocating, but `decodeJpeg` handed the buffer to jpeg-js with no options at all, so a JPEG's declared dimensions were held only to the library's 100MP default -- looser than the 67.1MP the engine's own decode ceiling allows at 4 bytes a pixel, and not a number this codebase had chosen. The call now passes that 67.1MP resolution limit explicitly, and pins the memory limit at 512MB rather than deriving one: jpeg-js measures its own coefficient buffers, about 14 bytes a pixel, so a figure computed from a 4-bytes-a-pixel ceiling would refuse a routine 20MP camera photo.

- **`pdf-locate` misses phrases the PDF plainly contains, and plain `pdf-extract` (no `--layout`) collapses every page to one line**: both built page text by joining pdfjs's text items with a literal space at every boundary, which inserts a space pdfjs never reported (turning `LLC.` next to `, a` into `LLC. ,`, so a pattern spanning that boundary never matched) and discards every line break pdfjs does report (`hasEOL: true`), so `--head`, `--tail`, and `--grep` on plain `pdf-extract` operated on whole pages rather than lines. pdfjs already marks inter-word gaps as explicit `" "` items and line ends as `hasEOL`, so the items are now joined on those flags instead of a fixed separator: a newline in `pdf-extract`'s plain mode (matching `--layout` line-for-line on a single-column page), a space in `pdf-locate` (so a phrase that legitimately wraps across a line break still matches within one page). Upgrading reindexes.

- **An equation authored in Word or PowerPoint reaches `docx-text`, `pptx-text`, `pptx-slide` and the embeddings index**: Office Math (OMML) keeps its text in `m:t` runs, never the `w:t`/`a:t` the readers matched, so every equation was dropped without trace -- an inline `E=mc²` left a double space mid-sentence, a display equation deleted its whole paragraph, a PowerPoint equation shape contributed nothing to slide text or the outline's character count, and `--grep "E=m"` found nothing in a file that plainly contains it. Nothing failed, because prose either side still read as prose. Matching `m:t` on the parsed tree is not the fix: fast-xml-parser folds consecutive same-name siblings into one array keyed at the first, so the equation arrives after the paragraph's tail rather than between the runs it sits between. The equation is now rewritten into an ordinary text run of the host namespace before the part is parsed, which keeps it in document order; PowerPoint's `a14:m` wrapper is dropped at the same time so the run is a true paragraph child. Word's own linear reading is used for the glue, so `E=mc²` reads as `E=mc2`. Upgrading reindexes.

- **`eslint` output names every file past the 200-line cap instead of collapsing to a head/tail slice**: the eslint compressor grouped only warnings by rule and kept every error line verbatim, and never measured its own combined output against the 200-line cap the shipping `balanced` profile ships into, so a run with hundreds of error lines fell through to the generic tail truncator, which keeps the first and last few lines rather than picking survivors by file identity; a real 40-file, 443-line capture (240 errors, 120 warnings) shipped with 1 of 40 file headers, and the handful of surviving error lines belonged to a file whose own header had already been cut. Every existing test drove the filter through the 1000-line default the shipping path never uses, so none of them caught it. Files are now rendered in three tiers, escalating only as far as the cap forces: unchanged if it already fits, errors additionally capped 3-per-rule-per-file with a "+N more" marker if that alone is not enough, and finally one line per file naming its error and warning counts by rule if the run is large enough that even the capped per-rule form cannot fit every file under the cap. A run already under the cap ships byte-identical.

- **Plain `diff`/`diff3`/`sdiff` output (not `git diff`) names every file on a multi-file change, and its normal-format branch discloses how much it actually dropped**: the unified-diff path capped hunks per file but never measured its combined output against the 200-line cap the shipping `balanced` profile ships into, and the compressor didn't even accept that cap as an argument, so a real 9-file, 319-line `diff -ru` capture fell through to the generic tail truncator and shipped with 1 of 9 headers, survivors picked by an error-keyword match against ordinary source text rather than by file identity. Separately, the non-unified (`3c3`/`<`/`>`) branch passed a fixed cap of 300 -- above the 200-line shipping cap -- so its own elision marker was itself among the lines the outer truncation then cut, disclosing a smaller drop than the real one (274 of 839 lines, not the true 668). Files are now collapsed to header plus a one-line summary, in order, only as many as it takes to fit the cap, so every filename survives even when its hunk body doesn't; the normal-format cap now leaves room for its own marker so the disclosed count is the real one.

- **`git diff` and `git show` name every file on a change spanning more than a handful of files**: the diff compressor bounded one hunk and one file but never measured its own combined output against the 200-line cap the shipping `balanced` profile ships into, so any diff whose per-hunk-compressed form still exceeded 200 lines -- an ordinary 9-to-25-file change -- fell through to the generic tail truncator, which keeps survivors by an error-keyword match rather than by diff structure; a real 9-file, 346-line capture from this repo shipped with 1 of 9 `diff --git` headers, the file list being the one thing missing. Files are now collapsed to header plus a one-line summary, in order, only as many as it takes to fit the cap, so earlier files keep their hunks and every filename survives; a diff already under the cap ships byte-identical.

- **`git log --stat`, `--name-only`, `--numstat` and `--name-status` keep every file line on a repository with multi-paragraph commit messages**: the stat-family compressor capped only the stat/patch lines per commit and left each commit's message body uncapped, so on a repo where commit bodies run to dozens of lines a 12-commit stat-shaped log reached 335-347 raw lines -- past the 200-line cap the shipping `balanced` profile actually enforces -- before the generic tail-truncation cap ever saw it, which then picked survivors by an error-keyword match against commit-message prose rather than by git structure, dropping 39 of 43 file lines while still reporting a clean compression percentage. Every existing test drove the filter through a 1000-line default the shipping path never uses, so none of them caught it. Each commit's message body is now collapsed to its header summary (hash, author, date, subject) only when the block's own line count would otherwise blow the cap it ships into, so a log that already fits is unaffected.

- **`git log --name-only`, `--numstat`, and `--stat=<width>` keep every filename**: the log filter's shape-detection set named `--stat`, `--shortstat`, and `--name-status`, but not `--name-only` or `--numstat`, and it matched `--stat` only by exact flag equality, so a `--stat=200` never matched it either; each of the three fell through to the plain-log collapser (above 10 commits) or the `--oneline` hash-only branch (at any commit count), both of which discard every line that is neither a commit header nor an indented subject -- a bare file path or a `N\tM\tpath` line is neither, so every filename or stat line vanished silently while the log still looked complete. All three flags are now recognised as a stat-family shape and pass through the per-commit block unchanged, the same as `--name-status` already did.

- **The pytest filter drops the default per-file progress line, not only the bare-dots continuation shape**: pytest leads each file's result run with its path (`tests/test_foo.py ..F.s    [ 50%]`) and prints bare dots only when a run wraps onto a second line, but the filter's progress pattern matched only the bare form, so every per-file line of a default run passed through. On a short failing run the surviving lines kept the saving below the net-benefit floor and the whole output shipped verbatim with no marker. A second pattern now recognises the path-led form when it ends in the percent column.

- **The `git blame --porcelain` filter names the author on every collapsed run, not just a commit's first one**: porcelain output prints a commit's `author`/`committer`/`summary` block only the first time that commit appears; every later run of the same commit is a bare `<sha> <orig> <final> [<n>]` header plus its content line. The filter re-learned the author from each run's own metadata and reset it to nothing at every new header, so on a real file 20 of 21 collapse notes read `[token-goat: 5 more lines by null (52d699ab)]`. Nothing failed, because the note was still well-formed and the only fixture repeated full metadata on every line, which is `--line-porcelain` shape, not `--porcelain`. The author is now remembered per commit hash across the whole output.

- **`xlsx-head`, `xlsx-range`, `xlsx-columns` and `xlsx-query` leave a never-calculated formula cell empty instead of printing 0**: a producer that writes formulas without evaluating them (openpyxl does this for every formula, and so does any writer that leaves calculation to Excel) emits `<c><f>A1*2</f></c>` with no cached `<v>` at all. The reader's numeric path converted the missing element with `Number('')`, which is 0, so every such cell read as a literal zero: a number the workbook never held, indistinguishable from a real cached 0 and invisible because 0 is a plausible spreadsheet value. The reader now reports a cell with no `<v>` text as empty, the display layer prints that as an empty field rather than the word `null`, and `--formulas` still shows the formula itself. In the same branch, a formula whose cached result is a boolean printed the JS spelling `false` next to typed boolean cells that print `FALSE`; both now use Excel's spelling. Upgrading reindexes.

- **`transcript` and `transcript-outline` decode WebVTT cue text escapes**: WebVTT has no way to write a literal `&` or `<` in cue text or a speaker annotation except as one of its six named escapes (`&amp;`, `&lt;`, `&gt;`, `&lrm;`, `&rlm;`, `&nbsp;`), and the parser stripped tags and passed those escapes through verbatim, so `<v Tom &amp; Jerry>` listed a speaker named `Tom &amp; Jerry`, the cue text read `Tom &amp; Jerry`, and a `--grep "Tom & Jerry"` slice matched nothing. Nothing failed, because the output was still readable text. The escapes are now decoded, after the tag strip so an escaped `&lt;i&gt;` stays the author's literal `<i>`, and only under a `WEBVTT` signature: SRT defines no escape syntax, so its `&amp;` remains the author's own bytes.

- **`transcript` and `transcript-outline` keep the speaker of a `<v>` tag that carries more than one class**: WebVTT lets a voice span start with any number of `.class` names before the speaker (`<v.first.loud Esme>` is the spec's own example), but the tag pattern accepted at most one, so a two-class tag matched nothing, the tag was then stripped as generic markup and the cue came out with no speaker at all, `transcript --speaker` could not select it and the outline's speaker list omitted the name. Nothing reported it, since a cue without a speaker is a legitimate shape. The pattern now accepts zero or more classes.

- **A Word text box is read once, not twice**: Word writes a text box as `mc:AlternateContent`, carrying the box's paragraphs under `mc:Choice` (the `w:drawing` form) and again, character for character, under `mc:Fallback` (the older `w:pict` form), and a consumer is meant to read one branch or the other. The docx readers walked both, so `docx-text` printed a box reading `Datum plane` as `Datum planeDatum plane`, the two copies joined with nothing between them, and the same doubled text went into the embeddings. Nothing signalled it, since a repeated phrase is not an error, and none of the documents this reader was checked against held a text box. The Fallback is now dropped from the parsed tree wherever a Choice stands beside it, before any paragraph, heading or table is collected; a Fallback with no Choice is kept, as it is then the only copy. Upgrading reindexes.

- **`docx-text`, `docx-outline` and `docx-tables` keep a Word tab as a tab**: Word writes a tab as an empty `<w:tab/>` element between `<w:t>` runs rather than as a character, and the readers collected only `<w:t>`, so `Section 1.<w:tab/>Definitions.` came out as `Section 1.Definitions.`; a Word-saved NDA on this machine had 16 of them and every one printed as nothing, with no error to show a character had gone. The parse tree cannot restore it, since same-name siblings fold into one array and the tab's place among the runs is lost, so the reader now rewrites each `<w:tab/>` into a `<w:t>` run holding the tab, and `<w:br/>`/`<w:cr/>` into one holding a newline, before the part is parsed; those the parse keeps in order, so the character lands exactly where Word put it, including between two text elements of the same run. Tab-stop definitions (`<w:tab w:val=".." w:pos=".."/>`) are untouched, and a manual page break still produces no paragraph of its own. Upgrading reindexes.

- **`trace` reads a JVM stack trace as logback prints it**: the frame pattern required the line to end at the closing `)`, so a frame carrying logback packaging data, `~[spring-boot-3.2.0.jar:3.2.0]` or `~[na:na]`, matched nothing; because the first frame decides whether the header line is a trace at all, the whole exception came back as "no traceback found" rather than a shorter frame list. Spring Boot turns packaging data on by default, so this covered every Spring Boot log. The header pattern also required `: message`, which the JDK omits when the message is null, so a `Caused by: java.lang.reflect.InvocationTargetException` block vanished even from plain `java` output. Both patterns now accept those forms; verified against a captured logback 1.5.32 run on OpenJDK 17.

- **`trace` keeps the exception line when the innermost Python frame has no source context**: the parser decided that the line after a `File "...", line N, in f` frame was that frame's source line whenever it was not another `File` or `Traceback` line, so an exception line that directly followed a context-less frame was absorbed as context and the block's exception came back empty. CPython omits the source line for frozen modules, `exec()` strings, and REPL input, and a `ModuleNotFoundError` always ends in a `<frozen importlib._bootstrap>` frame, so the most common import failure lost its exception text while the frame list looked normal. The parser now also requires the candidate line to be indented, which CPython does for every source line and never for an exception line.

- **`todo` reports a marker that follows a rejected word on the same line**: the scanner judged each line by its first kind word only, so `// Note that this is a hack; FIXME: replace it` lost its FIXME when the prose "Note" was rejected, and `label = "Note" // TODO: localize` lost its TODO when the quoted "Note" was excluded. Nothing signalled the loss: the line simply did not appear, the same as a line with no marker. Every occurrence on the line is now a candidate and the first genuine one outside a string wins; on this repository that recovers 7 markers the previous build never listed.

- **A Groovy generic method with an explicit return type is indexed**: `static <T> T first(List<T> self) {` was dropped because the C-style header reader requires the text before `(` to begin with a letter, and once the `static` modifier is stripped that text begins with `<`. The drop was silent and selective, since `def <T> T first(...)` begins with `def` and was indexed normally, so a class mixing the two spellings looked partially indexed rather than broken. The leading type-parameter list, bounded forms such as `<T extends Comparable<T>>` included, is now removed before the header is read, at class-member and script level alike. Upgrading reindexes.

- **C# `event` members are indexed**: neither spelling had a pattern that reached it. The field-like `public event EventHandler Changed;` has no accessor block for the property matcher to find, and `public event EventHandler Renamed { add { } remove { } }` carries `add`/`remove` where that matcher requires `get`/`set`, so events were the one C# member category missing from the index entirely while the properties and methods declared beside them were indexed normally. A declaration with several declarators, `public event EventHandler<Foo, Bar> Opened = null, Closed;`, contributes one symbol per declarator without mistaking the comma inside the generic argument list for a separator, and an `extern` or `unsafe` event is recognised alongside the `extern` and `unsafe` methods that already were. The accessor-block form is claimed as an event before the property matcher sees it, because that matcher only needs the substring `get` somewhere after the opening brace, which an `add` accessor's own body can supply, and the member would otherwise be indexed twice. Upgrading reindexes.

- **A C# `delegate` is filed as a type rather than an interface**: a delegate declares a function type, so `outline` and `symbol` reported a construct the language does not have, and put delegates in the same bucket as the real `interface` declarations beside them, which made a kind filter over a mixed file useless. They are now `type`, matching what the other language adapters use for the closest analog, a type alias naming a function signature. Upgrading reindexes.

- **The npm filter no longer deletes script output lines that merely contain the word "deprecated"**: the general deprecation pattern was unanchored, so it matched any line with that word anywhere in it, not just npm's own `npm WARN deprecated pkg@ver: msg` lines. `npm test`/`npm run <script>` fall through to this filter whenever the script is a compound command (`vitest run && tsc --noEmit`, for example), so a vitest suite named `deprecated flag handling`, or any failing test whose name contains the word, had its `FAIL` headers, `×` lines and code frames deleted wholesale and replaced with a fabricated `collapsed N deprecation warnings across M packages: <unknown>` trailer, while the pass/fail counts in the footer survived untouched. The pattern is now anchored to npm's own `npm warn deprecated ...` line shape, which still collapses real npm deprecation noise.

- **A recall pointer named by the already-served-lines notice now returns the lines it named, even after a later Read of a different window**: every windowed Read of one file in one session stored its served bytes under an id keyed only on the file path and cwd, never the offset or limit, so a second Read of a different window overwrote the first window's stored body under the same id. The notice handed to the model still named the old id, but `token-goat bash-output <id> --full` then returned whatever window was read most recently, not the lines the notice claimed were withheld -- a model told to recall specific lines got back a different, unrelated slice of the file. The same collision also meant only one served window per file could ever be tracked, so a fourth Read repeating an earlier window went unelided. The Bash surface never had this bug, because its command text already carries the requested range (`sed -n '120,160p'` hashes differently from `sed -n '100,140p'`); the Read surface's synthetic store key now carries the same offset/limit, so distinct windows of one file get distinct ids and each notice's pointer keeps returning the bytes it named.

- **A `.tif` file reaches the model as an image again instead of being denied as an oversized text file**: `.tiff` was recognised, `.tif` was not, so a TIFF using the shorter, more common spelling missed the image path entirely and hit the same large-file deny meant for oversized source or log files, offering commands like `token-goat symbol`/`skeleton` that make no sense against a binary image. `.tif` is now recognised alongside `.tiff`; the other common alternate-spelling pairs this set already covers (`.jpg`/`.jpeg`, `.heic`/`.heif`) were already both present.

- **A routine 24-megapixel camera or phone photo now reaches the model shrunk instead of untouched, and the ceiling that used to block it silently now shows up in `token-goat stats`**: `image_shrink.max_image_pixels` shipped at 16,000,000, well under a common 24MP DSLR or 48MP phone sensor and under the exact input this feature exists to shrink, and the header probe that enforces it swallowed its own rejection into the same bare "not a candidate" result as a corrupt or unsupported file, so an oversized photo passed through full-size with no stat, no skip, nothing to show why it never shrank. The default is now 64,000,000, chosen against the image engine's own hard decode ceiling (67,108,864 pixels) rather than picked arbitrarily, and a distinct `image_shrink_over_pixel_limit` stat is now recorded whenever the configured limit -- default or user-set -- is the reason a file was never a shrink candidate.

- **`token-goat fetch-image` shrinks a fetched image that is small in bytes but far over the normal resize target, matching what a `Read` of the same bytes from disk already does**: the pre-read hook qualifies an image on pixel dimensions as well as byte size, but `cmdFetchImage` called the shrink path with its plain 512KiB byte-size default and no dimension check, so a fetched image well under that threshold in bytes but well past the resize target in width or height -- a flat-colour or heavily-compressed PNG, for instance -- was written to disk untouched, while the identical bytes read from a local file were shrunk. `cmdFetchImage` now qualifies on dimensions the same way the read hook does.

- **Grep can search build output**: a Grep whose path was under `dist/` or another build or generated directory was refused with "Generated/build artifact, read the source file instead", as if a search were a full read of the bundle. Grep is now exempt, as it already was from every other read deny; a Read of build output is still redirected to the source.

- **Reading a file in ranges no longer blocks a later full read of it**: every Read counted toward the "already read this session, unchanged" deny whether or not it asked for a line range, so two ranged reads of a file the model had never seen whole could get the next unranged read, or even a second ranged read of different lines, hard-denied. Only a full read now arms the whole-file denies, including the size-based one that a single range of a large file used to trigger, and only full reads since the last context compaction count toward them; repeating the exact same range is still caught separately.

- **A repeated line-range read shows the new content when the file changed outside the session**: the deny for re-reading a range already served fired from the session's range history alone, so an edit made by your editor, another tool or another process was hidden behind "already read this session". The deny now fires only when the file is confirmed unchanged since that range was read, by its stored snapshot or, for large and non-text files that have none, by its size and modification time; otherwise the range history for that file is cleared and the read goes through.

- **A file deleted from the project is removed from the index even when the first attempt fails**: a failed removal was silently ignored, so the deleted file's symbols stayed in search results. It is now logged to the worker error log and retried within the same retry budget as a failed reindex, without holding up the rest of the batch.

- **`token-goat reconcile` stops re-hashing every file and stops reporting timestamp drift that never happened**: the stored modification time was in seconds and the file's current one in milliseconds, so the cheap timestamp check never matched, every run read and hashed the whole tracked set, and the summary claimed "N files had a newer timestamp but identical content" for all of them. On this repository that line said 1221 files; it now says 1, the one file actually touched.

- **Hooks recognise tool names however a harness spells them**: Copilot CLI, Codex and VS Code tool names are now matched case-insensitively, ignoring underscores and dashes and a `server:` prefix, so a spelling such as `web_search` or a prefixed built-in reaches the same handler as its standard name instead of passing through with no token-goat handling at all.

- **`brief` warns when the file behind a symbol has been deleted**: it printed the indexed body, callers and line numbers of a deleted file as if current, while `read` flagged it. It now prints the same deleted-file warning, and `--json` output carries `"deleted": true`.

- **The background indexer restarts itself after an upgrade**: a worker started before `npm install -g` or an upgrade kept running the old code until the machine restarted or someone killed it by hand, so indexing fixes in the new version never took effect. The worker now records which build it runs, and the next hook that finds it running an older copy of the same install stops it and starts a new one. A worker started by a different install sharing the same data folder is left alone.

- **`screenshot` waits up to 60 seconds for a page to load instead of 30**: slow pages that were still settling at 30 seconds failed to capture.

- **A symbol lookup made through MCP with an explicit project root resolves in that project when cross-project symbols are off**: with `indexing.cross_project_symbols = false`, the lookup was confined to the current working directory's project rather than the project the caller named, so the symbol was not found.

### Added

- **`section` finds a heading from a close but inexact name**: when no exact or widened match exists, a heading that differs by a typo, punctuation, a plural or stem, or an extra or missing word (for example "Pre-Commit Hook" for "Pre-Commit Hook Configurations") is now matched, as long as one heading is clearly the best candidate. `insert-section` still requires an exact heading, so it never writes under a guessed one.

- **`token-goat doctor --repair` (alias `--fix`) fixes what doctor finds**: it restores permissive read confinement and cross-project symbol search if they were turned off, turns networking and embeddings back on if the semantic model is missing because of offline mode, and downloads and verifies the model files. Doctor now also checks that the embedding model files are present, groups its results by category in plainer language, and points to `--repair` when a warning can be fixed automatically.

- **A hint for PowerShell `ConvertFrom-Json` pipelines and direct `jq` reads of a config file**: `Get-Content file.json | ConvertFrom-Json`, `[IO.File]::ReadAllText(...)` piped the same way, and `jq .key file.json` now get a pointer to `token-goat json-query`, and the existing `cat | jq` hint now suggests `json-query` as well.

- **A hint when a shell command prints 4KB or more uncompressed**: the hint gives the `token-goat compress -c` form of the command and a `bash-output` id for the stored output. It fires once per command per session, only for successful commands that token-goat could not already compress, and not when `TOKEN_GOAT_BASH_COMPRESS=0`. The command output is fenced with the hint outside the fence and ahead of it, so the pointer back to the full output survives truncation and nothing in the output can pass for token-goat text, and the hint is not emitted on harnesses whose hook cannot replace a tool result.

- **`session-audit` accepts a single `.jsonl` transcript, answers to `audit-session`, and finds VS Code and Cursor chat transcripts**: when `~/.claude/projects` has no transcripts, the default scan now looks in the VS Code, VS Code Insiders and Cursor workspace storage folders.

### Changed

- **MCP reads are no longer confined to the project root by default**: `mcp.confine_reads_to_project_root` now defaults to `false`, and doctor reports confinement being off as the recommended setup rather than a warning. With confinement turned on, reads under the standard skills, transcript and editor-storage folders (`~/.claude/skills`, `~/.claude/projects`, `~/.copilot/skills`, `~/.copilot/session-state`, the VS Code and Cursor workspace storage folders, and editor extension folders) and any root in `mcp.allowed_roots` are now allowed.
