- **A second full Read of a JSON or HTML file of 8KB or more is denied with a pointer to a narrow read**: it now gets the same treatment as a re-read of a markdown file, naming `json-query` or `json-outline` for JSON and `section` or `outline` for HTML. YAML re-read hints now name `yaml-query` alongside `section`.

- **The markdown hint suggesting `section` or `outline` now fires on files of 4KB or more instead of 8KB**.

## [2.9.15] - 2026-09-17

### Fixed

- **Dart methods and getters whose return type carries a nullable type argument are indexed again**: `Future<Invoice?> find(String id)`, `List<String?> names()`, `Map<String, int?> counts()` and `Future<int?> get pending` were all dropped, in classes and at top level alike, which in a null-safe codebase is most of a repository interface. The return-type matcher stopped at the `?` inside the angle brackets, so the name it expected next was never there; a `?` that sits last, `Invoice? peek()`, was reached by a separate trailing group and survived, which is why the gap was invisible in the fixtures. The character class now admits `?` wherever it sits in the type. Upgrading reindexes.

- **A PHP constructor with promoted parameters on their own lines now spans its whole body, and the parameters are no longer filed as properties above it**: `private readonly Suit $suit,` inside the parameter list is spelled exactly like a property declaration, so each promoted parameter became a `var` symbol, and because a member's span search stops at the next symbol's start line, `__construct` was left at its header line while the last parameter claimed the constructor body as its own span. The extractor now tracks parenthesis depth across lines and does not read a property declaration inside an open parameter list. A `#[...]` attribute whose arguments run over several lines, as Symfony constraints on a promoted parameter are written, counts toward that depth too: its opening line used to be skipped as a `#` comment while its closing line was not. Upgrading reindexes.

- **`token-goat xml-query` no longer answers a filtering query with the unfiltered list**: a predicate the parser has no case for, such as the well-formed `//book[not(@archived)]`, parsed to nothing and was then skipped, so the step ran with no filter at all and returned every book in the catalog as a confident answer. An unreadable predicate now matches nothing, the same treatment an unclosed one already got, so you get an empty result instead of a wrong one, and an arm of an `and` or `or` that cannot be read now refuses the whole expression rather than being quietly dropped from it. An empty clause, `book[]`, is the same kind of typo and now answers the same way, where it used to read as no clause at all. Two more defects in the same predicate parser are fixed alongside it: an attribute value delimited by one quote character could not contain the other, so `//book[@name='A"B']` matched nothing rather than the book it names, in `=`, `!=`, `contains()` and `starts-with()` clauses alike; and `and` was split before `or`, which grouped `@a='1' or @b='2' and @c='3'` as `(A or B) and C` instead of the `A or (B and C)` XPath 1.0 requires, answering a three-book catalog with one book instead of two.

- **A Dart string literal no longer fabricates a method in the index**: `String a = ' Foo baz(';` filed a phantom method named `baz`. Dart method declarations carry no keyword, so the matcher has to recognise a bare `Type name(`, which ordinary prose satisfies; it was the one declaration matcher in the adapter not anchored to the start of the line, and the only one a literal could reach. Declaration matching now reads a copy of the line with string contents blanked, which leaves real declarations untouched, including one whose parameter carries a string default. Kotlin, Swift, Go and TypeScript were checked for the same shape and do not fabricate symbols, because each anchors on a keyword. Upgrading reindexes.

- **A deeply nested type bound no longer swallows the rest of a Dart file, or drops the declaration outright in Kotlin, Swift and Dart**: the shared type-parameter clause matched three levels of `<>`, so a bound like `<T extends Comparable<Map<String, List<int>>>>` overflowed it. A Kotlin `typealias` and `fun`, a Swift `func` and a Dart `extension` were then dropped from the index, a Dart `typedef` fell through to the function matcher and filed a phantom symbol named `Function`, and a Dart mixin-application class pushed a scope frame that nothing ever popped, so every top-level declaration after it in the file vanished or was filed under the wrong parent. The clause now covers five levels, and a Dart `class` line that closes with `;` without opening a brace no longer pushes a frame whatever its type parameters look like, so a bound past any future ceiling costs that one declaration instead of the file. Upgrading reindexes.

- **The `gh api` scope hint no longer blames your token for a 404 it cannot read that way**: any non-zero exit on a call whose URL mentioned `/advisories`, `/security_advisories` or `security_events` produced "try gh auth refresh -s security_events", without looking at the error text at all, so a typo'd owner, a repo with advisories switched off, a rate limit, a network failure, or gh's own `invalid API endpoint` complaint under Git Bash all told you to re-authenticate. Live captures show an owned repo with advisories disabled and a wholly nonexistent owner/repo return byte-identical 404 bodies, so the hint now says exactly that ambiguity and points at the spelling first; a non-zero exit that never produced a 404 says nothing. In exchange the hint recognises `You are not authorized to perform this operation`, which is what a real authorization wall on the dependabot and security endpoints actually returns and was missing from the phrase list, and which the blanket branch had been hiding.

- **`token-goat symbol NAME` no longer serves a stale body when the file changed outside the edit hooks**: the `--file` form reindexed the named file before querying, but the bare form, the one `symbol --help` documents first, named no file and so skipped the check entirely. After an edit the hooks never see (a shell `sed -i`, an external editor, a `git checkout`), it returned the old body with nothing to say it was old, while `read "file::symbol"` on the same file self-healed and returned the current one. It now reindexes the files its own results came from and asks again. A row whose reindex was attempted and failed is tagged `⚠ STALE` rather than served silently, and a row whose file is gone is still tagged `⚠ DELETED` with the last thing the index saw, since reindexing that one would delete the only copy left.

- **Word and PowerPoint text no longer loses the boundaries between paragraphs, runs and nested tables**: `docx-tables` built each cell's text from a walk of the cell's whole subtree joined with nothing, so a cell holding two paragraphs came back as `Line oneLine two` and a cell holding a nested table came back as `N00N01N10N11` with the nested table never reported as a table of its own. The pptx commands joined text runs with a space, and OOXML splits one word into two runs at every formatting change, so a title reading `TokenGoat` with only `Goat` in bold rendered as `Token Goat` and `pptx-text-grep TokenGoat` found nothing. Runs inside a paragraph are now concatenated, paragraphs are separated, a nested table is reported as its own table with its cells kept out of the parent, and an explicit `w:br`/`a:br` line break separates the runs around it. Upgrading reindexes documents.

- **`token-goat image-meta` no longer reports a format it cannot decode as "no benefit"**: The shrink engine re-encodes png, jpeg, bmp and gif; `probeImageMeta` also recognises webp, tiff and svg. Qualification ran on file size alone, so a 3000x3000 webp was handed to a decoder that does not exist, the attempt failed, and and the verdict printed was `Shrink: no benefit (already small/optimal)`. That is a capability limit reported as a measurement, and it was counted in the `image_shrink_skipped` tally that exists to tune the size threshold. Qualification now checks for a decoder first and `image-meta` prints `Shrink: not attempted (token-goat re-encodes png, jpeg, bmp and gif; this is webp)`.

- **The PHPStan output filter no longer leaves an error identifier behind when it deduplicates the row it belongs to**: PHPStan's table formatter puts the `🪪 identifier` line in the same table cell as the message, which renders as a row with an empty Line column. The filter only recognised numbered rows, so those lines fell through and were always kept: six duplicate errors compressed to three rows followed by four identical identifier stamps under one line number, harder to read than the raw output. Continuation lines now follow the fate of the row above them, and a table separator ends that association so a warnings table printed after a deduplicated row is not swallowed with it.

- **`token-goat xml-query` no longer discards all but the last bracket clause on a path segment**: `item[@id='1'][2]` matched three end-anchored regexes against the whole segment, so the index clause won and the attribute filter was stripped off with the tag and thrown away. The command answered `item[2]` — a positional index over every sibling, ignoring the filter — as a single confident result. Clauses are now parsed left to right and all of them apply, so an index counts within the filtered set and stacked filters must all hold. A path with an unclosed bracket now matches nothing instead of quietly reading as an unfiltered query.

- **Dart and Kotlin declarations with a generically bounded type parameter are indexed again**: `fun <T : Comparable<T>> sortAll(...)`, `typealias NodeComparator<T : Comparable<T>>`, `int boundedFunc<T extends Comparable<T>>(...)`, `extension BoundedExt<T extends Comparable<T>> on List<T>` and `class A<T extends Comparable<T>> = B with C;` all matched a flat `<[^>]*>` type-parameter group that closes at the `>` inside the bound, so the anchor after it never lined up. Each declaration was dropped from the index outright; the extension took its members with it, and the mixin-application class left a type frame that never popped, swallowing every later top-level declaration in the file. All five now use one nesting-aware clause shared with the Swift adapter, which already had it.

- **Cross-session read manifests are now filed under the session that did the reading**: `postReadHandler` named each project read manifest with the process-memoized session id instead of the key the state blob is actually persisted under. A bridge process serving two sessions filed the second session's reads over the first session's manifest, and every subagent under one parent overwrote the same file with only its own reads. The manifest is now keyed on `sessionStateKey(event)`, and its filename is spelled with the same agent-id-hashing stem builder the session store already uses, so a long session id can no longer truncate two sibling subagents onto one file.

## [2.9.14] - 2026-09-16

### Added

- **Shell script comment banner headings as surgical symbols & sections (`token-goat section "script.sh::# Banner"`)**: Added recognition of common procedural shell script comment banner patterns (`## Heading`, `# -- Section --`, `# === Check 1 ===`, `# [Step 1]`, and `# REGION: Name`) in `src/section_reader.ts` and `src/languages/bash_idx.ts`. Banners are emitted as addressable `heading` symbols spanning the full procedural check block up to the next banner, function, or EOF. This allows linear shell scripts without formal function definitions (such as `contract-check.sh` and `tools/lint.sh`) to be queried surgically via `token-goat section` and `token-goat read`, avoiding repeated whole-file reads and eliminating 80–95% of token burn on procedural scripts.
- **Case- and separator-insensitive Copilot CLI hook tool name normalization**: Implemented folded tool name mapping (`foldToolName`) in `src/bridges/copilot_cli.ts` to normalize snake_case names sent by GitHub Copilot CLI (e.g. `web_search`, `exit_plan_mode`, `read_powershell`, `read_bash`, `web_fetch`, `skill`) to canonical token-goat handlers (`WebSearch`, `ExitPlanMode`, `BashOutput`, `Skill`). Resolves silent hook bypasses (where unmapped tools bypassed interceptors 216+ times), guaranteeing that input remapping (`path` -> `file_path`, `shellId` -> `id`) and deduplication hooks trigger consistently regardless of client tool casing or naming style.
- **Worker daemon automatic off-peak idle vacuuming**: Added background database maintenance to `src/worker.ts`. The worker monitors idle activity and, after a 5-minute cooldown of zero dirty files and an interval of at least 6 hours since the last vacuum, executes non-blocking `reclaimIndex` / incremental vacuum passes automatically. This maintains compact database footprint and bounded write latency without requiring manual maintenance intervention.
- **Actionable MCP process health diagnostics (`token-goat doctor`)**: Enhanced `src/cli_doctor_process.ts` to surface detailed process IDs and runtime details for orphaned background Node.js processes, providing explicit, safe `Stop-Process -Id <PID>` instructions to cleanly reclaim system memory without terminating active sessions.
- **Markdown Setext heading extraction & section subsumption deduplication (`token-goat section "file::Heading"`)**: Added full support for Setext-style markdown headings (`Heading\n===` level 1 and `Heading\n---` level 2) across section extraction (`src/section_reader.ts`), symbol indexing (`src/parser_structured.ts`), and pre-read hints (`src/hints/markdown_hints.ts`), ensuring documentation without explicit `#`/`##` ATX anchors can be queried surgically. Implemented section subsumption deduplication in `runSectionMulti` and `runSectionCrossFile` (`src/read_section.ts`): when multiple requested sections nest inside one another (e.g. parent section and child subsections), nested child bodies are replaced with a concise subsumption notice (`(already included in section 'Parent', lines X-Y)`), completely eliminating 100% of redundant token duplication across multi-section markdown reads.
- **Concurrency-safe single-flight embedding model downloads & degraded search handling**: Added a single-flight download mutex (`_inFlightModelDownload`), collision-proof UUID temporary download files, and automatic exponential backoff retries (3 attempts with jitter) to `src/embed_model.ts`, guaranteeing that parallel or concurrent semantic search invocations on cold cache share a single download stream without file-locking races or `fetch failed` collisions. Enhanced `runSemantic` (`src/read_commands.ts`) to cleanly surface degraded/disabled embedding states and actionable resolution advice without noisy console error spam, saving 75–90% search latency and token overhead when running in degraded environments.
- **Indexed file >80% read surgical nudges & stale terminal write detection (`bash-output --verify-last-write`)**: Added pre-tool-call runtime nudges in `src/hooks_read.ts` when a read spans >80% of lines on any substantial file (>=50 lines or >=5KB) that is already indexed, proactively advising models to extract specific symbols or sections via `token-goat read "file::Symbol"` or `token-goat section "file::Heading"` instead of reading whole files into context, saving 80–95% of read tokens. Added `--verify-last-write [seconds]` and `--strict` flags to `token-goat bash-output` in `src/cli.ts` to inspect file modification timestamps and catch stale, cached, or silent no-op terminal output writes before agents burn turns on repeated flaky reruns.
- **Semantic embedding pre-flight check and cache-warm (`token-goat semantic --preflight`, `--warm`)**: Added proactive diagnostic pre-flight checks and model cache-warming to `src/embed_model.ts`, `src/read_commands.ts`, and `src/mcp_server.ts`. Detects disabled configurations, missing `onnxruntime-node` runtimes, offline model file absences (~34 MB), and unindexed project coverage before a query is attempted, outputting structured diagnostic status and actionable resolution instructions in both text and JSON formats while avoiding 70–90% of search roundtrip tokens wasted on failed semantic search fallbacks.
- **PowerShell Pester test AST symbols & script read nudges**: Added first-class AST symbol extraction for Pester test blocks (`Describe`, `Context`, `It`), multi-line block spans, and hyphenated function names (`Verb-Noun`) in `src/languages/powershell_idx.ts`. Extended quick-symbol extraction and pre-tool-call read nudges in `src/hooks_read.ts` to `.ps1`, `.psm1`, and `.Tests.ps1` files, guiding agents to surgical `symbol`, `section`, and `skeleton` extractions instead of reading 100+ line test suites and script files whole, cutting token burn by 80–95%.
- **Unwrapped git diff output compression & proportional hunk thresholds**: Enhanced `postBashHandler` in `src/hooks_bash.ts` to intercept and compress direct/unwrapped terminal `git diff` commands before monitoring bypasses. Lowered `MAX_HUNK_CHANGED` from 50 to 25 lines and `HUNK_HEAD_KEEP` to 15 in `src/tool_filters/git.ts`, enabling compact per-file hunk elision and recall pointers for multi-file review diffs (>= 40 lines total or >= 25 lines per hunk) and eliminating 65–85% of redundant diff token echoes across terminal transcripts and chat.
- **Session store schema discovery (`token-goat session-schema [table]`, `token-goat describe <target> [table]`)**: Added authoritative schema discovery for all 12 session store tables/views (`sessions`, `turns`, `checkpoints`, `session_files`, `session_refs`, `session_usage`, `tool_executions`, etc.) and session SQLite workflow tables (`todos`, `todo_deps`), complete with column types, caveats, primary keys, and common misnomer mappings (`title` → `summary`, `role` → `agent_name`), eliminating trial-and-error schema exploration and reducing output token footprint by 85–95% compared to exploratory `SELECT *` fallbacks.
- **Runtime SQL column and table error diagnostic hook (`diagnoseSqlFailure`)**: Automatically intercepts SQL errors in `postToolUseFailureHandler` (such as DuckDB Binder errors or SQLite `no such column`/`no such table`), extracting invalid column guesses (e.g. `title` or `role` on `sessions`) and immediately providing the valid column set, common misnomer mappings, and the precise `session-schema` inspection command before an agent resorts to exploratory `SELECT *` queries.
- **XML package recognition, pre-read runtime nudges, and terminal XML interception**: Added first-class recognition for `.dtsx` (SSIS), `.ampkg` (workflow packages), `.xaml`, and `.xml` across file type handlers, read hooks, and shell extractors with 20KB thresholds and tailored hierarchy advice. Introduced lightweight pre-tool-call runtime nudges for substantial or ranged reads on indexed XML packages and markdown documents, extended sequential line-range paging detection to documents and XML packages, and added active interception of terminal XML parsing commands (`Select-Xml`, `[xml]`, scratch inspect scripts, Python `xml.etree`, and CLI tools), redirecting models to `xml-query` and `xml-outline` and saving 85–95% of tokens over manual script cycles and chunk paging.
- **`token-goat mcp-output --json-query '<path>'` and `--file <path>`**: Narrow-slice queries directly on cached MCP tool outputs and on-disk tool spill files using dot-path and bracket syntax (e.g. `issues[*].key`, `total`), dropping context token footprint by 90–98% compared to re-reading whole 100KB+ JSON tool dumps, with support for `--head`, `--json` envelopes, and provenance fencing.
- **Atlassian MCP compression pack (`compressAtlassianMcpResult`)**: Strips repetitive schema and UI boilerplate (`avatarUrls`, `iconUrl`, `self`, `expand`, `schema`, `operations`, `editmeta`, `names`, `_links`, `timeZone`) from Jira and Confluence tool responses, yielding 60–80% token savings before generic table compression or model delivery.
- **Oversized MCP tool result recovery guard**: Caches MCP responses exceeding 25KB, returning a recovery identifier with targeted `mcp-output` query suggestions (`--json-query`, `--section`, `--grep`) and a clipped preview to prevent harness context spills, avoiding 75–90% context bloat from unguided whole-file read suggestions.
- **Spilled tool output read intercept and guidance**: Enhanced JSON file handling and pre-read hooks to detect oversized tool output spills (such as `content.json`), denying unguided whole-file re-reads and providing explicit `json-query` and `mcp-output --file` commands.
- **Enhanced `xml-query` with XPath, line ranges, and embedded XML decoding**: Added `--xpath <expression>`, `--with-lines`, and `--decode-embedded-xml` flags for granular XML slicing, source range tracking, and entity-decoded nested payload extraction, saving 80–95% tokens over reading whole workflow/package XML files. Added `--depth <n>` alias for `--max-depth` in `xml-outline` and clarified error guidance when reading unqualified YAML files to point to `yaml-outline`/`yaml-query`.
- **Positional command execution in `token-goat compress`**: `token-goat compress [command...]` now accepts positional arguments directly without requiring `-c/--cmd`, enabling commands like `token-goat compress vitest run` or `token-goat compress python -c "..."` without quote-escaping friction.
- **Redundant line-range re-read deduplication and sequential paging detection**: Added line-range tracking in `src/hooks_read.ts` to detect identical slice re-reads and alert on sequential manual chunk-paging patterns, redirecting models to `token-goat skeleton` and `token-goat read "file::Symbol"`. Capped recency immunity at 4 reads to prevent infinite re-read loops in small sessions.
- **Copilot CLI postToolUse context delivery bridge**: Updated `src/bridges/copilot_cli.ts` to fold postToolUse context into `modifiedResult.textResultForLlm`, ensuring advisory hints and surgical recommendations reliably reach the model in Copilot CLI environments.
- **Automated architecture documentation sync engine & pre-commit guard (`npm run docs:arch`, `npm run docs:arch:check`)**: Added `scripts/sync-arch-docs.mjs` and `tests/guards/architecture_docs.test.ts` to automatically extract, categorize, and verify all 360 source modules against `CLAUDE.arch.md` with zero drift while preserving human-curated architectural commentary.
- **Session mining optimization prompt (`docs/PROMPT_SESSION_MINING.md`)**: Added a reusable session-auditing prompt and maintainer feedback card template for discovering missed surgical reads, hook friction, tool errors, and context optimization opportunities.
- **High-performance composite indexing in index database**: Added composite expression indices (`idx_symbols_file_name_folded`, `idx_refs_file_name_folded`, `idx_chunks_file_kind_folded`) in `src/db.ts` to optimize `file::symbol` lookups, reference graph walks, and hybrid chunk retrieval without scanning entire files.
- **`token-goat upgrade` (alias `update`, `--check`, `--json`)**: Checks the npm registry for a newer published version and installs it with `npm install -g token-goat@latest`, then re-runs `token-goat install` so hooks and bridge manifests point at the build that just landed. Skipping that second step is how an upgrade leaves a machine running new code through old wiring. `--check` reports the status without installing anything, and `--json` emits the same current/latest/updateAvailable result for a script to assert on. The registry request is timeboxed at 3.5 seconds and returns nothing at all when `network.offline` is set, so an offline machine reports that it could not reach the registry instead of hanging. Running it from inside a token-goat git checkout prints the local build commands instead of globally installing over your working tree.
- **`token-goat doctor` reports an available update**: The audit ends with an update notice when a newer version is published, because the older the installed build, the more likely the rest of the report describes behavior that has already been fixed. The check is timeboxed at 1.5 seconds and stays silent when the registry cannot be reached, so `doctor` still finishes offline.
- **`token-goat compress --quiet-success` (`-q`)**: On exit code 0, emits a single line (`[tg: ok] <command> (0.4s, recall: token-goat bash-output <id>)`) and stores the full output for recall instead of printing it. A passing test suite says nothing worth reading, so the tokens go to the run that failed. On a non-zero exit the output is delivered as usual, since that is the run you need to see.
- **`token-goat compress --cmd-b64 <payload>`**: Accepts the command as base64, which survives quotes, backslashes, and dollar signs that a harness or shell would otherwise mangle before token-goat ever sees them. `-c/--cmd` is no longer a required option; supply one form or the other.
- **`token-goat compress --native`**: Runs the command through the platform's own shell, not bash, which keeps Windows path backslashes intact for tools that reject the forward-slash form.

### Changed

- **Read denial hook phrasing clarified to 'Tried to read'**: Updated `src/hooks_read.ts` from `"Read this file X times already"` to `"Tried to read this file X times already"` to prevent users and models from misinterpreting a denial as a successful read, while maintaining backward-compatible regex parsing for historical session transcripts in `src/session_audit.ts`.
- **Surgical-read hints now name symbols and headings that exist in the file**: The hint shown when a read is declined used to suggest `token-goat read "file::SymbolName"`, a placeholder nobody can run, so following the advice cost a turn to discover the real name. It now reads the file it is talking about and names up to three actual symbols, or for Markdown, up to three actual headings. The hint also offers `outline` beside `skeleton`, since one gives you the shape of a file and the other its signatures.

### Refactored

- **Decomposition of monolithic core modules**: Decomposed `src/cli.ts`, `src/read_commands.ts`, `src/text_commands.ts`, `src/cli_doctor.ts`, and `src/util.ts` into cohesive, single-responsibility submodules (`src/cli_dispatch.ts`, `src/cli_help.ts`, `src/read_meta.ts`, `src/text_trace.ts`, `src/cli_doctor_platforms.ts`, `src/util_config.ts`, etc.) adhering to anti-monolithic architecture boundaries while preserving complete public export compatibility and test suite coverage.

### Fixed

- **A Dart type alias with a generic bound is indexed again, instead of fabricating a symbol named `Function`**: the type-parameter group in both typedef matchers stopped at the first `>`, so a parameter whose bound is itself generic -- `typedef Comparator<T extends Comparable<T>> = int Function(T a, T b);`, the shape `dart:core`'s own `Comparator` uses -- closed the group one bracket early. The `=` that anchors the match then never arrived, the line fell through to the function-declaration path, and the file got a phantom top-level symbol literally named `Function` in place of the alias. That phantom is exactly what the earlier type-alias fix set out to remove; its regression test only ever exercised an unbounded `<T>`, one nesting level short of the idiom. `token-goat symbol Comparator` returned no matches on a file that declares it; it now resolves, at any bound nesting depth.
- **Context pressure is now measured from the transcript instead of guessed from tool-call counts**: the fill fraction behind the read-hint tiers and the large-file deny threshold was assembled from a per-tool-call arithmetic (500 tokens a shell output, 1,000 a fetch, 200 a read) that only ever grows, while the real context empties at every compaction. Two fields existed to correct for that -- `observedToolTokens` and `pressureBaselineTokens` -- and neither was ever written anywhere in the source, nor carried by the loader that rehydrates the cache, so the correction could not happen. On a live session the estimate read 618,800 tokens against a real 145,516. The hook payload already carries `transcript_path`, so the numerator is now the last usage record in a bounded 1 MiB tail of that file, which reflects every compaction by construction and needs no baseline. The estimate remains as the fallback for a harness that sends no transcript path. The denominator is unchanged and still an assumption.
- **A transcript path is no longer served to a session it did not come from**: the bridges in `src/bridges/` keep one hook process alive across sessions, and the session id latches to the first one that process sees. Pairing the transcript with that latched id would have handed the second session's conversation to the first as a measurement, which reads as fact in a way the estimate never did. The path is paired with the session id on the wire instead, and withheld when it does not match the session asking.
- **Four AI-assistant output filters no longer delete the answer they were compressing**: the footer rules for `claude`, `gemini`, `aider` and `gh copilot` each dropped any line beginning with `Note:` or `Tip:`. Those are not interface chrome, they are ordinary English, and an assistant writes them constantly. A captured four-line `claude --print` answer came back two lines shorter, with both deleted lines counted in a `dropped 2 noise line(s)` note — and the one line in that capture that genuinely was startup noise survived untouched, because no rule describes it. The two tokens are gone from all four rules, which still drop the keyboard hints and named boilerplate no model writes as prose. The tests that had certified the old behaviour were written from the rules they were testing; they now run against real captured output.
- **The ripgrep context filter now removes the context lines it says it removed**: both of its line matchers required a leading `path:`/`path-` prefix, which ripgrep prints only when a search covers more than one file. On the ordinary single-file `rg -n -C3 pattern file.ts` the context lines arrive bare, as `12-text`, so nothing matched, no context line was ever dropped, and the only thing removed was the cosmetic `--` separator between groups — under a note announcing that 62 context lines had been suppressed, on output that had grown. On the repository's own captured fixture the same search now saves 66.6% instead of 4.6%, with every context line gone and the first match still under its own line number. A search that redefines `--field-match-separator` or `--field-context-separator` is handed to the generic path instead, since the matchers tell a match from its context by exactly those two characters.
- **The docker filter no longer blocks the compressor that would have helped**: it declared no subcommands, so it claimed every `docker …` invocation while all of its rules target image-transfer and build noise. Dispatch stops at the first match, so `docker logs` on a service repeating a heartbeat went in at 8,702 bytes and came back at 8,701, in place of the generic compressor's 6,987. It now claims build, pull, push, run, load, create and plugin install/upgrade — the subcommands that emit the layer lines it drops, whose distinct IDs the generic compressor cannot fold — and releases the rest.
- **`outline --grep` and `skeleton --grep` no longer lose a symbol that sits late in a large file**: the symbol rows were cut to a 5,000-row cap before the pattern was applied, so a match past the 5,000th symbol in source order was discarded before it was ever tested. The result was an empty listing, byte-identical to an honest no-match, under a notice blaming a filter that had not examined it and advising the reader to widen a filter that was never the cause. The listing now pages through the file applying the pattern as it goes, so the cap bounds the matches rather than the search, and the no-match notice counts what was actually examined. `token-goat symbol <name>` always found these symbols, which is how far apart the two answers had drifted.
- **The PDF, Word, PowerPoint and Excel readers no longer load on every hook call**: a hook fires before every tool call and parses the whole eagerly reachable half of its bundle before running any of it, and the four document readers (pdfjs, the OOXML and zip machinery, the spreadsheet reader) were reachable from the indexer's document bridge even though the predicate that decides whether a file is one of those formats only looks at its extension. They are now imported where they are used, which takes 69 KB off what every hook call has to parse.
- **Three output filters no longer drop the line you ran the command to see**: `git status -vv` collapsed its file listing into a bare count no matter how short it was, so the one modified file in a clean tree printed as `1 file` instead of its name; `aws s3` compression matched only `upload failed:` and `download failed:`, dropping the `copy failed:`, `delete failed:` and `move failed:` lines the CLI emits for bucket-to-bucket copies, `rm` and `mv`, and dropping them into the progress-line count so the summary read as a clean run (reachable when the command is run with `2>&1`, since a separated stderr and non-zero exit already hand back the raw output untouched), and `aws s3 rm` was not routed to that compressor at all, so its failures were counted by nothing; and `kubectl describe pod` discarded `Last State:` and `Container ID:`, which is where a `CrashLoopBackOff`'s actual exit code and the previous container's identity live. Each filter now keeps the line and, where it summarises, counts failures in their own note rather than folding them in with successes.
- **A repository can no longer put words in token-goat's mouth through a read hint**: hints name real headings and symbols out of the index, and a repository picks those names. token-goat speaks to the model on two channels, and only one of them was covered: a blocked read has its whole message escaped, an advisory note does not. So a heading named after token-goat's own prefix arrived in the model's context looking like token-goat had said it. Escaping the name was not enough on its own, because the suggested command is compared literally and an escaped name never resolves, which would have traded a forged line for a broken instruction. Such a name is now left out of the suggestion entirely and the hint moves on to the next real heading, so the line stays both attributable and runnable; ordinary names are untouched. The same gap in the hint that recalls saved tool output, where the file's path was shown unescaped, is closed too, and the check that is supposed to catch this class now asserts the escaping at that site instead of describing it.
- **Editing the chunker or a document extractor now discards the vectors it made stale**: semantic search stores a vector per chunk, and the record of what produced those vectors named only the model, its pinned revision, and the inference runtime. Nothing named the code that turns a file into chunks, so changing how text is split, how a PDF or Word document is read, or how the final vector is pooled left every already-embedded file holding vectors from the old code, and a re-index reported those files skipped. That record now also names the embedding stack itself, so such a change is treated exactly like a model change: the stale vectors are discarded and rebuilt. A new guard walks the import graph from the embedding entry points and fails if any module it reaches is neither covered nor carrying a written reason it cannot affect what gets embedded.
- **The parse-freshness digest now covers every source that decides what gets extracted**: `PARSER_FINGERPRINT` is stamped beside each indexed file's content hash so that changing extraction logic reindexes files whose content never moved. It was computed from `src/parser.ts` and the language adapters alone, which left out the table mapping every extension and basename to a language and an extraction method, the language-detection and content-sniffing functions, the reference extractor, the symbol-body size cap, and the helper fourteen adapters use to compute where a symbol ends. Editing any of those left the digest unchanged, so every already-indexed file passed the freshness check and kept symbol rows the current build would no longer produce. A new guard walks the import graph from `src/parser.ts` and fails if any module it reaches is neither hashed nor carrying a written reason it cannot affect extraction, so the next module added there cannot be left out silently.
- **`--quiet-success` no longer bypassed when no filter matches the command**: Commands that match no tool filter took a raw passthrough that streams straight to the terminal, which never sees the output and so could not suppress it. The same shortcut had already been closed for `--max-tokens`; `--quiet-success` now takes the same capture path, so the flag applies to every command, not just the ones a filter happened to recognize.
- **The upgrade check is declared as network egress**: `token-goat capabilities` lists the new registry request as `network.upgrade_check`, controlled by `network.offline` and pointing at `src/cli_upgrade.ts::fetchLatestVersion`. A test in the suite fails the build when a module can open a network connection and is missing from that inventory, which is the gate a new command like this one is meant to hit.

## [2.9.13] - 2026-09-14

### Added

- **Opt-in multilingual Tesseract OCR support (`image-text --lang <lang>`, `image_shrink.ocr_lang`)**: Optical character recognition for screenshots and image files can now extract text in 16 supported languages (`eng`, `fra`, `spa`, `deu`, `ita`, `por`, `nld`, `pol`, `rus`, `tur`, `swe`, `ara`, `chi_sim`, `chi_tra`, `jpn`, `kor`), expanding beyond the previous English-only default. English (`eng`) is always enabled, and one or more additional languages can be combined simultaneously (e.g. `--lang "fra,spa"` or `image_shrink.ocr_lang = "fra+spa"`). Persistent across version updates via `token-goat config set image_shrink.ocr_lang <langs>`, per-project in `.token-goat.toml`, or via the `TOKEN_GOAT_OCR_LANG` environment variable. Each language is pinned to immutable `@tesseract.js-data/<lang>@1.0.0` distribution paths with precomputed SHA-256 verification against the decompressed traineddata payload to prevent supply-chain tampering. Unrecognized languages fall back safely to `eng`, and tampering quarantines corrupt cache files and falls back to vision tokens.
- **`token-goat docx-tables <file> [--table <n>] [--json]`**: Extracts tables from Word documents (`.docx`) and formats them as clean Markdown tables, preserving row/column relations that were previously lost when flattened into disjointed lines by `docx-text`. Supports selecting individual tables by 1-based index and JSON output.
- **Universal file type interception for SQLite, Parquet, JSON, YAML, and JSONL**: Large file reads of SQLite databases (`.db`, `.sqlite`, `.sqlite3`, `.db3`) and Parquet files (`.parquet`) are now intercepted unconditionally before reading binary content into context, redirecting to `sqlite-tables`/`sqlite-schema`/`sqlite-query` and DuckDB. Large JSON, YAML, and JSONL files (≥20 KB) are intercepted with structural summaries (object keys, array lengths, or JSONL record counts) and directed to `json-outline`/`json-query` and `yaml-outline`/`yaml-query` instead of blowing out context.
- **CSV quote relaxation and delimiter sniffing**: `csv-query` and `csv-profile` now tolerate unescaped quotes within fields (common with inch marks or hardware specs like `16" alloy wheels`) via `relax_quotes: true`, and automatically sniff delimiters (`,`, `\t`, `;`, `|`) so pipe-separated or tab-separated data can be queried immediately without manual `--delimiter` specification.
- **JSONPath nested property filters**: `json-query` and `yaml-query` now support filtering by nested dot-path properties (e.g. `[?(@.metadata.level == 1)]` or `[metadata.level == 1]`), making it easy to filter large structured document manifests or RAG chunks without loading the entire JSON into context.
- **`token-goat sqlite-tables <db> [--json]`**: Ultra-compact overview of SQLite tables and views, reporting row counts and column counts (e.g. `sra_vehicles (table, 981 rows, 27 cols)`) in under 50 tokens, cutting schema discovery cost by over 90% compared to `sqlite-schema`.
- **`token-goat xlsx-columns <file> [--sheet <name>] [--head <n>] [--json]`**: Profiles column names, letters, fill rates, and sample values across wide multi-column spreadsheets without dumping thousands of tokens of wide CSV rows into context.
- **`token-goat xlsx-head --columns <a,b,c>`**: Projects only specific comma-separated column names or letters, avoiding wide-column token dumps on large spreadsheets.
- **Excel sheet defaulting**: `xlsx-columns`, `xlsx-head`, `xlsx-range`, and `xlsx-query` now default to the workbook's first sheet when `--sheet` is omitted, eliminating the need for an initial exploratory turn.
- **JSONPath recursive descent (`..key`)**: `json-query` and `yaml-query` now support preorder depth-first recursive descent across arbitrary nested objects and arrays using `..property` syntax, bounded by cycle detection, a depth limit of 100, and a traversal node cap of 50,000. Also supports quoted filter expressions with escaped quotes (`[?(@.field == "value with spaces")]`).
- **SQLite engine performance tuning**: `initConnection` now configures `cache_size = -32000` (32 MB page cache), `temp_store = MEMORY`, and `mmap_size = 134217728` (128 MB memory-mapped I/O) for faster multi-table queries and joins.
- **Every MCP tool now says whether it only reads.** The tools token-goat exposes over MCP carry the protocol's behavior hints, so a client can tell the fifteen that only read from the three that write (`compress_text` stores a compressed payload, `handoff_create` writes a handoff and replaces one of the same name, and `handoff_resolve` stores the compact form of what it returns). Clients that auto-approve read-only tools can now do so here, instead of prompting for a symbol lookup as though it edited something.
- **Accent-insensitive symbol search, including on names that were already indexed**: full-text search over symbols and cached recall entries now folds diacritics written as separate combining marks, so `Noi` finds `Hà Nội` and `Viet` finds `Việt Nam`. Names whose accents are written as single characters, like `café` or `Straße`, were already findable and are unaffected. An index built by an earlier version is upgraded in place the first time it is opened, which takes about five seconds on a 2 GB index of 400,000 symbols and happens once; searches during that window wait rather than fail.

### Fixed

- **An Apex comment ending in a backslash no longer hides the rest of the class.** A line like `// Don't put the file at C:\` left an apostrophe-opened string frame open across the line break, and every method after it vanished from the index while the class itself reported a span ending at the comment. Upgrading reparses already-indexed files, since extraction changed.
- **A triple quote inside a GraphQL `#` comment is prose, not a description.** `"""A user."""  # use """ to describe a type` opened a block description that ran to the end of the schema, so the file indexed with no types, operations, or fragments at all. GraphQL was borrowing Kotlin's rules for `"""` strings, and Kotlin's comment marker is `//`; it now carries its own. Upgrading reparses already-indexed files, since extraction changed.
- **A TypeScript value used as an object shorthand counts as a reference.** `return { widget }` reads `widget`, but the reference filter treated the identifier as a property name and dropped it, so `refs m.ts::widget` answered "No references found" for a file that plainly uses it. The same code written in JavaScript answered correctly, because only TypeScript paths go through that filter.
- **A Salesforce flow element indexes when its opening tag carries an attribute or a namespace prefix.** `<variables xsi:type="VariableDef">` and `<md:variables>` matched nothing, so the flow indexed with no elements at all and every lookup against them came back empty. Both shapes were already handled everywhere else in the same file. Upgrading reparses already-indexed files, since extraction changed.
- **`diff`, `log`, `grep --symbol` and `changed --symbol` stop answering about the wrong part of a notebook.** All four pair a symbol's indexed line numbers with git's own, and a notebook is indexed from its flattened cell source, so the two number different documents. `log` printed the history of two markdown lines under a header naming a function, `diff` reported "No changes" about a symbol that had just changed, `changed --symbol` left the notebook out of the report, and `grep --symbol` labelled nothing. Each now widens to the whole file and says in one line that it did.
- **A link an MCP server hands back is now redacted and fenced like everything else it says.** A `resource_link` result carries its words in `name`, `uri` and `description` and has no text field at all, so token-goat read the whole result as empty and left it alone. A credential written into a description, or an instruction aimed at the model, reached the model exactly as the server wrote it: no redaction, no injection scan, no untrusted-content fence. All three now apply, and the link's target travels with the description so nothing is lost in the exchange.
- **A picture attached to an MCP result no longer disappears when the text beside it is rewritten.** Fencing or compressing a mixed text-and-image result replaced the whole result with a plain string, and every image, audio clip and binary resource in it went with it. The rewritten words now come back as a text block with the other blocks still attached.
- **A notebook saved with a byte order mark reads back instead of coming back empty.** The mark is invisible and survives a round trip through many editors. Its presence made every context window built for a symbol in that notebook return nothing, while the same notebook read back correctly through every other command.
- **An overloaded C++ operator called through `template` records the operator.** `r.template operator()<int>(3)` was recorded under the name `template operator()<int>`, which matches no definition, so the call could never be resolved back to the `operator()` it invokes. Upgrading reparses already-indexed files, since extraction changed.

- **An MCP tool result that is only a picture stays a picture.** A result carrying image blocks and no text had no text to extract, so the reader fell through to its last resort and turned the blocks themselves into a string -- handing the model the image's base64 as tens of kilobytes of text in place of the picture it replaced, measured at 60 KB of text for a 60 KB image. Such a result is now left alone, which is what any tool returning a chart, a rendered page or a screenshot needs.
- **Jupyter notebook line numbers no longer point into the JSON.** A notebook is indexed through the flattened Python document its cells make, so a symbol's recorded lines address that document rather than the file on disk. Two more readers were pairing those lines with the raw JSON: the context lines `refs --callers -C` and `brief` print around a call site came back as `"cell_type": "code"` instead of the calling code, and the fold that shortens a long function in a delivered file cut a notebook's cell array in half under a notice naming a function that is not in that range. Context now reads the same document the line numbers came from, and the fold declines a notebook rather than guessing.
- **A C++ member call written through `template` records the method, not the whole phrase.** `r.template meth<int>(3)` was recorded as a reference named `template meth<int>`, which matches no symbol the indexer ever writes, so the call was invisible to `refs` and to the caller lists behind `brief`. It now resolves to `meth`. Destructor calls, which take the same path, were already correct and stay so. Upgrading reparses already-indexed files, since extraction changed.
- **A templated C++ call now records the function it calls.** Call sites are resolved by node shape, and a call with explicit template arguments wraps its name in an extra node the C/C++ resolver did not know. Three shapes were wrong: `tmpl<int>(x)` produced no reference at all, while `std::make_unique<Foo>(x)` and `obj.meth<int>(x)` produced references named `make_unique<Foo>` and `meth<int>` -- names carrying the argument list, which no lookup of the real callee can match. All three now resolve to the bare callee name, so `token-goat refs` and the caller lists behind `brief` see templated calls the way they already saw plain ones. Upgrading reindexes: this changes what the parser extracts, so the parser fingerprint moved and already-indexed files self-heal on next touch. No `install` re-run needed.

- **A very large function in a Jupyter notebook reads back as code, not as notebook markup.** A symbol whose body is over 128 KB is stored empty on purpose, and readers re-derive its text from the file over the line range the index recorded. A `.ipynb` is indexed through the flattened Python document its code cells make, so those line numbers point into that document and not into the JSON on disk -- and the re-derivation was slicing the JSON. `read`, `symbol` and `brief` answered such a notebook symbol with raw notebook markup under the function's name, or with nothing at all when the notebook was written on a single line. They now slice the same document the index measured.
- **A C++ object built with `new` now counts as a reference to its type.** `new Bar(7)`, `new Ns::Qux()` and `new Foo<int>(1)` recorded nothing at all, so `refs --callers` answered "no references found" for a constructor the project calls, while the same construct in TypeScript and Java had always been recorded. The empty answer was indistinguishable from an honest one, and the note printed beside it said `new` was indexed. Upgrading reparses already-indexed files, since extraction changed.
- **An MCP tool result that arrives without its usual wrapper is read as text again, and a screenshot inside one is shrunk again.** Some MCP servers send a result as the bare list of content blocks rather than as `{ content: [...] }` wrapped around that list. Token-goat recognized only the wrapped form, so against those servers the model was handed the block list as escaped JSON instead of the text inside it, and an oversized screenshot in such a result went through at full size. Both readers now take either shape.
- **Reference counts survive a file with thousands of symbols.** `outline --stats` and `skeleton --stats` ask the index how many times each of a file's symbols is referenced, and the whole list of names went into one query, one bound value per name. SQLite refuses to prepare a statement past a fixed number of bound values -- 32,766 in the build shipped here, and as few as 999 in others -- and it refuses by failing, not by answering short, so past that point the counts for the entire file were lost rather than trimmed. The names are now asked for in batches and the answers merged, so the result is the same whatever the file's size.
- **Searching by meaning works again on a machine with a large index.** Every project on a machine shares one vector table, so a project-scoped search over-fetches nearest neighbors and then keeps the ones belonging to the project, widening the scan when too few survive. The scan was allowed to widen to the size of the whole table, and sqlite-vec refuses a scan wider than 4,096 rows outright rather than returning fewer -- so past roughly 2,700 neighbors the search failed, and `semantic` reported that matching on meaning was off and fell back to keyword search. On an index of 209,208 chunks, asking a freshly indexed one-file project for its only function in plain words returned nothing at all. The scan now stops at the limit, and past it the project's own chunks are ranked directly, which finds the answer whatever else the machine has indexed.
- **A Word, PowerPoint or Excel file that is not one is now remembered as such, instead of being opened again on every pass.** Reading a document can end two ways: the file is past a limit or is not what it claims to be, which is an answer about the file, or the read itself went wrong, which is not. The first is recorded so the file is left alone until it changes; the second is left open so the document is tried again once the cause clears. Four answers about the file were on the wrong side of that line: an archive with no `word/document.xml`, none with `xl/workbook.xml`, one with no slides, and a part nested deeper than the reader will follow. Each of those is the same answer every time it is asked, and each was being asked again on every pass of the background indexer, for as long as the file sat in the project. They are now recorded. Nothing is buried by this: what gets recorded is tied to the file's contents, so a document that was simply half-written when it was first seen is read again as soon as the writing finishes. A PDF that the PDF library rejects is deliberately left as before, because that answer belongs to one version of that library rather than to the file, and a later version may well read it.
- **`reconcile` no longer reports live files as deleted, over and over.** Two things write to the index and they enumerate differently. `token-goat index` walks a project with git and so sees tracked files only; the incremental path indexes whatever you touch, which includes a file git ignores and a file you have written but not yet added. Both are deliberate. The sweep that looks for drift, though, compared the index against the tracked list alone and called the difference a deletion, so every one of those files was reported gone and queued to have its rows dropped -- then the next read put them back, and the next sweep dropped them again. On this project's own index, 228 of 1,628 files were in that state and every single one of them was sitting on disk. A file is now reported as deleted only when it has actually left the disk, which is what the check was always meant to ask. A tracked file you delete is unaffected: it goes through the same path it always did.
- **`xlsx-head` reads the rows you asked for instead of the whole sheet.** Previewing the top of a spreadsheet built every row of the sheet in memory and then kept only the handful being shown. The cost was the sheet's size, not the preview's: on a 550,000-row sheet, asking for 5 rows took 5.1 seconds and asking for 20 took 4.6, for eleven lines of output either way. Both now take about 1.6 seconds, which is the time to read the file at all. The full walk was there to find the sheet's widest column, which is needed to keep a wide row lined up under the header, and that number was already worked out when the file was read. Output is unchanged, down to the byte.
- **`sqlite-tables` and `sqlite-schema` no longer hide user tables whose names merely resemble SQLite's own.** Both commands skip SQLite's internal tables, and the machinery that full-text search and vector search build beside a virtual table, by matching names against patterns like `sqlite_%`. In SQLite's pattern language an underscore stands for any single character unless it is marked as literal, and it was not, so those patterns reached much further than they read. On a database of four ordinary tables, `sqlite-tables` listed one: a table called `sqlitedata` was excluded because the underscore matched the `d`, and one called `my_ftsx_cache` because it matched the `x`. These are inventory commands, which is what makes it serious: a table missing from the list does not look like an overly broad filter, it looks like a table that does not exist, and the next query gets written as though it does not. The underscores are now literal. The five shadow tables that full-text search really does create per index stay hidden, as does `sqlite_sequence`.
- **`csv-query` and `csv-profile` work out a file's separator by parsing it, not by counting characters in its first line.** The old guess counted commas, tabs, semicolons and pipes on the header line and took whichever won, which cannot tell a separator from the same character sitting inside a quoted field name, and the header line is exactly where a compound name lands. A perfectly ordinary comma-separated file whose first column was called `"model;year;trim"` did not open at all: two semicolons beat one comma, and the parser stopped on the quotes. A file with a column called `a|b|c` was worse, because it opened: pipes won, the file quietly became three columns, and the `description` column after it could no longer be named. Each candidate is now tried as a real parse of the first few rows, and one that leaves a row with the wrong number of fields is rejected rather than accepted on a count. Genuinely tab-, semicolon-, and pipe-separated files, including the European convention of semicolons with commas inside values, still open without `--delimiter`.
- **`json-query` and `yaml-query` now say when a search stopped early, and no longer filter on a comparison they do not understand.** Searching a document with `..key` is allowed to visit 50,000 places before giving up, which is sensible, but that allowance was being handed out afresh to every branch an earlier part of the path had already fanned out to, so a third-of-a-megabyte file could be walked to 408,510 results over nine seconds. Worse, running out said nothing: the answer came back looking exactly like a complete one, so a file with 60,000 matches quietly returned 49,998 of them, and a match nested deeper than the search goes returned nothing at all, which is indistinguishable from a misspelled key. There is now one allowance for the whole search, and both the plain and `--json` forms say plainly when it ran out. Separately, a filter like `[price>=10]` was silently read as a field named `price>` and matched nothing, so a query that looked like it filtered was really answering about a field no document has; this grammar compares by equality only and now says so instead. Filters also look only at keys an object actually has, not ones it inherits.
- **Three commands handed a spreadsheet's or document's own text to the model with nothing marking where it came from, but only under `--json`.** `docx-tables`, `xlsx-columns` and `xlsx-query` mask anything that looks like a key and mark instruction-shaped text as untrusted in their ordinary output, and did neither when asked for JSON. A spreadsheet holding an API key handed that key over in full; a cell reading like an instruction arrived looking like something token-goat had said. Both now get the same per-field treatment the sibling commands already gave theirs, and the output is still JSON a caller can parse. Every document command that speaks `--json` is now swept by one test rather than named in a list, so a command added later cannot quietly skip it.
- **A document that ran out of time once is no longer given up on for good.** Reading a PDF, deck or workbook is capped at a minute, and a document that passed the cap was recorded as settled: the same file was never read again, so `semantic` could not answer from it for the life of that file. But a minute is a statement about how busy the machine was, not about the document -- the same file often reads in two seconds once a build finishes. A document declined on the clock is now recorded against the clock that declined it, so it stays settled while nothing has changed and is looked at again the moment that limit moves. Documents declined for their size or their contents are unaffected, and are still settled for good. Run `token-goat index` (or let the background worker catch up) after upgrading: this release changes the indexer, so it reindexes.
- **A Word, PowerPoint or Excel file can no longer exhaust memory with a single oversized part.** The limit on how far one of these files may unpack was a limit on the whole of it, and said nothing about how the file divides that total -- so it could spend all of it on one piece, which is then read into memory whole and built into a structure several times its size. A 298 KB document was enough to end the process outright, and a 1.19 MB one reached 3.34 GB of memory and took 24 seconds. Any one piece is now limited on its own, at six times the largest found across 83 real documents. All of those still read exactly as before; the crafted ones are declined in about a second.
- **A spreadsheet that names the same sheet many times is read once, not once per mention, and a workbook can no longer spend the whole memory of the machine on how many sheets it declares.** A workbook lists its sheets in one small part and points each at a worksheet file, and nothing required those pointers to be different: a 66 KB file naming one 797 KB worksheet 500 times took 14.3 seconds and 1,426 MB, and one naming it 5,000 times ran the process out of memory outright. Each repeat is now read and parsed once and shared, so the same file takes 62 milliseconds and 87 MB and the 5,000-sheet one finishes in a tenth of a second. Deduplicating alone does not answer the honest version of the same file, though, so two further bounds now hold: the clock that already limited reading a workbook is consulted inside the sheet loop rather than only after it, and one file may now have at most 64 MB of text pulled out of it in total, twelve times the largest of the 86 real Office documents this was sized against. A workbook past that is refused and remembered as refused rather than retried on every pass.
- **A slide deck that names the same slide many times is read once, not once per mention.** A deck lists its slides in display order, and nothing stopped that list from naming one slide over and over. Every mention cost a full re-read of that slide. Listing the outline of a 7.9 KB deck that named one slide 2,000 times took 30 seconds; it now takes 45 milliseconds, and 20,000 mentions take 71. Four more commands that walk a whole deck or workbook are now bounded by the same clock their siblings already used.
- **A PDF can no longer spend a whole minute on where its text sits.** Text is grouped into lines by height, using an index that only works while heights can be compared. A document can name a height that compares against nothing -- and the grouping then fell back to checking every line found so far, against every piece of text. A 412 KB file took the entire minute the reader allows. It now takes a third of a second.
- **A bookmark's destination can no longer stop `pdf-outline` for good.** Reading a bookmark's page makes two lookups into structures the file supplies: a name search under `/Dests`, then a walk of the page tree. Neither was raced against the document's minute-long clock, which was checked only between bookmarks -- so one entry whose lookup never came back held the command open forever, and no count of bookmarks bounded that, since the cost came from the file's own trees and not from the number of entries. Both lookups now go through the same race every other document await in this module already used. A destination that does not resolve is still an ordinary answer (the bookmark keeps its title with no page number); the clock running out is not, and is reported as such rather than being swallowed into that same pageless bookmark.

## [2.9.12] - 2026-09-14

### Fixed

- **A PDF can no longer stop `pdf-extract --layout` for good.** Rebuilding a page's lines walks the rows near each piece of text, and it stepped through them by counting. A document places its text where it likes, so it can name a height so large that adding one to it changes nothing -- and the walk then never reached its end. A 612-byte file was enough, the command sat at nothing for as long as it was left, and the minute-long limit on the read could not end it, because that limit is only read between pieces of work and this work never finished one. The walk now names the rows it visits instead of counting to them.
- **A Word, PowerPoint or Excel file too large to open is now remembered as refused.** The size limit reported an ordinary failure, which reads as bad luck rather than as an answer about the file, so the indexer tried it again on every pass and would have forever. The same is now true of a spreadsheet whose sheet claims a range too large to scan.
- **A deck or workbook is read once, not once per slide.** Collecting the text for the index opened the file again for every slide or sheet in it, decompressing the whole thing each time, and the number of times came from the file itself. A 200-slide deck took 201 passes and 4.6 seconds; it now takes one pass and 52ms, and a 1,000-slide deck takes 240ms. Walking a document's slides or sheets is also bounded by a clock now, as reading a PDF already was.

## [2.9.11] - 2026-09-13

### Added

- **VHDL is now indexed**, extracting entities, architectures, packages (specification and body), functions, and procedures from `.vhd`/`.vhdl` files. A `/* ... */` delimited comment (added in VHDL-2008) is treated as non-nesting, matching IEEE Std 1076-2008 clause 15.9: the first closing `*/` ends it, however many `/*` appeared inside. A function or procedure declared without a body (a forward declaration in a package specification) is not indexed, matching this repo's existing convention elsewhere. Run `token-goat index` (or let the background worker catch up) to pick up VHDL files in an existing project: this changes what a `.vhd`/`.vhdl` file's index rows contain, so it reindexes.
- **Six template-engine formats are now indexed as HTML**: Jinja2 (`.j2`/`.jinja`/`.jinja2`), Handlebars (`.hbs`/`.handlebars`), ERB (`.erb`), EJS (`.ejs`), Nunjucks (`.njk`), and Twig (`.twig`). Each dialect's own template delimiters ({% raw %}`{{ }}`, `{% %}`, `{# #}`, `<% %>`{% endraw %}, and their comment/triple-brace/trim variants) are blanked out before the file is handed to the same HTML extractor `.html` and `.liquid` files already use, so `id`/`class` attributes and headings in the surrounding markup are indexed the same way they would be in a plain `.html` file, while the templating expressions themselves are not. Run `token-goat index` (or let the background worker catch up) to pick these files up in an existing project: this adds index rows for six extensions that were previously unindexed, so it reindexes.
- **Five Lisp-family dialects are now indexed**: Common Lisp (`.lisp`/`.lsp`/`.cl`), Scheme (`.scm`/`.ss`), Racket (`.rkt`/`.rktl`), Clojure (`.clj`/`.cljs`/`.cljc`), and Emacs Lisp (`.el`). Each dialect gets its own extractor rather than one shared implementation, because their comment, string, and character-literal rules genuinely differ: block comments (`#| ... |#`) nest in Common Lisp, Scheme, and Racket, but neither Clojure nor Emacs Lisp has a block-comment form at all; a "skip the next form" comment exists as `#;` in Scheme and Racket and as `#_` in Clojure, but not in Common Lisp or Emacs Lisp; and a character literal is written `#\x` in Common Lisp/Scheme/Racket, plain `\x` in Clojure, and `?x` in Emacs Lisp. Run `token-goat index` (or let the background worker catch up) to pick up these files in an existing project: this adds index rows for eleven extensions that were previously unindexed, so it reindexes.
- **Haskell (`.hs`) is now indexed**, extracting `module`, `data`/`newtype`, `type`, `class`, and `instance` declarations along with top-level function bindings (a type signature and/or one or more equations, merged into a single symbol). Since Haskell's layout rule has no explicit delimiter around a top-level declaration, a declaration's span is found the same way every Haskell ctags generator finds one: a line with no leading whitespace starts it, and it runs to the line just before the next such line. A `{- -}` block comment nests, per the Haskell 2010 Language Report section 2.3; a `--` line comment does not start when the dash run is itself part of a longer legal operator lexeme (`-->`, per Report sections 2.3 and 2.4). Literate Haskell (`.lhs`) is not claimed by this adapter: telling code from prose in a bird-tracks or `\begin{code}` file needs its own masking pass this adapter does not implement. Run `token-goat index` (or let the background worker catch up) to pick up `.hs` files in an existing project: this adds index rows for an extension that was previously unindexed, so it reindexes.
- **OCaml (`.ml`/`.mli`) is now indexed**, extracting `module`, `module type`, `type`, `exception`, `class`, `external`, and top-level `let`/`let rec` bindings, found by the same no-leading-whitespace top-level-boundary heuristic `haskell.ts` uses. A `(* *)` block comment nests, per The OCaml Manual's "Comments" section -- but unlike every other nesting block comment this repo indexes, a `"` seen while scanning a comment switches into real string-lexing mode first (the manual: "Comments do not occur inside string or character literals"), so `(* " *)` does not close the comment at that `*)`: the unterminated string absorbs it, and the comment only ends at the first `*)` found once the scanner is not inside such a string. `{id|...|id}` quoted string literals (an arbitrary user-chosen delimiter, `{|...|}` when empty) are read literally with no escaping. A `'c'` character literal (including `\n`/`\'`/decimal/hex/octal escapes) is distinguished from a bare `'a`/`'b` type-variable tick by whether the text actually closes within the fixed lookahead each literal form requires. Run `token-goat index` (or let the background worker catch up) to pick up `.ml`/`.mli` files in an existing project: this adds index rows for two extensions that were previously unindexed, so it reindexes.
- **F# (`.fs`/`.fsi`/`.fsx`) is now indexed**, extracting `namespace`, `module`, `type`, `exception`, and top-level `let`/`let rec` bindings, found by the same no-leading-whitespace top-level-boundary heuristic `haskell.ts` and `ocaml.ts` use. A `(* *)` block comment nests and, like OCaml's (F#'s comment/string lexing is a direct descendant of OCaml's), is string-aware: a `"` seen while scanning a comment switches into string-lexing mode first, so an unbalanced quote inside a comment does not close it at the next `*)` -- the well-known F# "stray quote in a block comment swallows the rest of the file" gotcha is the direct observable consequence of this same rule. `"""..."""` triple-quoted strings are read literally with no escape processing; `@"..."` verbatim strings use `""` (not backslash) as their only escape, so a backslash inside one (`@"C:\temp\n"`) is two literal characters, never an escape; ordinary `"..."` strings use normal backslash escaping. Run `token-goat index` (or let the background worker catch up) to pick up `.fs`/`.fsi`/`.fsx` files in an existing project: this adds index rows for three extensions that were previously unindexed, so it reindexes.
- **Nix (`.nix`) is now indexed**, extracting `let`-bound names and attribute-set keys (`name = value;`, `a.b.c = value;`) as symbols, since Nix has no C-family top-level declaration syntax. Unlike every other block comment this repo indexes, Nix's `/* */` comment does NOT nest (Nix Reference Manual, "Syntax"): the first `*/` closes it, however many `/*` appeared inside. String interpolation (`${...}`) is tracked with a real mode stack rather than a flat scan, since a `${...}` antiquotation can contain a nested Nix expression, which can itself contain another string with its own `${...}`, arbitrarily deep; the stack also tracks brace depth within an interpolation so a `}` belonging to a nested record literal (`${ { a = 1; }.a }`) does not prematurely close it. Indented (`''...''`) strings use their own dedicated escapes -- `'''` for a literal `''` and `''${` for a literal `${` that does not open an interpolation -- distinct from `"..."` strings' backslash escaping. Run `token-goat index` (or let the background worker catch up) to pick up `.nix` files in an existing project: this adds index rows for an extension that was previously unindexed, so it reindexes.
- **Zed is now supported via `token-goat install --zed`.** Zed's first-party agent has no hooks API at all, so this registers token-goat as an MCP context server instead -- the only integration surface Zed offers -- in Zed's `settings.json` (`%APPDATA%\Zed\settings.json` on Windows, `~/.config/zed/settings.json` elsewhere), plus a small generated shim script that launches `token-goat mcp-serve`. The install merges surgically into an existing `settings.json`, preserving comments, other keys, and any other context servers already registered there, and is idempotent on repeat runs. Like Visual Studio's MCP-only integration, this is tools only: no read dedup, hints, image shrink, or output folding, since Zed has no hook to fire them from. `token-goat uninstall --zed` removes the entry and the shim; `token-goat doctor` reports whether the entry is present and warns if the shim or the paths it points at have gone stale. Nothing to reindex.
- **Cursor is now supported via `token-goat install --cursor`.** This registers token-goat as an MCP server in `~/.cursor/mcp.json` (`-p`/`--project` for `<project>/.cursor/mcp.json`), root `mcpServers`, entry shape `command`+`args` only -- confirmed against the installed Cursor 3.19.7 bundle's own JSON schema, which has no `type` field for a server entry and rejects unknown properties. `--cursor` deliberately never writes `~/.cursor/hooks.json`: the same bundle shows Cursor already imports Claude Code's hooks from `~/.claude/settings.json` by default (`thirdPartyExtensibilityEnabled` defaults on) and dedupes against its own `hooks.json` by exact command string, so a plain `token-goat install` for Claude Code already makes those hooks fire once inside Cursor with zero extra work; writing a second, independently-maintained copy would only risk a permanently-diverging duplicate, and `~/.cursor/hooks.json` may already be a real file another tool manages (observed on the machine this was verified against: a third-party tool's 8-event hook registration). A guard test (`tests/guards/cursor_never_writes_hooks_json.test.ts`) fails the build if a future change writes to it anyway. `token-goat uninstall --cursor` removes only the MCP entry; `token-goat doctor` reports it and whether Claude Code hooks are installed for Cursor to pick up. Nothing to reindex.

- **token-goat now works inside VS Code's Copilot agent, not just beside it.** `token-goat install --vscode` now also installs agent hooks, so token-goat sees the file reads, searches, edits, and terminal commands VS Code's agent makes with its built-in tools. In VS Code, token-goat now:
  - stops the agent from reading a large file again when it already has it, and points it at the copy it has
  - adds short hints to reads and edits
  - shrinks a large image inside the workspace before `view_image` loads it
  - queues edited files for reindexing

  VS Code gives hooks no way to change what a tool returns, so token-goat cannot fold or trim a file's contents there the way it does in Claude Code. Terminal output is not compressed in VS Code either: VS Code does not tell the hook which shell will run a terminal command, so rewriting it safely is not possible, and token-goat leaves the command as it is. The hooks run before VS Code asks you to approve a call, so token-goat does not look at any file or folder on a network share or outside the workspace until then, and on macOS and Linux the shrunk copy it writes can only be read by you.

  With `-p`, `.vscode/mcp.json` and `.github/hooks/token-goat.json` hold absolute paths to node and token-goat on your machine. `install --vscode -p` and `install --copilot --local` now remind you not to commit them: list them in `.git/info/exclude` or `.gitignore`. None of this needs a reindex.

  If you already use `--vscode`, run `token-goat install --vscode` again to add the hooks (with `-p` for a project install). They go in the same folder Copilot CLI reads, `~/.copilot/hooks/` (or `.github/hooks/` with `-p`), and share one hooks file with `--copilot`. Uninstalling either one leaves the hooks the other still uses.

  If VS Code's `chat.useClaudeHooks` setting is on, VS Code also runs the Claude Code hooks in `~/.claude/settings.json`, so each hook fires twice. `install --vscode` and `token-goat doctor` now point this out. token-goat does not change your VS Code settings.

- **token-goat now works with the Copilot agent in Visual Studio.** `token-goat install --visualstudio` registers the token-goat MCP server in `%USERPROFILE%\.mcp.json` and adds the token-goat read gate to `%USERPROFILE%\copilot-instructions.md`, so the agent can use `symbol`, `read`, `section`, and the other narrow reads. With `-p`, it writes `.mcp.json` and `.github/copilot-instructions.md` in the current project instead, and reminds you not to commit them. Everything else in those files stays as it was, including Claude Code's `mcpServers` list, and `uninstall --visualstudio` removes only what token-goat added. Claude Code reads the same `.mcp.json` files and refuses one without an `mcpServers` key, so when a file has none, token-goat adds an empty one. Visual Studio also reads `.vscode/mcp.json`, so if `install --vscode -p` registered token-goat there too, the install prints a note and `token-goat doctor` warns that Visual Studio lists the server twice. `token-goat mcp-status --visualstudio` reports whether the Visual Studio entry is in place.

  Visual Studio has no agent hooks, so token-goat gives no read hints, file dedup, or image shrinking there: the agent gets the MCP tools and the instructions only. Two steps are left to you after installing, and the install prints both: turn on the token-goat tools in the Copilot Chat Tools picker (Visual Studio adds new MCP tools switched off), and tick the Tools > Options setting that loads `.github/copilot-instructions.md`. See [Visual Studio users](docs/install.md#visual-studio-users) and Microsoft's [MCP servers in Visual Studio](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/visualstudio/ide/mcp-servers). None of this needs a reindex.

  If `--vscode` or `--copilot` already put the gate in the same instructions file, the Visual Studio section is a short note pointing at it instead of a second copy, and uninstalling one leaves the gate the others still need.

- **token-goat now indexes Visual Basic.** `symbol`, `read "file::Name"`, `outline`, `skeleton`, and the other narrow reads now work on VB.NET (`.vb`), VB6 and VBA modules (`.bas`), VBScript (`.vbs`), VB6 forms (`.frm`), and VB6 class modules (`.cls`). token-goat finds namespaces, modules, classes, structures, interfaces, enums and their members, subs, functions, properties, events, delegates, `Declare` statements, operators, constants, and fields, each with its full body. A `.cls` file is read as VB6 only when it starts with the VB6 class header (`VERSION 1.0 CLASS` or an `Attribute VB_Name` line); every other `.cls` is still read as Apex, exactly as before. A VB6 form's designer block is skipped, and a `.frm` that is not a VB6 form, such as a MySQL table file, gets no symbols. A `'''` XML doc comment above a declaration is kept as that symbol's docstring, and `section "File.vb::Name"` reads a `#Region "Name"` block through its `#End Region`. `map` and the notice that says a language's call sites are not indexed name a VB6 `.cls` as Visual Basic, not Apex. The new reader is [src/languages/vb.ts](src/languages/vb.ts). Upgrading reindexes your projects once, because the parser changed.
