- **token-goat now indexes COBOL and Natural.** `symbol`, `read "file::Name"`, `outline`, `skeleton`, `imports`, and `refs` now work on COBOL source and copybooks (`.cbl`, `.cob`, `.cobol`, `.cpy`) in fixed or free format, and on Software AG Natural sources (`.nsp`, `.nsn`, `.nss`, `.nsa`, `.nsl`, `.nsg`, `.nsc`, `.nsh`), in any letter case. In COBOL it finds programs (nested ones too), sections, paragraphs, level 01 and 77 data items, and file descriptions, and it follows `COPY` members and `PERFORM`, `GO TO`, and `CALL 'name'` references. In Natural it finds the object itself, inline subroutines, the `DEFINE DATA` block with its level-1 fields and views, and it follows `USING` data areas, `INCLUDE` copycode, and `PERFORM`, `CALLNAT`, and `FETCH` references. Natural maps (`.nsm`) and DDMs (`.nsd`) are not indexed yet, and `outline` says so. `dead` does not judge these two languages, because a COBOL paragraph can run by falling through from the one above it. Upgrading reindexes your projects once.
- **token-goat now indexes more file types it already knows how to read, and Oracle PL/SQL packages.** zsh, ksh and Bats scripts (`.zsh`, `.ksh`, `.bats`) are read as shell, Bazel and Starlark files (`.bzl`, `.star`, `BUILD`, `BUILD.bazel`, `WORKSPACE`, `WORKSPACE.bazel`, `MODULE.bazel`) as Python, and JSON with comments (`.jsonc`) and Avro schemas (`.avsc`) as JSON. PL/SQL files (`.pks`, `.pkb`, `.pls`, `.plsql`, `.pck`, `.prc`, `.fnc`, `.trg`, `.tps`, `.tpb`) are read as SQL, and the SQL indexer now finds package specs and bodies, the procedures and functions inside them, type bodies and triggers, each ending at its own `END name;`. COBOL lines continued with a `-` in the indicator column now read as one line, so a `PERFORM` split across two lines is still followed. Upgrading reindexes your projects once.
- **token-goat now indexes ABAP, SAS, PL/I, RPG, JCL and OpenEdge ABL.** `symbol`, `read "file::Name"`, `outline`, `skeleton`, and `imports` now work on these files, in any letter case:
  - ABAP (`.abap`): programs, class definitions and implementations with their methods, interfaces, forms, function modules, dialog modules, and macros, plus `INCLUDE` programs.
  - SAS (`.sas`): macros, nested ones too, and DATA steps, plus `%INCLUDE` files. Comments and `DATALINES` data are skipped.
  - PL/I (`.pli`, `.pl1`): procedures, nested ones too, and packages, plus `%INCLUDE` members. Sequence numbers in columns 73 to 80 are ignored.
  - RPG (`.rpgle`, `.sqlrpgle`): procedures, subroutines, prototypes, data structures, standalone fields, constants, and files, in `**FREE`, column-limited free, or fixed form, plus `/COPY` and `/INCLUDE` members. Old RPG II and RPG III `.rpg` files are not indexed.
  - JCL (`.jcl`): jobs, in-stream procedures, and named steps, plus `INCLUDE` members. In-stream data is skipped.
  - OpenEdge ABL (`.p`, `.w`, and `.cls`): internal procedures, functions, classes, interfaces, and enums with their methods, constructors, and destructors, and temp-tables, plus `{file.i}` includes and `USING` types.

  Other languages use `.p`, `.w`, and `.cls` too, so token-goat reads the start of the file first. A `.p` or `.w` is read as ABL only when it has a line only ABL writes, such as `DEFINE VARIABLE`, `FOR EACH`, or `END PROCEDURE.`. A Pascal or CWEB file is left alone, as before. A `.cls` is still read as Apex unless it has the VB6 class header or ABL lines like these. `.i` include files are not indexed on their own. The new readers are in [src/languages](src/languages). Upgrading reindexes your projects once.

- **token-goat now indexes Objective-C, Groovy, Perl, Solidity, Thrift, and the GLSL, HLSL, WGSL, and Metal shader languages.** `symbol`, `read "file::Name"`, `outline`, `skeleton`, and `imports` now work on these files:
  - Objective-C (`.mm`, plus `.m` and `.h` files that are Objective-C): classes, categories, extensions, and protocols up to their `@end`, methods named by their full selector (such as `tableView:didSelectRowAtIndexPath:`), properties, C functions, structs, and enums, plus `#import` and `@import` targets.
  - Groovy (`.groovy`, `.gvy`, `.gradle`, and `Jenkinsfile`): classes, interfaces, traits, enums, and records with their methods and constructors, Spock feature methods, Jenkins pipeline stages, and Gradle tasks, plus imports.
  - Perl (`.pl`, `.pm`, plus `.t` files that are Perl): packages and subs, plus the modules named by `use` and `require`. POD, heredocs, and everything after `__END__` are skipped.
  - Solidity (`.sol`): contracts, interfaces, and libraries with their functions, constructors, modifiers, events, errors, structs, and enums, plus imports.
  - Thrift (`.thrift`): structs, unions, exceptions, enums, services with their methods, typedefs, and constants, plus `include` files.
  - GLSL (`.glsl`, `.vert`, `.frag`, `.comp`, `.geom`, `.tesc`, `.tese`), HLSL (`.hlsl`, `.hlsli`), and Metal (`.metal`): functions, structs, uniform blocks, and `cbuffer` blocks, plus `#include` files. A function declared above its body is listed once.
  - WGSL (`.wgsl`): functions, structs, aliases, and module-level `const`, `override`, and `var` declarations.

  Other languages use `.m`, `.h`, `.pl`, and `.t` too, so token-goat reads the start of the file first. A `.m` is read as Objective-C only when it has an `#import <file>` or `#import "file"`, `@interface`, `@implementation`, or `@protocol` line, so a MATLAB file is not read as Objective-C. A `.h` stays C unless it has an `@interface` or `@protocol` line. A `.pl` that is Prolog is left alone. `.fx` is not indexed, because several languages use it. The new readers are in [src/languages](src/languages). Upgrading reindexes your projects once.

- **token-goat now indexes Fortran, Pascal and Delphi, MATLAB and Octave, and CMake.** `symbol`, `read "file::Name"`, `outline`, `skeleton`, and `imports` now work on these files:
  - Fortran (`.f`, `.for`, `.f77`, `.f90`, `.f95`, `.f03`, `.f08`): programs, modules, submodules, subroutines, functions, derived types, named interfaces, and `BLOCK DATA`, plus `USE` modules and `INCLUDE` files. `.f`, `.for`, and `.f77` files in the old fixed layout (comment marks in column 1, continuation marks in column 6) are read that way.
  - Pascal and Delphi (`.pas`, `.dpr`, `.dpk`, `.lpr`, plus `.pp` files that are Pascal): the unit, program, library, or package, classes, records, interfaces, and enums with their methods and properties, and procedures and functions with the ones nested inside them, plus `uses`, `requires`, and `contains` lists. A method written as `TFoo.Bar` is listed under `TFoo` with its body. Delphi form files (`.dfm`) saved as text list their components; binary forms are skipped.
  - MATLAB and Octave (`.m` files that are MATLAB): functions, including nested functions and files whose functions have no `end`, and `classdef` classes with their properties, methods, and events, plus `import` lines. An `end` used as an index, such as `x(end)`, does not end a block.
  - CMake (`CMakeLists.txt` and `.cmake`): functions, macros, the `project` name, and the targets made by `add_library`, `add_executable`, and `add_custom_target`, plus `include`, `add_subdirectory`, and `find_package`.

  A `.m` is read as MATLAB when it is not Objective-C and has a `function` or `classdef` line, so a Mathematica or Mercury file, or a MATLAB script with no functions, is left alone. A `.pp` is read as Pascal only when it starts with a `unit`, `program`, or `library` line, so a Puppet manifest is left alone. `.inc` and `.fpp` are not indexed, because they are used in more than one way. The new readers are in [src/languages](src/languages). Upgrading reindexes your projects once.
- **token-goat now indexes Assembly, Windows batch files, and Erlang.** `symbol`, `read "file::Name"`, `outline`, `skeleton`, and `imports` now work on these files:
  - Assembly (`.s`, `.S`, `.asm`, `.nasm`): labels written with a colon, `.macro` and `%macro` definitions, and NASM `struc` structures, plus `.include` and `%include` files. An `.asm` file written for IBM High Level Assembler is read that way instead: its `CSECT`, `DSECT`, `RSECT`, and `START` sections, and its `MACRO` definitions named by the prototype statement. A label whose name starts with a period, such as `.L1`, is local and is left out.
  - Windows batch files (`.bat`, `.cmd`): the `:label` blocks, each one running to the next label, plus the batch files a `call` runs.
  - Erlang (`.erl`, `.hrl`): the module, functions with all of their clauses, `-record` definitions, `-define` macros, and `-type` and `-opaque` declarations, plus `-include`, `-include_lib`, and `-import`.

  The first `token-goat index` after this version reindexes the project, so files in these languages get symbols.

- **The package now ships a third-party notices file.** Building `dist/` copies about two dozen small npm packages into the files you install, and their licenses ask for their copyright notices to come along. [THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md](THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md) now reproduces each one in full and is part of the published package. Run `npm run notices` to rebuild it: it reads the list from the build itself, so it names exactly what the bundle contains, and never a package that only sits in `package.json`. A new test, [tests/guards/third_party_notices.test.ts](tests/guards/third_party_notices.test.ts), fails if the file falls behind the build, if a bundled package is missing from it, or if a package that is not permissively licensed reaches the bundle at all. This needs no reindex.

- **Three new commands read an HTML file without pulling in the whole thing: `html-outline`, `html-query`, and `html-lint`.** They work on a saved web page, a generated report, or a template, and need no browser and no extra packages.
  - `html-outline` gives the shape of a document: its title, how many elements it holds, its headings, landmarks, tables, and forms, with the line each one starts on. Add `--json` for a form a program can read.
  - `html-query` pulls out the parts you name with a CSS selector, such as `#id`, `.class`, a tag name, a `>` child combinator, or an attribute test like `[href]`, `[type=text]`, `[class*=btn]`, `[href^=https]`, or `[src$=.png]`. It returns the matching HTML, or only the readable text with `--text`, or one attribute's values with `--attr`. `--head` limits how many matches come back, and `--json` returns them structured.
  - `html-lint` reports structural problems: tags left unclosed, a closing tag matching nothing, a closing tag on a void element, a DOM id used twice, a missing viewport or charset, images with no alt text, and oversized inline scripts.

  The commands are listed in [docs/cli.md](docs/cli.md). None of this needs a reindex.

- **The architecture and technical questions document now answers 31 questions instead of 14.** [docs/architecture-qa.html](docs/architecture-qa.html) adds answers on how PreToolUse rewriting works, how task success rate is measured, how skill compaction is cached, what each SQLite file holds, how to fill a cache on a machine with no network, the [PolyForm license](https://polyformproject.org/), supply chain provenance, standalone packaging, and what the token costs come to. The document names no organization, system, or tracker id. This needs no reindex.

### Security

Most of these harden the containment walk and `install --vscode`'s project-scope default, which is project scope again as of this release. Four of them concern builds already out. The first entry is one of those in part: **four** shipped flags have accepted a project-relative target. `--vscode` is the fourth, since it wrote `<cwd>/.vscode/mcp.json` and `<cwd>/.github/copilot-instructions.md` unconditionally from its very first release, v2.6.23, four releases before v2.6.28 gave it a `-p`/`--project` switch and made user scope the default. `--visualstudio` and `--cursor` gain their project scopes in this release, so no build has shipped one without the fix. The other three previously released defects are `~/.token-goat`'s directory mode and the two containment-walk defects.

- **A repository could make a project-scope install read a private file, copy it into the working tree, and write back through the link.** Every installer that accepts a project-relative target was affected: `install --project`, `install --copilot --local`, `install --pi --local`, `install --vscode` (`--project` from v2.6.28 on), `install --visualstudio --project` and `install --cursor --project`. **Four of those have shipped**, with these affected ranges -- each re-derived from the tagged trees rather than from memory, since a wrong lower bound here tells a real user they were never exposed:

  - `install --project` / `-p`, **v2.0.0 through v2.9.10**. Project scope arrived with the TypeScript rewrite in v2.0.0 (`-p, --project` in that tag's `src/cli.ts`; the v1.x Python line had no project scope at all), and `<cwd>/.claude/settings.json` has been read and rewritten through whatever that path resolves to ever since. The stronger half -- copying the link target's *bytes* into the working tree -- starts later, at **v2.6.3**, the first release to define `backupFile`, whose body was a bare four-line `copyFileSync` to a `.bak.<ISO>` sibling -- and `copyFileSync` follows a symlink at the source.
  - `install --pi --local`, **v2.6.3 through v2.9.10**. v2.6.3 is the first release whose CLI passes `{ local: opts.local }` into `installPi`, pointing it at `<cwd>/.pi/extensions/token-goat.ts`.
  - `install --copilot --local`, **v2.6.6 through v2.9.10**. Not v2.6.5: that release's bridge already had the `local` option and the project hooks directory, but its CLI called `installCopilotCli()` with no arguments and documented `--local` as meaning something only alongside `--pi`, so the combination was unreachable. v2.6.6 is where `installCopilotCli({ local: opts.local === true })` first appears.
  - `install --vscode`, **v2.6.23 through v2.9.10**. v2.6.23 is the first release containing `src/bridges/vscode_install.ts` at all, and its `installVscode(projectRoot = process.cwd())` had no scope option: every install wrote `<cwd>/.vscode/mcp.json` and `<cwd>/.github/copilot-instructions.md`, with the help text calling them "project-local". The `-p`/`--project` switch and the user-scope default only arrived at v2.6.28, so the flag spelling that names the exposure today is five releases younger than the exposure.

  `install --project`'s backup step used `copyFileSync`, which follows a symlink at the source; `--copilot --local`, `--pi --local` and `--vscode` had no backup step to copy anything out, but still read and rewrote through a symlinked `.github/copilot-instructions.md`, `.pi/extensions/token-goat.ts` or `.vscode/mcp.json`, so a clone could steer token-goat's own block into a file outside the tree. Nothing checked what any of those targets actually was. A clone that committed `.github/copilot-instructions.md` as a symlink to `~/.ssh/id_ed25519`, or `.vscode/mcp.json` as one to `~/.docker/config.json`, would get that file's bytes written to `<repo>/<name>.bak.<ISO>` -- untracked, inside the clone, and swept up by the next `git add -A`. The same follow also meant the installer read and rewrote through the link. Containment is now enforced by the write helpers themselves rather than by each installer remembering to ask for it. An installer declares its scope once at its entry point, and `backupFile`, `ensureDirSync`, the atomic writers and `upsertDelimitedBlock` each refuse a write that the declared project root does not contain, naming the path. The check sits in the write helpers rather than in each installer, so an installer cannot forget to call it. What it does **not** do is fail closed when an installer declares no scope. It cannot: `backupFile`, `ensureDirSync`, the atomic writers and `upsertDelimitedBlock` are the whole codebase's write path, not the installers' -- the indexer, the worker, snapshots, the created-configs ledger and `write-file` all go through them with no install run to declare anything -- so refusing every undeclared write would refuse essentially every write token-goat makes. Declaring a user root at the install dispatcher does not rescue it either: a project-scope install legitimately writes user-scope files (`~/.claude/CLAUDE.md`, the skill, the shared hook shim), and confining those broke `install -p` outright once already. So an installer that declares no scope writes exactly as it did before, and what catches the omission is a **structural** guard rather than a runtime one: a test enumerates every module that writes a repo-relative path and fails on any that does not declare a scope, a second enumerates every filesystem mutation inside those modules and fails on any that bypasses a containment-checking helper, and a third fails on any of them containing `async`/`await`, since the scope is a module-level variable restored synchronously. Each pins its own population with a floor, a ceiling and exact member anchors, so a rename or a moved file cannot empty it silently. A separate behavioral sweep runs the built binary against poisoned clones for each of the six flags above. The containment check rather than a leaf `lstat` is what is needed, because a repository can equally commit `.github` itself as a directory symlink. Containment holds whether or not the file exists yet, which is the case that matters: `realpathSync` throws the moment any component is missing, so an implementation that resolves only whole paths falls back to a lexical comparison in precisely the situation an installer is always in -- about to create a file. Containment is therefore decided by walking the path one segment at a time from its root, following each link with `readlink` as it is met and re-appending the segments that do not exist yet -- not by resolving the nearest existing ancestor in one call, which is the shape that first shipped here and which had two blind spots of its own (see the two entries below). `copyFileSync` also now passes `COPYFILE_EXCL`, so the backup cannot be steered by a symlink planted at the destination; a name already taken -- by a planted link, or by token-goat's own backup of the same file earlier in the same millisecond, which a project-scope install really does produce -- gets a numeric suffix rather than aborting the install. User-scope installs are unchanged: a symlinked `~/.copilot/...` dotfile is a normal setup and still works.
- **A repository could switch off every VS Code hook by committing one file.** The user-scope hook stood down whenever `.github/hooks/token-goat.json` merely existed in the workspace, on the reasoning that a project-scope copy would answer instead. A clone that committed that path containing `{}` had no project copy to answer, so read dedup, session hints, image shrinking, edit-queue reindexing and the pre-approval path gate all went quiet for that workspace, with no error and no output -- indistinguishable from working. Standing down now requires two independent agreements: the ownership sidecar `token-goat.owners` naming `vscode`, and the created-configs ledger in token-goat's own data directory recording that an install on this machine wrote that exact path. A clone can still forge the sidecar, so this raises the cost of the attack rather than closing the class; the ledger is the half a clone cannot reach. Setting `TOKEN_GOAT_LOG` now prints one line to stderr whenever a copy stands down, so the silent case is observable.
- **A UNC workspace root made the VS Code hook open an SMB connection before the user approved anything.** That same lookup joined the workspace `cwd` VS Code supplied straight onto a path and statted it. On Windows, statting `\\host\share\...` opens a connection to `host` and offers an NTLM authentication, and the in-process hook path has no timeout to bound it. The lookup now runs through `vscodePathAllowed`, the same gate every pre-approval handler uses, which declines a two-separator root outright. A new guard (`tests/guards/pre_handler_fs_touches_are_gated.test.ts`) covers the structural half: `tests/vscode_pre_handler_path_gate.test.ts` sweeps the handler registry and so cannot see anything the relay does before dispatch, which is where this call sat and where two earlier pre-approval touches also sat. The new guard walks the call graph from `relayInProcess` up to the first handler dispatch and fails on any `fs`/`net` call that is neither gated nor named in an exemption table with its reason, so an unclassified call site is red on arrival rather than red once somebody notices.
- **The VS Code hook could create token-goat's data directory world-readable.** On a fresh install the hook can be the first thing that ever writes there, and the dedupe marker directory was created with a bare recursive `mkdir` and its prune stamp with a bare `writeFileSync`, both taking the process umask -- 0755 on a stock Linux box, leaving cached command output, fetched pages and the per-project index listable by every local user. Both now go through `ensureDirSync`/`atomicWriteText`, the helpers that harden the enclosing storage root to 0700 first, and so does every other directory creation in token-goat's own storage -- the created-configs ledger, the per-session sidecars for queued context and tool-failure tracking, the embedding model cache, the OCR cache and the skill-output directory, eight sites in all. Two of those eight (the OCR cache, and the per-session sidecars) resolve under `~/.token-goat` rather than under the data directory, and for one release the helper hardened the data directory only, so those two were routed and got nothing; see the entry below. A new guard (`tests/guards/storage_dirs_are_hardened.test.ts`) enumerates them from the call graph and fails on any bare `mkdir` on a storage path that is not named in an exemption table with its reason, so the next one is red on arrival rather than found by the next audit. Windows is unaffected: Node ignores POSIX modes there and the directory inherits its parent's ACL.
- **`~/.token-goat` was left at the umask default, so on a stock Linux box every local user could read another user's session snapshots.** This one is about released builds, not the unreleased project scope above: `~/.token-goat` has existed since before v2.9.10 and nothing has ever mode-hardened it, while the data directory next door was hardened to 0700. It is the more sensitive of the two roots. `session_snapshots/` holds verbatim copies of files the model read, `sessions/` holds session state and its pending-context sidecars, `ocr-cache/` holds text lifted out of images the user viewed, `image_shrink_cache/` copies of those images, and the blob subdirectories hold fetched web bodies -- so `saveSessionState` was redacting secrets before writing into a tree any local account could list and read. On a stock Debian or Ubuntu box `$HOME` is 0755, which is what makes this reachable rather than theoretical; a `$HOME` of 0700 already blocked it. `ensureDirSync` now hardens whichever of the two roots the requested path falls under, in the same two steps the data root uses (the parent is left at the umask default, because `$HOME` is not token-goat's to tighten), and an existing permissive directory is chmodded down on the next run. Windows is unaffected: Node ignores POSIX modes there. Not remotely exploitable -- it needs a local account on the same machine.
- **A dangling symlink sitting directly in a Windows drive root read as inside that root.** The containment walk found the nearest existing ancestor with `path.posix.dirname`, and `path.posix.dirname('x:/name')` is `'x:'` with no slash, so the loop ended before the drive root itself was ever tried and the merely-lexical answer was returned without the link ever being read. `isInsideRoot('x:/link', 'x:/')` therefore answered true for a link pointing anywhere. One level deeper the same shape was refused correctly, and POSIX was never affected (`dirname('/name')` is `/`), so this was invisible on two of the three CI platforms. Measured on a real `subst` drive against the built artifact, before and after.
- **A `..` segment could step out through a symlink and back in by name.** `<root>/link/../sneak.txt`, with `link` a junction pointing outside the root, read as inside it: `..` was collapsed lexically with `path.resolve` before any link was resolved, which cancels the link instead of the directory the link actually names. The walk now resolves each segment in order and applies `..` to where the path has actually arrived, so the same string is refused; an ordinary `sub/../a.ts` inside the root is still accepted.
- **Migrating a user-scope install to project scope deleted the recovery copies it had just written.** The first project-scope install after the upgrade migrates an existing user-scope one by calling the uninstall path, which ends by sweeping the `.bak.<ISO>` files token-goat created -- correct when the user asked to uninstall, wrong here. This is the one path that rewrites a user-scope file without being asked to, so it is exactly where the "every overwritten file gets a recovery copy" guarantee added earlier in this release had to hold, and it was the one place it did not: the copy was written and deleted seconds later, in the same run. A migration now runs the uninstall with backups kept; a real `uninstall` still sweeps them.
- **One long path from the model could hang the VS Code hook before the user approved anything.** The containment walk added earlier in this release resolved a path one segment at a time with no length limit, and the cost grew faster than quadratically: measured on one machine, a 106-byte path answered in 1.3 ms, a 6,946-byte one in 38 ms, and a 1.89 MB one in 1,301,906 ms -- 21 minutes of pinned CPU for a single call, against 14.9 ms for one `realpathSync` of the same string. The reachable path is the pre-approval gate every read, write and image hook goes through, on a path the model chose and the user has not yet approved. In VS Code that hook runs in-process with no timeout available, so the editor's hook does not get slow, it stops answering; under Claude Code it is a subprocess holding a core for as long as it takes. The walk now refuses any path over 4,096 bytes -- PATH_MAX on Linux, and four times the longest path Windows or macOS will hand it -- both on entry and on the resolved form as links expand it, and an over-length path is treated as unresolvable, which fails closed. The same 1.89 MB path now answers in about 2 ms. The queue the walk consumed was also an `Array.shift()`, quadratic on its own; that is now an index cursor.
- **An unreadable directory made the containment walk answer "inside the project" instead of "unknown".** Every error from the per-segment `lstat` was treated as "this component is not a symlink", which is true for a component that does not exist and false for one the process cannot read: an EACCES on an ancestor produced an under-resolved path and a permissive verdict, on the same pre-approval gate. Absence (`ENOENT`, `ENOTDIR`) still lets the walk continue, because an installer is always about to create a file that is not there yet; every other errno is now unresolvable, and unresolvable is never contained.
- **A storage root that failed to harden once was never hardened again.** `ensureDataDirPrivate` and `ensureHomeDirPrivate` recorded the root as done *before* attempting the `mkdir` and `chmod`, under a deliberately empty catch. One transient failure -- a backup tool holding the directory, a momentary EACCES, a parent that is briefly a file -- therefore banked itself as a success for the rest of the process, which in the CLI is one command and in the long-lived worker is the whole session, and every write after it landed in a directory left at the umask default with nothing retrying. The memo is now written only after the work actually completes.
- **`TOKEN_GOAT_HOME` was accepted as a relative path, which silently switched off 0700 hardening.** Its siblings `LOCALAPPDATA` and `XDG_DATA_HOME` already ran through a validator requiring an absolute path; this one had a bare empty-string check and returned whatever it was given. Since hardening is dispatched on whether a directory falls under `tokenGoatHome()`, a relative value resolved the storage tree against the current working directory and matched nothing -- and inside a VS Code hook the working directory is the workspace folder, so session snapshots of every file the model read would have been written into an untrusted clone at the umask default. It now goes through the same validator, and a relative or blank value falls back to `~/.token-goat`.
- **On Linux, uninstalling could delete a settings file token-goat never created.** The ledger recording which config files an install wrote folded its keys to lower case unconditionally, so on a case-sensitive filesystem `~/Repo/.zed/settings.json` and `~/repo/.zed/settings.json` -- two different files -- answered to one entry. `uninstall --zed` deletes an empty `settings.json` on exactly that answer. The mirror of the same bug meant the second directory's entry was seen as already present and never recorded, so its backups were orphaned at uninstall while the output said they had been removed. The ledger now folds case only where the filesystem does. macOS and Windows were unaffected, since there the two spellings really are one file.

- **Reports now escape the text a document supplies, everywhere token-goat speaks in its own voice.** A file, a PDF, a video or a transcript can name itself after one of token-goat's own messages, and several commands printed that name straight into a line that reads as token-goat's. Every one of them now neutralizes it first, so the text still shows but no longer reads as an instruction from the tool:
  - `pdf-meta` prints the Title and Author from a PDF's information dictionary. Both are chosen by whoever made the file, and neither was checked or redacted on either the text or the `--json` branch. They now go through the same redaction and escaping every other document command already used, so a secret in a title is hidden and a forged message is shown as ordinary text.
  - `video-chapters` prints chapter titles and subtitle stream names read from the video file.
  - `transcript-outline` prints the first 60 characters of a cue, one line below the speaker labels that were already escaped.
  - `transcript` now wraps its output in the same "untrusted content" fence the other document commands use, and redacts secrets before printing. A spoken or pasted password in a meeting transcript was printed as it was.
  - `sharepoint-resolve` printed the sharing link you passed it, query string and all. SharePoint puts access tokens in that query string, so it is now removed before the link is shown, matching what the rest of that code already promised.
  - The error messages every command falls back to: a message quoting a spreadsheet's sheet names, a file name, or up to 50 lines of a file being edited went to the error stream unescaped.
  - `install --vscode`, `install --visualstudio`, `purge`, and `session-outline` print paths and transcript text that can carry the same forged names.

  None of this needs a reindex.

- **On VS Code with no folder open, hooks no longer treat your home directory as the project.** VS Code starts a hook in your home directory when no workspace folder is open, and it runs hooks before it asks you to approve the call. Token-goat used that directory as the boundary for which files a hook may open, which allowed any file under your home directory. Token-goat now marks a working directory it had to choose for itself and refuses to use one as a boundary, so these hooks open nothing until VS Code tells them where the workspace is. The rule that decides whether a path is inside the workspace is unchanged, and so is behavior when a folder is open.

- **A repository could steer the unrestricted-spawn advisory into reading files outside the project.** `findRestrictedAgentNames()` walks the home and project `.claude/agents` rosters to build that advisory, and followed symlinked directories in both. Following them under the home roster is deliberate, since a user may keep a shared collection directory there, but a project roster is repository-authored content: a checked-in symlink at or under `.claude/agents` pointed the walk anywhere on the machine, and the `name` field of whatever `.md` files it found was surfaced in a model-facing advisory. The project half of the walk no longer follows symlinks. Changed in [src/hooks_agent_spawn.ts](src/hooks_agent_spawn.ts).

- **A failed tool call's error text is now redacted before it is written to disk.** `postToolUseFailureHandler` derives a dedup signature from the raw error a failed call returns (Bash stderr, an MCP tool's own error, a fetch failure) and persists it to a per-session `.tool-failures.json` sidecar. Nothing on that path called `redactSecrets`, so an authentication error that echoed the key it rejected, or a `curl` failure carrying a bearer token, landed in plaintext and stayed there for the life of the session. The error text is redacted before the signature is derived. Changed in [src/hooks_tool_failure.ts](src/hooks_tool_failure.ts).

- **`pr-slice` and the document-extraction commands now redact secrets, not only fence them.** `pr-slice`'s diff, comment bodies and description, and the output of `pdf-outline`, `pdf-locate`, `xlsx-*`, `pptx-*` and `docx-outline`, were fenced against prompt injection but never passed through `redactSecrets`. A pull-request comment or a spreadsheet cell can carry a leaked credential exactly as a WebSearch, WebFetch or MCP result can, and those already redact. Both families now redact at the choke point each already funnels through. Changed in [src/read_commands.ts](src/read_commands.ts) and [src/cli.ts](src/cli.ts).

- **The screenshot browser now refuses the same private and reserved IP ranges `webfetch` does.** `screenshot.ts` kept its own copy of the range table and had drifted from `webfetch.ts`: it admitted carrier-grade NAT (`100.64.0.0/10`), IETF protocol assignments (`192.0.0.0/24`), benchmarking space (`198.18.0.0/15`), multicast (`224.0.0.0/4`) and reserved space (`240.0.0.0/4`), all of which `webfetch` already refused, and the name-resolution half reused the same narrow table. Both the literal-IP refusal and the hook that re-applies it to every redirect hop and sub-resource sit on the path from a URL an attacker can influence. There is now one classifier, shared by both. Changed in [src/screenshot.ts](src/screenshot.ts).

- **A crafted PDF can no longer crash `token-goat pdf-outline`.** The PDF specification puts no bound on how deeply bookmarks may nest, and the outline tree is marshaled through an in-process `structuredClone` inside `pdfjs-dist`. A file under 100 KB chaining roughly 800 single-child bookmarks overflowed the call stack during that clone, and the failure surfaced as an unhandled rejection inside library internals rather than a rejection of the promise being awaited, so an ordinary `try`/`catch` never saw it and Node ended the process. Outline depth is now bounded. Changed in [src/pdf_extract.ts](src/pdf_extract.ts).

- **A spreadsheet can no longer declare a scan larger than the data it holds.** `listSheets`, `headSheet` and `querySheet` scanned from the first row and column up to the sheet's declared last row and column, and a sheet declares those from the highest cell reference in its XML. One cell placed at the far corner of the format's maximum grid (about a million rows by sixteen thousand columns) is enough to declare it. A 6.4 KB file with a single cell at `XFD1048576` made the row-by-column loop run roughly 17 billion iterations, a synchronous hang lasting minutes. The scan is now bounded by what the sheet actually contains. Changed in [src/xlsx_extract.ts](src/xlsx_extract.ts).

- **Three more places where a filename or a command string could forge token-goat's own voice are now escaped.** The pre-compaction manifest's `SAFE_TO_DISCARD` superseded-read rows interpolated a file path raw, unlike every sibling row in the same manifest; the duplicate-subagent advisory interpolated a prior outstanding prompt raw; and the manifest's rerun and cached-output rows interpolated a shell command that had been redacted but never marker-neutralized. That manifest is emitted under a preamble asking the summarizing model to reproduce those rows verbatim, so a repository that names a file after token-goat's own marker, or a command string carrying one, could speak in token-goat's voice. All three now route through the same neutralizer the other sites use. Changed in [src/hooks_compact.ts](src/hooks_compact.ts) and [src/hooks_agent_spawn.ts](src/hooks_agent_spawn.ts).

- **An install path containing a double quote can no longer break out of the command line token-goat writes.** `hookCommandFor` wrapped the Node executable path, the script path and the entry path in bare double quotes with no escaping, then wrote the result into another tool's config file (Claude Code `settings.json`, and the Grok, Kimi and Copilot CLI configs) for that tool's shell to parse later. A double quote is a legal filename character on macOS and Linux. Embedded quotes are now escaped. Windows filenames cannot contain that character, so that platform was never affected. Changed in [src/util.ts](src/util.ts).

- **The MCP confinement gate no longer admits a target it cannot resolve.** `checkWithinProjectRoot` compares the resolved location of a requested path against the resolved location of the project root. When resolution threw, the helper behind both sides returned the path it was given, so the comparison quietly degraded from a real-location check to a text prefix check, and anything that merely looked like it sat under the root was admitted -- the read that followed was no longer bounded by the gate. A symlink loop, a permission error on an intermediate directory, or a path too long for the OS all took that branch. Only absence is a legitimate reason to fall back to the text form -- naming a file that does not exist yet is an ordinary read failure, not a boundary breach -- so absence still does, and every other failure now refuses. Affected **v2.6.28 through v2.9.10**: v2.6.28 is the first release containing `checkWithinProjectRoot` at all, and it shipped with the fallback already in place. Changed in [src/mcp_server.ts](src/mcp_server.ts), with `tests/mcp_server_unresolvable_target_fails_closed.test.ts` building a real symlink loop, reading the resulting errno off the live OS rather than assuming it, and failing if either half of the rule breaks.

- **A path the confinement gate rewrote before checking it could be read from outside the workspace.** The gate resolves a requested path against the project root after normalizing it, but the handler forwards the caller's original spelling, which is deliberate: normalizing on the way through would mean checking one string and reading another. That protects against the gate being looser than the reader, and nothing protected against the reverse. Normalization rewrites the WSL mount form `/mnt/c/...` into `c:/...` on every platform, because a program running under WSL emits that form while on Linux. On Linux and macOS `c:/...` is a *relative* path, so a request for `/mnt/c/Users/you/.ssh/id_rsa` was measured as a harmless file sitting inside the project and approved, while the read that followed used the untouched original and reached the real file. The identity pin did not catch it either: the pin was recorded under the rewritten spelling, so the read's own lookup missed and fell through to an unchecked read. Windows was never affected, because `c:/...` names a drive there. Both spellings must now land inside the root. Changed in [src/mcp_server.ts](src/mcp_server.ts), with `tests/mcp_server_normalization_asymmetry.test.ts` covering it on Linux and macOS, where the case exists.

- **A relative path given to the MCP `grep` tool was searched from the wrong directory.** `grep` accepts a list of files and directories. The confinement gate measured each one against the project root the caller supplied, but the search itself resolved a relative entry against the directory the server process happened to start in. Where those two differ -- the ordinary case, since the root arrives with each call -- searching for `secret.txt` checked one file and read another, and because the two spellings disagreed, the identity pin recorded by the gate never matched and the read went unchecked. Relative search paths now resolve against the same root the gate used. The command-line `grep` is unchanged: it supplies no project root, so its paths stay relative to your current directory as before. Changed in [src/read_commands.ts](src/read_commands.ts), with `tests/mcp_server_grep_relative_path_anchoring.test.ts` covering both halves -- that the escape is refused, and that a relative path genuinely inside the root is still searched.

- **Reads under a WSL-mounted project root were left unverified after being allowed.** When the confinement gate approves a file it also records that file's identity, so the read can prove it opened the same object rather than one swapped in behind the path afterwards. The record is filed under the path, and the gate filed it under a rewritten spelling of the path while the read looked it up under the original. On a project root reached through the WSL mount form -- `/mnt/c/...`, the ordinary way a project on a Windows drive is opened from inside WSL -- the two never matched, so every read under such a root skipped the identity check and the companion check that covers a file created between approval and reading. The gate's own allow-or-refuse decision was correct throughout, which is why no test saw it: the tests all ask whether a path is allowed, and this was a file being read that was genuinely allowed. Both spellings are now recorded. `tests/mcp_server_pins_the_spelling_the_reader_opens.test.ts` asserts the rule the gate has to satisfy -- whatever spelling the read will look up must be one the gate recorded -- rather than asserting the fix, so a future rewrite that invents a third spelling fails it too. Changed in [src/mcp_server.ts](src/mcp_server.ts).

- **A blocked read caused by an unreadable workspace root no longer reports itself as an attempt to read outside the project.** If the project root itself could not be resolved -- an unmounted network share, a deleted directory, a permission change on a parent -- every tool refused with a message saying the requested file was outside the project root, which sends you looking for a traversal that never happened. The three refusals are now told apart: outside the root, a target that cannot be resolved, and a root that cannot be resolved. All three still refuse. Changed in [src/mcp_server.ts](src/mcp_server.ts).

- **A directory whose name differed from the project root only by a character JavaScript folds and the filesystem does not could be read as if it were inside the root.** The confinement check compares the target path against the root after lowercasing both, so that `C:\Work\a.ts` and `c:\work\a.ts` are recognised as the same place on a case-insensitive volume. It lowercased by Unicode's rules, and NTFS folds by its own table; the two disagree on roughly a hundred characters. Every disagreement where JavaScript folds a pair NTFS keeps apart is a way in: with the project root set to `<base>\work`, a `read` of `<base>\worK\secret.txt` -- the same name with a KELVIN SIGN (U+212A) in place of the `k` -- returned the file, and `grep` walked the same directory, because the gate saw the root's own spelling while the filesystem opened a genuinely separate directory. The identity pin offered nothing here: the gate pinned the file it went on to open, having already concluded it was in-root. Containment now folds ASCII only, which is stricter than either table -- it can refuse a legitimate read whose root and target differ in the case of a non-ASCII letter, and it cannot admit one that is outside. The fold used for stored path keys is deliberately unchanged, since it is mirrored into SQL and the two must stay byte-identical. `tests/containment_case_fold_is_ascii_only.test.ts` creates both directories on the real volume, refuses to draw a conclusion unless that volume actually keeps them apart, and then checks the refusal end-to-end through the MCP server.

- **A symlink inside the workspace could make the containment check itself contact a file server.** The pre-tool gate refuses a UNC path outright, because a stat of `\\host\share` opens an SMB connection to an address the model chose. That check reads the path as written, and a symlink does not have to be written that way: a link at `C:\work\link` pointing at a share, with a request to read `C:\work\link\x.txt`, is an entirely local spelling. The lexical check passed it, and then the containment walk followed the link and stat'd the path under the share -- reaching the host from inside the function whose job was to stop exactly that, while the user was still being asked whether to allow the call. The walk now refuses a link whose target leaves the local filesystem for a share, before the stat that would reach it; reading the link's own bytes is local, so the decision can be made without contacting anything. A project whose root is itself on a share is untouched, since only an escape onto one is refused. The verdict was never wrong here -- the walk would have answered "outside" a moment later -- so a test asking what it decided could not see this at all. `tests/symlink_into_a_share_is_refused_before_it_dials.test.ts` records every path handed to the filesystem and fails on a network one whatever the answer was. Two follow-ons from the same review are fixed with it: the refusal now asks the root the walk is standing on rather than the path as the caller spelled it, which had wrongly refused a legitimate second share under a working directory that was already on one; and `\\.\pipe\name` is no longer normalized the way a `\\?\` junction target is, which had turned a link into the device namespace into the relative `pipe/name` and left the device refusal with no device to see. A link onto a mapped drive letter is deliberately not refused, and the reason is recorded in the source: telling a mapped letter from a local one needs a call that touches the drive, and a mapped letter reaches only where the user already chose to mount, never an address a planted link names. Changed in [src/path_containment.ts](src/path_containment.ts).

- **A pre-tool hook contacted a file server the model named, before the user approved the tool call.** Hooks run ahead of the approval prompt, so a path a model puts in a `Read` or `Write` is untrusted until the user has said yes; on Windows even a `stat` of a UNC path such as `\\server\share\x.txt` opens an SMB connection to that host, which is an outbound request to an address the model chose. That was already refused, but only on VS Code -- the check sat behind a test for the VS Code harness marker, and nothing about it is VS-Code-specific. Measured on the default harness, a `Read` of a UNC path pointing at an unroutable address took 21.0 seconds against 21 milliseconds for a local file: the whole connect timeout, spent before the prompt appeared. UNC and device paths (`\\server\share`, `//server/share`, `\\?\`, `\\.\`) are now declined by every pre-tool handler on every harness. The other half of the gate, which confines a path to the workspace folder, stays VS Code only, because only VS Code supplies a workspace folder to confine it to. `tests/pre_tool_unc_gate_is_harness_independent.test.ts` sweeps the live hook registry with a default-harness event and fails if any handler touches such a path, and checks that an ordinary path outside the project is still read, so the fix is not a blanket refusal.

- **A search pattern could hang the process it ran in, and take the whole MCP server down with it.** JavaScript's regex engine backtracks, and once `test` has started there is no timeout, no abort signal, and no way to stop it. `grep` handed its pattern straight to the engine and then ran it over every line of every file it walked, so `grep` with a pattern like `^(a+)+$` against a line of forty ordinary characters never came back: measured here, still running after 166 seconds, against a millisecond for the same search now. On the command line that is a hung command someone can interrupt. Over MCP it is worse, because the server is single-threaded and the pattern comes from the model rather than from the person at the keyboard, so one poisoned instruction in a file the model had read would stall every other tool in the session. Every command that takes a pattern from a model or a command line -- `grep`, every `--grep` flag including `pptx-text` and `transcript`, `pdf-locate`, `affected --filter`, `csv-query --where col~=`, `commands` -- now compiles it through one guard. The guard decides by measurement, not by shape: it runs the compiled pattern against inputs of growing length and refuses one whose time climbs out of a millisecond budget. The ladder of lengths is the guard, and each end of it exists for a defect. It starts at four characters because the probe was otherwise the weapon: `^(a|a|a|a)+$` multiplies by four per character and took 27.8 seconds at sixteen, which was the first length tried, so the guard hung inside the measurement it was taking to decide whether the pattern could hang anything -- and no budget can help, because a budget is read after a synchronous match returns and JavaScript cannot interrupt one. It ends at 512 because super-linear is not the same as exponential: `^(a+)(a+)(a+)(a+)b$` costs half a millisecond at 36 characters and 18.6 seconds at 800, and `grep` with it against a file holding one 3000-character line -- a minified bundle, a base64 blob -- did not return in two minutes. A list of dangerous shapes is only ever a list of the shapes somebody thought of, and the two directions it gets wrong both cost something real. `^(a|a)+$` and `^(b|bb)+$` repeat no group that already repeats, and both hang; `^(?:[a-z]+-)+[a-z]+$` is a slug matcher that looks exactly like `(a+)+` and matched 200 KB in a millisecond, so refusing it by shape would have taken away a search somebody wanted. The shape check survives only to word the refusal. The inputs are built from the characters the pattern itself names as well as from a fixed alphabet, because what makes a pattern explode is ambiguity over the text it matches -- probing `^(b|bb)+$` with `aaaa` finds nothing. Escapes count as characters they name: skipping them meant `^(\s|\s\s)+$`, the same defect spelled with whitespace, took 20.9 seconds against forty spaces while being accepted, and `^(\x62|\x62\x62)+$` took 2.9 seconds while containing no literal at all. A character class is compiled and asked what it matches rather than read: `[^a0]` names the only two characters it will not match, so reading it seeded the one input set guaranteed to fail at position zero, and `^([^a0]+)+$` was accepted while taking 113.8 seconds against thirty-four characters. The character the input ends with is chosen the same way, by trying candidates until one makes the pattern fail, because an input that matches straight through never backtracks and measures nothing. `\cA` is read as the control character it names and `\p{Script=Greek}` is asked rather than spelled out, both of which were previously seeded with the ASCII of their own notation.

  Two things the ladder cannot reach are now handled without running them. A quadratic pattern clears every rung and every ratio honestly -- `^(a+)(a+)(a+)!$` costs 13 ms at 512 characters and 92 ms at 1000, a factor of seven over a doubling -- and 2.4 seconds against one 3000-character line, so the last doubling is extrapolated out to a realistic line length instead of being run at one. A gate is the other one: a prefix that matches nothing until the input reaches some length, and then hands whatever is left over to the ambiguous part behind it. The ladder used to double, and a doubling ladder walks straight past a gate -- `^a{300}(a|aa)+$` times zero at the 256-character rung and then, at 512, hands 212 characters to `(a|aa)+` and never comes back, so the probe becomes the weapon. The ladder now climbs in fixed four-character steps to 512, which is the only shape that bounds what a gate can release: the first rung that opens one hands the ambiguous part at most four characters more than the rung below it, so the explosion is measured at its start rather than in the middle. A zero-width gate is not bounded by that at all, because it consumes nothing -- `^(?=a{300})(a|aa)+$` hands over the whole 300 characters the moment the assertion passes -- so a pattern carrying assertions is rewritten before it is run rather than measured as written. A positive assertion is unwrapped into an ordinary group, not deleted: deleting it threw away the length cap it was there to impose, and `^(?=.{1,8}$)(\w+\s?)+$`, a matcher that can never see more than eight characters, was refused as though it could see a thousand. A negative assertion cannot be unwrapped, since unwrapping inverts it, so it is removed and its body measured separately. Counted repeats are clamped to eight during that rewrite, so the cost the gate was hiding shows up at a length the guard can survive. Finally, the cap on how many of the pattern's own characters get probed was itself a bypass while the kept ones were simply the first few: the samples are now ordered by how often the pattern names each character, because a pattern is ambiguous over a character it names more than once.

  Four ways of hiding a character from the seeding were closing at the same time. The character an input ends with has to be one the pattern refuses, and the candidate list held only printable ones -- but `.` refuses nothing except a line terminator, so `^(.|..)+$` found no terminator, timed zero everywhere, and was accepted; a newline and U+2028 are now in the list. A legacy octal escape names a character in a notation the reader did not know: `\142` is a `b`, and reading it as a backslash and three digits seeded `^(\142|\142\142)+$` with digits it can never match. A character class is compiled and asked what it matches, which returns nothing when the class negates every candidate it is offered, so the class that negated all of them was accepted; there is now a fallback sweep across the printable ASCII range and a spread of higher code points behind the candidate list. And a pattern long enough that compiling and probing it is itself the expense is refused up front at 4096 characters, rather than measured -- the measurement was linear in the pattern's length at every one of 128 rungs. In the other direction, a rung that reads slow is now timed a second time and the smaller of the two stands, because a single garbage collection pause during one rung was enough to refuse an ordinary pattern.

  A terminator chosen once, at the shortest rung, was the last way of making every rung meaningless. The input has to FAIL for anything to backtrack, so the guard picks the first terminator the pattern refuses at four characters -- and `^((a|aa){5,})!$` refuses `aaaa!` for the one reason that stops applying at the very next rung, that it has not got five repetitions yet. From five characters on `aaaaa!` matches, a matching run stops at its first successful path, and all 128 rungs read 0.0 ms while the pattern costs 18.2 seconds against forty-five characters; `^(a|aa){5,}!$` is the same 20.0 seconds without the outer group. A measurement now carries whether the run matched, and a rung whose run matched re-picks a terminator from the same list rather than believing the number. Every attempt is timed and held to the same budget, so the re-pick cannot become the weapon either, and an ordinary pattern pays nothing for it because its calibrated terminator still fails on the first try. `^((a|aa){5,10})!$` is still accepted, and is in the test file's must-accept list to keep it that way: it can never see more than twenty characters, so distrusting counted minima wholesale would have cost a real search. The projection that extrapolates the last doubling now finds its two rungs by length rather than by position, which is the one change in this release a wall-clock test cannot see -- on clean timings the exponent comes out the same from the adjacent pair, and what that pair loses is noise tolerance, since a ratio taken over four characters puts 0.0113 in the denominator and multiplies a garbage-collection pause by eighty-eight. Three tests feed it synthetic ladders instead.

  One level up from that, the list of terminators is finite, and a pattern can simply name every character in it. `^(a|aa)+[!a0 ￿\n ]?$` holds all seven, so no input the ladder can build is capable of failing, every run matched, the re-pick above had nothing left to re-pick, and all 128 rungs read 0.0 ms while the pattern cost 16.7 seconds against forty-five characters -- 30.8 seconds for the `*` spelling. A ladder that finds nothing in the list now sweeps a wider alphabet behind it, at most three times per pattern, and remembers what worked so the remaining rungs pay once. The tails it sweeps have to grow, not just vary: the guard appends one tail character and `^(a|aa)+[\s\S]?$` swallows exactly that one, so only `bb` falsifies it and only `bbb` falsifies the `{0,2}` spelling. They stop growing at nine, one past the bound every counted quantifier is clamped to before the pattern is judged -- which is why `{0,500}` needs no tail of its own. Length alone is not the whole of it: a run of nine identical characters is a *shape*, and a pattern can name that shape and match it, which is what `^(a|aa)+(?:[\s\S]?|([\s\S])\2{8})$` does. Each sweep now also builds a run whose last character differs from the rest, which no repetition of a single character can absorb. A gate is also cut down when it is spelled as a run rather than as a count. Cutting `^a{600}(a|aa)+$` down to `{8}` is what lets a rung reach past the gate and find the ambiguous tail behind it, but six hundred literal `a` characters are the same gate written the other way and were copied through untouched: one pattern character is one input character, the ladder's last rung is 512, so no rung ever got there. Every rung read 0 ms, the pattern was accepted in 1 ms, and it cost 62 ms at thirty-four characters past the gate and roughly tripled with each two after that. A run of one repeated atom longer than that bound is now cut to it, which makes the clamp about gates rather than about brace syntax. A pattern that really does match everything, `^[\s\S]*$`, is the honest version of the same shape and is still accepted; the three-sweep cap is what keeps it from costing the whole alphabet on every rung.

  `tests/guards/caller_supplied_patterns_are_guarded.test.ts` now reads the whole argument expression at each `new RegExp` by balancing brackets, and judges the names inside it, rather than matching a bare name: of nine ways to spell the same exposure -- `new RegExp(opts.grep.trim())`, `new RegExp(String(opts.grep))`, `new RegExp(this.opts.grep)` and the rest -- the extractor that shipped saw one, and none of the other eight exist in the tree, which is why a guard that could not see them was indistinguishable from a guard with nothing to find. `grep`, `affected --filter` and the three document readers say why and stop; the `--grep` filters fall back to a literal substring match, which is what they already did for a pattern that would not compile. An ordinary pattern is unaffected -- the check costs microseconds on anything that is not super-linear. `tests/search_patterns_cannot_stall_the_process.test.ts` measures a wall clock rather than asserting a shape, so a guard that refuses the right patterns for the wrong reason still fails it, and `tests/guards/caller_supplied_patterns_are_guarded.test.ts` sweeps every `new RegExp` in the source for one that takes a caller's pattern without the guard, which is how the three document readers were found to have been missed.


- **The containment fold asked the platform whether the volume folds case, and the two can disagree.** `isInsideRoot` lowercases both paths before comparing them when it is running on Windows or macOS, which is right for a normal NTFS or HFS+ volume and wrong for the case-sensitive ones both platforms will hand you: Windows carries a per-directory flag that WSL sets on everything it creates, and macOS will format a case-sensitive APFS volume on request. On one of those, `<base>\repo` and `<base>\Repo` are two different directories with two different sets of files, and folding reads the second as the first -- so with the project root at `<base>\repo`, everything under a genuinely separate `<base>\Repo` was admitted as being inside the project. The fold is still applied, because dropping it would refuse legitimate reads (git and the shell disagree about a root's casing more or less at random), but it now only decides that a path is *not* inside the root. When the two spellings differ, the prefix is resolved through the filesystem and admitted only if the disk agrees they are the same directory.

  Where the disk cannot be asked, the answer is now *no*. Two cases could not be asked, and both were admitting. A network share cannot be resolved at all here: doing so opens an SMB connection to a host named in the path, and this runs inside the hook gate, before the user has approved the tool call -- the same 21.0-second dial the link walk already refuses to make. A case-sensitive SMB export really does keep `\\host\share\repo` and `\\host\share\Repo` apart, so admitting the second on the platform's say-so was the same disclosure, arriving where nothing could check it. And a spelling of the root that is not on disk while the root itself is means the volume does not fold: on one that did, the two names would have resolved to one directory. Admitting that let a create put a new directory beside the project rather than inside it, and an unreadable directory -- `EACCES` -- says nothing at all, which is the "unreadable means inside" mistake the containment walk had removed from it earlier in this release. A root that is *itself* absent still keeps the platform's answer: a project directory that has not been created yet has nothing to compare and no second directory to be let into.

  Two leading slashes is not the question, either. `\\?\C:\work` is spelled like a share and is not one -- it is this volume, one local resolve away -- so drive-letter and volume-GUID device paths are still resolved normally, and only `\\host\share` and its `\\?\UNC\host\share` spelling are refused. `tests/containment_folds_only_where_the_volume_folds.test.ts` asks the volume it is running on for a case-sensitive directory (on Windows it asks for one explicitly) and refuses to draw a conclusion from a volume that cannot hold both names. It also calls the same-directory check directly for the share case, because no CI runner has a file server and an unreachable host is refused several seconds earlier by the link walk -- left to the containment path alone, the branch would have been exercised on one of three platforms and taken on faith by the other two.

  What this closes is the spelling, and it is worth saying what it therefore does not close, because "the gate cannot dial" would be the wrong thing to read into it. A drive letter mapped to a share -- `Z:` bound to `\\host\share`, or a junction pointing at one -- reads as a local path and the walk will still contact that server, and a root that is itself on a share is resolved because the target is allowed to be there. The distinction is not cosmetic: a mapped drive reaches a host the person at the keyboard already mounted, while `\\host\share` in a tool call reaches any host the model can name, which is the case this closes. Telling a mapped letter from a local one without asking the network needs a Windows call Node does not expose, so it is stated here rather than claimed away.

- **A repository could still choose what a USER-scope VS Code install read, because confinement was keyed on the scope of the run rather than on where the path came from.** The guard above is a no-op in user scope, and for everything that install *writes* that is right: every one of those files is under the home directory. But the run also consults `<cwd>/.vscode/mcp.json`, to refuse registering token-goat in both scopes at once, and `token-goat mcp-status` reads the same path -- which is the command someone runs inside a repository they have just cloned. Checked in as a link, that path decided what both of them opened: the same private file, or the same host on the network, the write-side refusal exists for. Neither one discloses the contents, so what a repository gets is an oracle and a way to make an install fail, but the file was read and on Windows the host was contacted. Both now decline to look at a workspace path that resolves outside the workspace, and decline by answering "nothing installed there" rather than by throwing -- the question is whether the other scope already has it, a link out of the project is not an answer to that, and throwing would hand any repository a way to block a user-scope install. `mcp-status` leaves such a path out of the list of what it checked, because that list is what it checked. Nothing to reindex.

- **A pattern the Apex indexer built out of the file it was indexing grew with that file.** To spot a constructor written without an access modifier, `src/languages/apex.ts` interpolated an alternation of every type name the file declared into one regular expression, so a file declaring 6,000 types produced an alternation with 6,000 branches. Nothing about that shape is catastrophic in the usual sense -- there is no nested quantifier and a shape check reports it clean -- but the arity itself is the cost: a replica of the pattern measured 0.78 seconds against 2,000 names, 4.87 against 4,000 and 17.3 against 6,000, an exponent near three, and the 2 MB default of `indexing.large_file_skip_kb` admits roughly 25,000. `indexFileSync` runs inside the worker's drain loop, which has no per-file deadline, so a file like that costs its seconds to every file queued behind it. The pass now captures the identifier and looks it up in the set of declared names, folding case because Apex does; the same file costs 21 ms at 4,000 names.

  Two more passes over the same file were quadratic for unrelated reasons and are fixed with it. Deciding whether a symbol overlapped one already emitted walked every symbol found so far, which is a bitmap now. And a block whose closing brace was never found rescanned to end of file, once per unclosed brace, which is a single-pass brace map cached against the file's own text -- the reason a first attempt at the fix above still measured 1,414 ms, and the only reason the third defect was found at all.

  `src/languages/salesforce_metadata.ts` interpolated the root element's name into the closing-tag pattern without escaping it, and an XML name may legally contain a `.`, which is a wildcard there. `<Custom.Object>` therefore accepted `</CustomXObject>` as its own closing tag, and a document that is not well formed passed the adapter's only test that the file is what its name claims. `tests/indexer_patterns_cannot_stall_the_worker.test.ts` covers all four. It is a separate file from the search-pattern guard on purpose: that guard flags a `new RegExp` by the *name* of its argument, against names that mean "this came from a `--grep` or a `--filter`", and the language adapters interpolate under names like `namesAlt` and `root`, so a green run of it had never been evidence about `src/languages/` at all. Run `token-goat index` to pick up the corrected rows: this changes what an Apex or Salesforce metadata file's index rows contain, so it reindexes.

- **The pre-approval sweep that proves no hook touches an untrusted path excluded the one tool that parses paths out of a command.** A `PreToolUse` hook fires before the user is asked to approve the call, so the path in it is the model's choice until then, and on Windows a `stat` of `\\host\share\...` opens an SMB session -- an authentication attempt to a host a repository can name -- that approving nothing afterwards undoes. The registry sweep that proves this cannot happen was built by filtering the tool list, and the filter dropped the terminal tool on the reasoning that it carries a command rather than a path. It carries about twenty paths, extracted from the command, and two of them were measured on disk.

  Reaching the measurement needed one more thing, and it was there: the check for "is this a scratch file in a temp directory", which decides whether a size is worth taking, tested for `AppData/Local/Temp/` anywhere in the string. A share path ending in those segments satisfied it. That clause is anchored to a local drive root now, and the two sites ask the same gate every other pre-approval hook asks before their first filesystem call, so a network or device path -- and, in the editor, a path outside the workspace -- is declined rather than measured. Declining costs nothing: the size only decides whether to print a hint, and not printing one is what an unmeasurable file already got. The recall of a previously downloaded file is gated the same way, since it stats a path recalled from the session store.

  The sweep no longer builds its population by name. A guard whose coverage is decided by a filter is only as good as the filter, and this one narrowed itself in a line nothing else pointed at.

- **The same pre-approval gate read the spelling of a path, and a link makes the spelling a poor witness.** `\\host\share\x` is refused everywhere, but a repository can check in an ordinary-looking directory as a link to that share, and then a path written entirely in local characters -- `<repo>/vendor/notes.md` -- stats onto the network anyway. In the editor this was already closed, because workspace containment resolves links before it answers; on every other harness the gate stopped at the spelling and let it through, and nothing about who gets dialled depends on which editor is running.

  The link walk containment already uses is the answer on those harnesses now. It is network-free by construction: each segment is `lstat`-ed, which does not follow, and a link's own bytes are read with `readlink`, which is local -- so a link pointing at a share is recognised from the reparse point, before anything dials. A chain it cannot see to the end of -- an unreadable ancestor, too many hops -- is refused rather than allowed, because "could not see" is not "safe".

  It runs after the editor's own containment rather than before it, which is a correctness point rather than an ordering preference: the walk `lstat`s each segment, and a path the editor has already refused for sitting outside the workspace must not be touched at all. Each harness reaches exactly one of the two, and neither loses a case.

- **A link whose target named a drive letter with nothing after it certified the project as containing the place it led to.** `Z:elsewhere` is neither absolute nor relative to the link's own directory: Windows resolves it against drive Z's own current directory, which is per-process state no Node API exposes. The containment walk read it as a segment literally *named* `Z:elsewhere` and appended it to the directory it was standing on, so the answer stayed under the project root -- and a link leading out of the project was the evidence that it did not. Every guard keyed on containment inherited that: the installer write helpers, the MCP tool confinement, and the pre-approval hook gate.

  There is no honest resolution available, so there is no resolution: the walk now answers "cannot resolve", which every caller already fails closed on. The half the existing tests could not catch is that this was not a refusal touching the wrong thing -- it was the verdict coming out the wrong way round.

- **A 160 KB PDF could take the process down, and take the background indexer down over and over.** A content stream is compressed, so how much text a PDF yields is a number its file size does not carry: the measured fixture is 160 KB on disk and 35 million characters of text, and the same trick at 2 MB reaches gigabytes. Nothing bounded it. `pdf-extract`, `pdf-locate` and the semantic indexer all built the whole thing in memory and died on the way -- and the indexer opens every PDF in a tree it has just been pointed at, without being asked and with the result discarded, so the worker crash-looped on a file nobody had chosen to read.

  Extraction now streams a page at a time and refuses at 8 MB of text, which is more than any model's context takes and far less than the process can survive; a file over 50 MB is refused before it is opened at all. The ZIP-based formats have been bounded this way since the beginning -- this brings PDFs up to the same footing.

  A second review pass found three ways past that budget. It counted UTF-16 code units, so accented or CJK text encoded to two or three times the limit it had just cleared; the finished document is now measured in the bytes the limit is stated in. It counted only characters, so a page of a million one-character items sat well inside 8 MB while costing hundreds of megabytes to hold; a page is now bounded by its item count as well. And refusing frees the memory without stopping pdfjs, which cannot be cancelled part-way through a page, so a crafted file kept the extractor working long after it had been refused. Reading one document's text now stops after a minute whatever it is doing.

  Later passes found that a bound is only worth what its enforcement is worth. The clock was read once per chunk of text, so a page carrying nothing but graphics operators -- parsed in full, yielding no text at all -- never reached it; the wait for the next chunk is what carries the clock now, and walking to a further page checks it too. Refusing on the clock also left the document's own teardown waiting on a read that would never finish, which turned the refusal into a hang; teardown is now bounded as well. `--layout` reassembled a page by comparing every text item against every row already found, which is fine for a page and quadratic for a crafted one; it now goes to the rows at the right height directly. `pdf-outline` was bounded in depth but not in width, so a flat tree of bookmarks was one page lookup and one attacker-written title per sibling, for as many siblings as the file cared to carry; the number of entries and the length of each title are both capped. `pdf-locate` sized each snippet around the match but never bounded the match, so a pattern like `[\s\S]+` returned whole pages under a `--context` of 80; `--max-matches` and `--context` are both capped and the snippet is now the width the flag promises.

  The line above about a refusal being recorded as settled was true only of PDFs. `docx-text`, `pptx-outline` and the spreadsheet commands raise their own size refusals, and the indexer read those as a bad moment rather than a verdict, so an over-budget archive paid its whole decompression again on every pass. All four formats now share one refusal type, which is what the indexer asks about.

  A PDF is also no longer read through a name that can change between the size check and the read. The file is opened once, and its type, its size and its bytes all come off that one descriptor. A file swapped for a link to an endless device, or one still growing while it is read, is refused rather than followed. Anything that is not a regular file is refused outright, because a FIFO or a character device reports a size of zero and zero passes any ceiling.

- **A PDF the indexer failed to read once was never read again.** The background indexer treats a document with no extractable text as settled: it clears the file's embeddings and records it as done, so an unchanged file is not re-read on every drain. A failure took the same path. If pdfjs was briefly unavailable, or the file was being written at that moment, that PDF was retired permanently, and fixing the cause changed nothing because the indexer had stopped looking. A refusal is still recorded as settled, since the same bytes are past the same bound on every run. A failure now leaves the file alone and the next run picks it up. Changed in [src/parser.ts](src/parser.ts) and [src/doc_embed_extract.ts](src/doc_embed_extract.ts).

- **The VS Code extension sent your selection to chat unfenced, and could stall the editor scrubbing it.** Before a selection reaches Copilot Chat the extension removes emails, phone numbers, ID numbers and card numbers from it, then wraps it in a fence so the model can see where your text ends and the extension's own sentence begins. Neither held all the way. The email pattern let one part of a domain match the separator after it, so a long run of characters that never completes an address was retried from every position: 32,000 characters of it cost 1.35 seconds on the thread that draws the editor, and twice the input cost four times the time. The pattern is now bounded at the real addressing limits, and the same input costs 8 milliseconds. And when the decoder was not set up yet, which is where every user starts, the selection was returned with no fence at all, so it ran straight into the surrounding prose in both directions. Both branches are fenced now, and the note saying what was removed is shown on both.

### Documentation

- **Two counts in the README were selling the tool short, and the guard on one of them could not tell.** The banner claimed 180+ filter and interception rules against a real 210, and the compression section claimed 130+ dev tool CLIs against 157 filters covering 260 binary names. `tests/guards/filter_count_readme_sync.test.ts` only ever asked whether the advertised floor exceeded reality, and a floor that is too low is true, so 30 rules' worth of drift passed every run. The numbers now read 205+ and 150+, and each claim is held inside a band: at or under the real count, and no more than fifteen below it. Fifteen is the slack a floor needs to survive retiring a filter without failing the build on the same commit, and it is small enough that a year of additions cannot hide inside it. The CLI claim had no guard at all before this. Nothing to reindex.

- **Eighteen links on the published architecture page pointed at files that are not in the tree.** `docs/architecture-qa.html` cites its evidence by full GitHub URL rather than by relative path, and `tests/guards/doc_links_resolve.test.ts` reads `.md` files only and skips anything starting `https:`, so both halves of that shape were outside what it checked. Three links named `docs/legal.md`, which has never existed here, and fifteen named five modules under names they no longer carry (`src/doctor.ts`, `src/redact.ts`, `src/compress.ts`, `src/mcp/server.ts`, and the command matrix before it was sharded). Each now points at where the behaviour its claim describes actually lives, and the guard gained a second pass over `blob/` URLs in `.md` and `.html` alike, scoped to this repository so a link into someone else's tree is not judged against this checkout. A citation on the same page pointed at README lines 663-667, which the License section left when the file grew, and now points at 704-714.
