That one the guard cannot cover and does not claim to: a range on a 715-line file resolves to five real lines whatever has since moved into them, which makes it a content question rather than a link question. Nothing to reindex.

- **The install guide now says plainly why there is no Windsurf agent-hook integration.** Windsurf's Cascade agent hooks (`cascadeHooksJson`) are pushed from Windsurf's own servers per team, not read from a file on disk -- confirmed by tracing every reference to that field across the installed Windsurf 1.110.1 bundle, where it only ever appears as a protobuf field or a class-constructor default alongside other server-pushed team settings. There is no local hook file for token-goat to install, so Windsurf keeps its existing terminal-output compression only, and a guard test (`tests/guards/windsurf_never_writes_cascade_hooks.test.ts`) now fails the build if a future change tries to write one anyway. Nothing to reindex.

- **The install guide now says plainly that JetBrains IDEs (WebStorm, IntelliJ, PyCharm, Rider, PhpStorm) have no integration today: no hooks, no MCP, no terminal filter.** Junie, the JetBrains ACP agent, documents `.junie/AGENTS.md` for guidelines and `~/.junie/mcp/mcp.json` for MCP servers, and a secondary source describes `~/.junie/config.json` for hooks -- but none of this was confirmed against a live Junie run: no `~/.junie` directory exists anywhere on the machine this was checked from, so the on-disk shape of any of those files is unverified. A guard test (`tests/guards/jetbrains_never_writes_junie_config.test.ts`) now fails the build if a future change writes one of those paths anyway without fresh live evidence. Separately, GitHub documents its Copilot-for-JetBrains plugin previewing agent hooks in `.github/hooks/` -- the same folder `--copilot` and `--vscode` already write -- which would mean a project already gets some pickup for free with no JetBrains-specific code, but the real plugin is not installed here (only its theme jar is present) so this is noted as unconfirmed, not relied upon. No `BRIDGE_CAPABILITY_MATRIX` row was added, matching the Windsurf precedent above: that matrix tracks hook-event parity for a real install writer, and JetBrains has none. Nothing to reindex.

### Changed

- **`token-goat install --vscode` now installs into the current project instead of user scope, because a user-scope install was silently doing nothing outside the first folder of a multi-root workspace.** VS Code resolves an agent hook's working directory from the hook *file's* own location -- the workspace folder containing it, or the workspace's first folder when no folder contains it. A user-scope hooks file lives in `~/.copilot/hooks/`, which is inside no workspace folder, so every invocation ran with `folders[0]` as its working directory; since token-goat confines every pre-approval hook to that directory, read hints, image shrinking and edit interception did nothing at all for every other folder of a multi-root workspace, with no error and no output to notice. Captured live against VS Code 1.137.0 in a two-root workspace: the user-scope copy reported root A as its `cwd` for an event raised in root B, while a project-scope copy in root B reported root B. **If you already have the user-scope install, just run `token-goat install --vscode` again in each project you want it in** -- the first run detects the old install and moves it, printing a note saying so, rather than leaving both in place. That matters because VS Code runs *every* hooks file it discovers, in both scopes at once (also captured: two invocations of every event inside one session id), so an unmigrated machine would fire every hook twice. `--user` keeps the old behavior deliberately, with a printed note naming the multi-root limitation it opts into; `-p`/`--project` is still accepted and now selects the default. `token-goat doctor` gained a check that reports a user-scope install still sitting there, and a stronger one for a machine that has both. Passing `-p` and `--user` together is an error rather than one silently winning. Where two copies of one hook do still reach token-goat -- a pathless event such as session start, or a terminal command, neither of which carries a path the workspace confinement can decide on -- exactly one is elected by an exclusive-create marker keyed on `(session id, event, the payload's own timestamp)`, under token-goat's data directory and never the repository or the working directory, pruned after an hour. Every part of that election fails open: if the marker cannot be written the hook runs, because a duplicate hook is recoverable and a silenced one is not. Nothing to reindex.

- **A default install is smaller: it no longer downloads `sharp`, or the libvips image library that comes with it.** Image shrinking is built into token-goat and needs no extra package, so `sharp` was being installed and never loaded. A default install goes from 70 packages to 62, and an install without optional packages is unchanged at 2. `sharp` is now a development dependency, where the tests use it as a second opinion on token-goat's own image output. Every copyleft license in an installed copy came in with libvips and is gone with it, leaving six packages that need a human answer instead of twenty: see [SECURITY.md](SECURITY.md#dependency-licenses). Nothing needs reindexing.

- **Hooks start faster.** The language adapters that read symbols out of Fortran, COBOL, Pascal, PowerShell and the other 50-odd file types without a tree-sitter grammar now load only where files are indexed. A hook running in process, which is how the installed shim handles nearly every tool call, compiles 2.170 MB across 8 chunks and never loads the 131 KB adapter chunk at all. Hosts that shell out to `token-goat hook <event>` still compile the adapters, because token-goat's own read commands call them. Nothing about what gets indexed changes, but the first `token-goat index` after this version reparses every file once, because the extraction code moved.

### Fixed

- **A clone of this repository on Windows arrived with CRLF line endings, which broke checks that compare a generated file to the committed one.** `.gitattributes` normalized line endings in git's own storage but left the working copy to each machine's git configuration, and the Windows default converts them. Anyone who cloned the repository on Windows with that default therefore got files whose bytes did not match what the generators here produce, and the tooling that checks the two agree could not pass no matter how correct the content was. The working copy is now pinned to one form on every platform, so a fresh clone matches on Windows, Linux and macOS alike. This affects contributors to this repository only; nothing in the published package changes.
- **`token-goat install --vscode` (and every other single-harness scope flag) silently also ran the base Claude Code install -- fixed.** A `--vscode`-only (or `--codex`-only, `--gemini`-only, etc.) install or uninstall used to unconditionally touch three files no single-harness integration needs: it rewrote `~/.claude/settings.json` (adding token-goat's hook entries), appended a delimited block to the user's own hand-maintained `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md`, and created `~/.claude/skills/token-goat/SKILL.md`. If you ran `install` with any single harness flag before this fix, check `~/.claude/settings.json` and `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` for token-goat content you did not ask for (a `<!-- token-goat-begin -->` … `<!-- token-goat-end -->` block in `CLAUDE.md`, or a `token-goat` hook command in `settings.json`'s `hooks` section) and `~/.claude/skills/token-goat/` for a directory you did not expect; `token-goat uninstall` (no flag) removes all three cleanly. A plain `install`/`uninstall` with no harness flag, or `--hermes`, still installs the Claude Code base as before; every other harness flag now touches only its own files. A guard test (`tests/guards/scoped_install_never_writes_claude_code_base.test.ts`) fails the build if a scoped harness branch calls a Claude-Code-base install/uninstall function again, and a behavioral test (`tests/install_vscode_never_touches_claude_code_home.test.ts`) runs the real built binary against a disposable home directory and checks the literal file list. Nothing to reindex.
- **The installer could rewrite an existing file with no recovery copy -- fixed for every file it writes.** Before this fix, `writeJsonSettings` (used for `~/.claude/settings.json` and several harnesses' JSON configs) was the only writer that backed up the file it was about to overwrite. Nothing else did: an `upsertDelimitedBlock`/`stripDelimitedBlock` rewrite of `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` or any harness's `AGENTS.md`/instructions file, `~/.claude/skills/token-goat/SKILL.md`, and the JSON merge-writers for VS Code's, Visual Studio's, and Cursor's `mcp.json` and Zed's `settings.json` all overwrote their target with zero recovery copy. Zed's `settings.json` in particular is not a token-goat-exclusive file -- it can hold a user's entire editor configuration (theme, keybindings, vim mode, every other extension's own settings) alongside the one `context_servers.token-goat` entry token-goat manages, so a bad merge there was not a one-line loss. Every one of those writers now writes a `<file>.bak.<ISO-timestamp>` sibling right next to the file, byte-for-byte before its own overwrite, capped at 5 backups per file and cleaned up again on a full `token-goat uninstall`. If you ran an affected install before this fix and still have the original file's content elsewhere (git, an editor's local history, a real filesystem backup), there is no `.bak` from token-goat to recover from for that earlier run -- only installs from this version onward are covered. A new guard test (`tests/guards/installer_writes_are_always_backed_up.test.ts`) enumerates every installer write site in `src/install.ts` and `src/bridges/*_install.ts` and fails the build if a new one is added without going through the backup path or an explicitly named, justified exemption (a purely generated shim/plugin script regenerated identically on every install, or token-goat's own internal bookkeeping ledger). A behavioral test (`tests/install_backs_up_preexisting_config_before_overwrite.test.ts`) runs the real built binary against a hand-authored `mcp.json`/`settings.json` with known content and checks the backup's bytes match the original exactly. Nothing to reindex.
- **Emacs Lisp no longer loses the rest of a line to a plain character-literal escape.** A basic escape naming a character outside alnum/`^`/`-` (for example `?\"`, `?\;`, `?\(`) left that one escaped character unmasked, so a following real string quote (or a definition on the same line) could be misread through it -- `?\"` in particular let the next `"` open a real string span, silently swallowing everything after it, including any `defun` on that line. A lone `\<c>` now always consumes exactly the one character it names, per Emacs Lisp's Basic Char Syntax. Run `token-goat index` (or let the background worker catch up) to pick up the fix: this changes what an `.el` file's index rows contain for any file with this pattern, so it reindexes.

- **Retrying a `read`/`brief` command with the exact qualified name token-goat suggested for an ambiguous symbol could still report the same ambiguity, with the same suggestion, forever.** When two same-named symbols sit at different places in one file and one of them is enclosed by the other (for example, an HTML file with a `<h2>Overview</h2>` heading and an unrelated element `id="Overview"` that falls inside that heading's section), token-goat's ambiguity message qualified the inner one as `Overview.Overview` -- using the enclosing heading's own name as the qualifier. Retrying with that exact `Overview.Overview` spec fed it back into the same lookup, which mistakenly treated the outer heading as containing itself and kept both candidates in play, so the retry it just suggested resolved nothing new. The lookup now only counts a symbol as "inside" a container when it is a different symbol from it, so this qualifier -- and every other qualified retry built the same way -- picks out exactly the one candidate it names. No reindex is needed.

- **A LaTeX `.cls` file is no longer reported as Salesforce Apex.** `.cls` is shared by Apex, VB6 class modules, and LaTeX document/package class files; the check that told Apex from VB6 and OpenEdge ABL never checked for LaTeX at all, so any `.cls` that was not VB6 or ABL fell through to the Apex default. A LaTeX `.cls` (one that starts with `\ProvidesClass{...}` or `\documentclass{...}`) is now recognized and reported as an unsupported file type instead, rather than misclaimed as Apex. There is no LaTeX symbol extractor yet, so a LaTeX `.cls` file's symbols are no longer indexed at all -- this changes what `token-goat index` does with any `.cls` file that used to be misindexed as Apex, so it reindexes any project that has one.

- **`recall` no longer points you at a cached command output that is already gone.** Each `bash-output`/`web-output`/`mcp-output` entry `recall` finds used to always print the exact command to fetch its full body, even after that body's on-disk copy had already been cleaned up (by age, or by the normal count/size eviction every cache uses). Following that pointer returned nothing, with no warning it would. `recall` now checks whether the file is actually still there before printing the pointer, and says "blob expired" instead when it isn't. The index also no longer keeps a `recall` entry around forever once its blob is gone: entries expire on the same 24-hour clock the blob cache already uses, so the two can no longer drift apart. No reindex is needed.

- **`token-goat stats`'s own history table is now bounded instead of growing forever.** Every recorded saving event stayed in the database indefinitely; on one long-running machine this reached over 400,000 rows. Events older than 180 days are now rolled up into daily totals (by day, source, and harness -- the same breakdown `stats` already reports) and the individual rows removed, so long-window and all-time totals stay correct while the table stops growing without bound. No reindex is needed.

- **A handful of bundled third-party JavaScript files no longer pollute `refs` with a compiler-inserted call that names no real symbol.** esbuild inserts a `__name(fn, "name")` wrapper call around named functions in some build targets; indexing that call as a reference filled `refs` with call-sites for a name nothing in any project actually defines. Only this exact, unambiguous compiler artifact is excluded -- generic test-framework identifiers like `expect`/`it`/`describe` are deliberately left alone, since a broader exclusion list risks quietly hiding a real symbol some other query relies on. Run `token-goat index` to reparse: `__name` references recorded by a prior index are not retroactively removed, only future extractions no longer record new ones.

- **`refs`, `ask`, `semantic`, and `trace --bodies` now warn and self-heal when they answer from a file that changed on disk, the same way `symbol`/`read`/`skeleton`/`outline` already did.** Those four single-file commands already caught a stale row (index says one thing, the file on disk says another) and reindexed it on the spot before answering. `refs`, `ask`, and `semantic` answer from however many distinct files their query happens to match, and `trace --bodies` resolves each traceback frame's file the same way `symbol`/`read` resolve a spec's file -- none of them ran that check at all, so editing a file outside the edit hook (a manual save, an external tool, a merge) left them silently serving pre-edit content with no warning and no self-heal. All four now run the same check across every file they are about to answer from, printing one summary warning when any of them turn out stale. No reindex is needed; a stale hit corrects itself in place the moment one of these commands notices it.

- **A dead background worker is now revived by whatever queues work for it, not only by an editor's post-edit hook.** If the background worker had crashed, been killed by hand, or not survived a machine sleep/wake, only the Claude Code/Codex post-edit hook noticed and restarted it. Every other way a file gets queued for reindexing -- a Bash-command hook rewriting a file, `token-goat replace`/`write-file`, and the self-heal that runs when a stale read is served -- appended to the dirty queue and reported success without ever checking whether anything was running to drain it, so the queue could sit untouched indefinitely with no warning. The auto-revive check now lives at the one place every one of those paths already shares (the function that appends to the queue), so all of them nudge a dead worker back to life the same way the edit hook always did. No reindex is needed.

- **Stopping the background worker no longer silently drops an embedding that was still being computed.** `token-goat worker stop`, and a daemon restart that replaces an old one mid-batch, send the running daemon SIGTERM. The daemon used to exit immediately on that signal regardless of any embedding call still in progress, so that file's vectors never got written -- `symbol`/`read` stayed correct (parse rows commit before embedding starts), but `semantic` silently missed that file's content until something re-touched it. The daemon now waits (up to 5 seconds) for in-flight embeddings to finish before exiting, so a stop or restart during active indexing no longer drops that work; the wait is capped so a genuinely stuck embedding call can never block a replacement daemon from starting. No reindex is needed.

- **Reparsing a file no longer forces its embedding to be redone from scratch when the content didn't change.** Every reparse (a parser-fingerprint bump, a touched mtime, `--force-refresh`) rewrote the file's row without its `embed_sha`, so the next embedding pass always saw "no embedding on record" and recomputed one for content that already had a correct, unchanged embedding. The row write now carries the prior `embed_sha` forward whenever the new content hash matches the old one, and still resets it when the content actually changed, so re-embedding only happens for content that is actually new. No reindex is needed; a `reindex --force-refresh` you already have queued will now skip re-embedding files whose content it finds unchanged, where it previously redid them anyway.

- **`reconcile` now finds a file that a parser upgrade left stale, not only a file whose content changed.** The drift sweep behind `token-goat reconcile` compared a file's bytes on disk against the row's stored hash, and skipped straight to the next file the moment neither had moved. That misses the case a parser fix or a new parser version creates: a file nobody edited afterward still holds symbol and reference rows written by the old extractor. The per-file checks in the indexer and worker already knew to compare a row's `parser_sha` against the current parser fingerprint; `reconcile`'s own sweep never did, so a file only self-repaired if something happened to touch it later. `reconcile` now checks both, and reports how many files it found this way (`--dry-run` says "N files ... were unchanged on disk but indexed by an older parser"). Run `token-goat reconcile` to reindex anything a past parser change left behind; no output changes for a project already reconciled since its last parser upgrade.

- **A secret sitting near a length cutoff could survive as an unrecognizable fragment on four different surfaces, because the cutoff ran before redaction instead of after it.** Redaction requires a minimum match length; cutting a credential short first can leave too little of it for the pattern to recognize, so the leftover fragment shipped raw even though the surface "went through redaction" downstream. This hit the short preview token-goat builds for a cached MCP call's arguments (truncated to 120 characters before redaction ran), the shared tool-output pipeline every Bash filter uses (several length caps ran ahead of the redaction pass), and the compressed summary an MCP result is folded into (compression ran on the raw result text instead of the already-redacted one). A fourth, related gap let a raw agent-spawn prompt persist to disk unredacted entirely — the session field that tracks prompts for spawned agents never redacted at all, not merely too late. And a fifth, differently-shaped bug on the same tool-output path: the pre-hook that rewrites a Bash command into a compression call correctly redacts its result, but discarded that redacted text and fell back to the raw, unredacted original whenever compression didn't save enough bytes to clear its own worth-applying floor — a gate meant to decide whether *compression* was worth it was also discarding *redaction*, which it should never do. All five now redact ahead of any cutoff or compression step, or redact where none existed before. Two new local checks make this specific mistake shape hard to reintroduce: every length-shortening helper in the codebase must now be explicitly recorded as either safe or unsafe for secret-bearing text, and every field of the saved session state must be explicitly recorded as covered by redaction, with an unrecorded new one failing the check by default. Changed in [src/mcp_cache.ts](src/mcp_cache.ts), [src/tool_filters/base.ts](src/tool_filters/base.ts), [src/tool_filters/dispatch.ts](src/tool_filters/dispatch.ts), [src/hooks_mcp.ts](src/hooks_mcp.ts), and [src/session.ts](src/session.ts). No reindex is needed.

- **Pruning old config backups no longer risks deleting a file you made yourself.** Once a config file has more than 5 backups, the oldest ones are removed to keep the pile from growing forever. That prune used to pick candidates by matching `<name>.bak.*` against a directory listing, which is exactly the kind of name a user's own copy of the file could carry. It now consults the same record of what token-goat actually created that uninstall already uses, so a look-alike file you wrote by hand is never a candidate. No reindex is needed.

- **A stale build file no longer ships inside the published package.** `dist/_m.mjs`, left over from a build-output name retired earlier, was riding along in every release because `dist/` ships as a whole directory. The build now clears any leftover file in `dist/` it did not just write, the same way it already cleared old chunk files, so a renamed or retired build output cannot pile up again. No reindex is needed.

- **[SECURITY.md](SECURITY.md#dependency-advisories) no longer claims a clean `npm audit` for this repository when it isn't one.** Two moderate advisories now sit in development-only transitive dependencies (`adm-zip`, `hono`); neither reaches an installed copy of token-goat, which is what the other two rows in that table already measure. The page now says so, and names the packages, instead of rounding a real finding to "clean." No reindex is needed.

- **Uninstalling now takes the backup copies token-goat made of your config files with it.** Whenever an install rewrites a config, it first copies the old one to `<name>.bak.<timestamp>`. Those copies were never removed, so a machine that had installed a few times kept a copy of every config token-goat had ever touched, each holding whatever was in that file at the time, in a folder you had been told the product was gone from. Uninstall now deletes the ones it made, and only those: a backup counts as token-goat's only if token-goat recorded creating it, so a `.bak` file you made yourself stays put even when its name looks exactly the same. No reindex is needed.

- **The demo preview image loads everywhere again.** `demo/screenshots/approval-demo.webp` was a PNG carrying a `.webp` name, which some viewers refuse to render and others only render by guessing. It is now `approval-demo.png`, and the link in [the demo README](demo/README.md) points at the new name. Three images in `assets/` that nothing linked to are gone as well, so the published site no longer carries them. No reindex is needed.

- **Groovy slashy strings, Elixir sigils, and Scala symbol literals now read as the strings they are.** Each one used to look like code, or like a string that never ended, which moved or dropped the symbols around it:
  - Groovy `/.../` and `$/.../$` strings are read as strings, so a brace, an apostrophe, a `//` or a `/*` inside one no longer opens a block, a string, or a comment. Groovy tells a slashy string from a division by where the `/` sits: after an operator, a comma, an opening bracket, or a keyword such as `return`, it starts a string; after a value, it divides. `pack --strip-comments` follows the same rule, where before it could cut a line short at a `//` inside one of these strings.
  - Elixir sigils such as `~w(...)` and `~r/.../` can span lines, including the bracket forms that nest.
  - Scala `'symbol` literals are no longer read as the start of a character literal, so the rest of the line still gets indexed.

  Run `token-goat index` to reparse: the symbols and line numbers recorded for Groovy, Elixir, and Scala files change.

- **`xlsx-range` turns down a range bigger than it can scan instead of trying to read it.** Asking for `A1:XFD1048576` used to walk 17 billion cells and run out of memory. The ceiling is the same 20 million cell limit the other sheet reads already use, counted here from the range you ask for rather than the size the file declares. When a sheet is too large to scan, the message now suggests a smaller `--range` instead of pointing at a read with no limit of its own.

- **Six languages now index files they used to read wrongly or skip.** Upgrading reindexes your projects once.
  - **Fortran:** a file written in free form but saved with a fixed-form extension, such as `.f` or `.for`, indexed nothing at all. token-goat now decides the form from what is in the file rather than from its extension alone.
  - **COBOL:** a copybook of well over a hundred thousand lines failed to index instead of returning its symbols.
  - **Perl:** text inside a string that happened to look like a heredoc marker ended the scan early, so every symbol after it was lost.
  - **PHP:** functions written in the HTML outside the `<?php` tags were indexed as PHP, inventing symbols that are not code. Only the code between the tags is read now.
  - **SQL:** a string holding a backslash followed by an apostrophe ran the scan past the end of the string, dropping every symbol after it.
  - **Visual Basic:** a file with one very long line took time growing with the square of its length. A 50 KB line now scans about 45 times faster.

- **`token-goat uninstall` no longer deletes a config file it did not create.** `uninstall --visualstudio` removed `.mcp.json` whenever it was left holding nothing, which deleted a file you had written yourself once token-goat's own entry came out of it. token-goat now remembers which files it created and removes only those. `uninstall --vscode` follows the same rule, and no longer leaves an empty `mcp.json` behind when the file was token-goat's to remove.

- **token-goat now escapes text it quotes back from your files.** A heading, a sentence, or an MCP server name shaped like one of token-goat's own notices read as token-goat speaking when it appeared in a read hint, a fold notice, or `token-goat mcp-audit`. Such text is now written so it cannot be mistaken for token-goat's own words. What token-goat shows inside a fenced block is unchanged.

- **The same protection now covers the text quoted out of HTML and XML documents.** A saved web page is written by someone else, so `html-query` now returns the page's markup inside a marked block that says it is data and not instructions, and a page that contains that block's own closing marker can no longer end it early. `html-lint`, `html-outline`, and `xml-outline` quote a document's title, headings, element names, ids, and form fields inside lines token-goat speaks in its own voice, so a document shaped like one of token-goat's own notices is now written so it cannot be mistaken for them. `token-goat capabilities`, `bash-history`, `web-history`, `mcp-history`, and `bench` do the same for the entry names they list. What `--json` returns is unchanged, so anything reading it still parses the same values. This needs no reindex.

- **Command reports now escape text that comes from your project.** The reports print names and lines they read from your files: a package name and version from a lockfile, the text after a `TODO`, a config value, a CLAUDE.md heading, a file path, a branch name on a conflict marker, a function name from a coverage report. Text shaped like one of token-goat's own notices is now written so it cannot be mistaken for token-goat speaking. This covers `todo`, `lockdeps`, `config list` and `config get`, `doctor`, `memory`, `bootstrap-audit`, `context-stats`, `refs` and `callers`, `trace` and `logfold`, `conflicts`, `coverage-report-gaps`, `dep-docs`, `openapi-op`, `sqlite-schema`, `session-audit`, `reclaim-index`, and the `bash-history`, `web-history`, `mcp-history` and `cache-audit` listings. `token-goat blame` now puts the `git blame` output it prints inside a marked block, because the author names and commit messages in it come from the repository. The session manifest token-goat builds before a compact now escapes the file paths and web addresses it lists, for the same reason. The warning token-goat prints when a `config.toml` or a project `.token-goat.toml` does not parse now escapes the line of the file it quotes back: that warning can appear ahead of any command you run. The file names that `token-goat --purge` and `token-goat pack --scan-secrets` report on are escaped too. The file contents that `read`, `symbol` and `skeleton` return are unchanged, as is anything token-goat already shows inside a marked block. Nothing is reindexed.

- **A shrunk image is now written so only you can read it.** On macOS and Linux, the temporary copy token-goat writes for the pi and shrink-block bridges was readable by every account on the machine.

- **`token-goat doctor` is clearer, and drops one false warning.** When tree-sitter's core will not load, doctor now says that it only checked the grammar packages are installed, because whether each one loads cannot be tested while the core is down. It also no longer reports that Visual Studio registers token-goat twice when the user and project paths are two spellings of the same file, which happens when the project is your home folder.

- **A container listing no longer flags a healthy row whose name contains "failed" or "unknown".** An image named `unknown-service` or a container named `failed-jobs` was reported as a problem. Only the status words themselves count now.

- **Hook commands are now quoted correctly for PowerShell.** A path holding a `$` was expanded as a variable, and a path holding a single quote ended the quoted argument early, so the hook did not run.

- **`token-goat install --copilot` now reports the Visual Studio guidance it refreshed.** The install shares one instructions file with `install --visualstudio -p`, and a run that rewrote the Visual Studio block in it still said everything was already installed.

- **`token-goat index` now indexes and counts every file it finds.** Some files only get a language after token-goat reads their contents, such as an OpenEdge ABL `.p` or an Objective-C `.m`. The walk listed them, but the index step then skipped them. So `index . --walk` reported one file fewer than it found for each such file, and those files were only indexed the first time another command looked them up.

- **Search hints now fire for every source language token-goat indexes.** A search inside `.mjs`, `.cjs`, `.mts`, `.cts`, `.pyi`, `.kts`, `.hxx`, `.ps1`, `.psm1`, `.cls`, `.trigger`, `.sc`, `.lua`, `.ex`, `.exs`, `.dart`, `.zig` or `.r` files used to get no hint pointing at `symbol` or `read`, even though those files are indexed. They now get the same hint as the rest, and Clojure files, which are not indexed, no longer do.
- **`token-goat doctor` now says why tree-sitter did not load, and how to fix it.** It tells apart three cases: the `tree-sitter` package is not installed (reinstall with `npm install -g token-goat --include=optional`), it is installed but has no native build for your platform (npm 12 skips install scripts unless you allow them with `--allow-scripts=tree-sitter`), and its native binary will not load (reinstall under the Node version you run token-goat with). It also lists the languages affected, TypeScript, JavaScript, Python, Go, Rust, Ruby, Java, C and C++, which fall back to a rougher scan that finds fewer symbols and no references, says every other language indexes as usual, and names any grammar package that is missing. See [Troubleshooting](docs/install.md#troubleshooting).

- **`outline`, `skeleton`, and `read "file::Name"` now say when token-goat cannot read symbols from a file type.** Before, a file type token-goat has no reader for, such as Fortran or an extension it does not know, got "No indexed symbols found", which looked like an empty or out-of-date index. The message now says token-goat has no symbol extractor for that file type, that grep and plain reads still work, and how to ask for support: [open an issue](https://github.com/DFKHelper/token-goat/issues) or email token-goat@dfkhelper.com. When a TypeScript, Python, or other tree-sitter file shows no symbols because tree-sitter did not load, the message says so and points at `token-goat doctor`.

- **`token-goat mcp-audit` no longer reads a `.mcp.json` that holds only a `servers` list as one server named "servers".** Such a file, the format Visual Studio uses, now reads as having no Claude Code servers.

- **`token-goat install --vscode` no longer edits the project you run it from.** A user-scope install wrote its routing block into `.github/copilot-instructions.md` in whatever folder you were in. It now writes `~/.copilot/instructions/token-goat.instructions.md`, a personal instructions file VS Code applies in every workspace, and `uninstall --vscode` removes it. With `-p`, the block still goes to the project's `.github/copilot-instructions.md`. If an earlier install left a block between `<!-- token-goat-vscode-begin -->` and `<!-- token-goat-vscode-end -->` in some project's `.github/copilot-instructions.md`, delete it by hand. Run `token-goat install --vscode` again to write the new file. No reindex needed.

- **`token-goat stats` no longer counts an image saving the model never got.** In VS Code, Copilot CLI, opencode, pi, and OpenClaw, a shrunk image reaches the model only as a temporary file that the tool is pointed at instead of the original. token-goat recorded the saving before that file was written, so if the write failed, the model read the full-size original and `stats` still showed the saving. In VS Code, token-goat now writes the file itself and records the saving only once the file exists. In Copilot CLI, opencode, pi, and OpenClaw, the file is written by the harness's own plugin after token-goat has already answered, so token-goat cannot see whether it worked. Those harnesses now record no image saving at all. The image is still shrunk and delivered the same way. opencode and OpenClaw are recognized only when they set their session variable, so a session without it is still counted the old way. Also on these five, a screenshot that is mostly text now gets the shrunk image. Before, the text-reading step took over, but none of these harnesses can swap an image for text, so the full-size original was loaded instead. Changed in [src/image_shrink.ts](src/image_shrink.ts) and [src/bridges/vscode_hooks.ts](src/bridges/vscode_hooks.ts). No reindex and no `install` re-run needed.

- **More declaration forms now index in Elixir, Zig, Terraform, SQL, PowerShell, and R.** Each of these adapters matches declarations with patterns rather than a grammar, and each was missing forms the language documents. They now index, and what they index as:
  - **Elixir:** `defmacrop`, `defguard`, `defguardp`, and `defdelegate` index as functions under their module. `defimpl Size, for: Map` indexes as `Size.Map`, the module it compiles to, and the functions inside it are recorded under that name instead of appearing as stray top-level functions. `defexception` records the module's struct the same way `defstruct` does.
  - **Zig:** named tests, both `test "adds numbers" {` and `test namedTest {`, index as `test`, including tests written inside a struct.
  - **Terraform:** `check`, `ephemeral`, `import`, `moved`, and `removed` blocks index, named by their Terraform address where they have one (`check.health`, `ephemeral.random_password.db`).
  - **SQL:** `CREATE UNLOGGED TABLE`, `CREATE GLOBAL TEMPORARY TABLE`, `CREATE OR REPLACE TABLE`, `CREATE SEQUENCE`, SQL Server's `CREATE OR ALTER` procedures, functions, views, and triggers, and its short `CREATE PROC` spelling all index.
  - **PowerShell:** `workflow` and DSC `configuration` blocks index as named commands.
  - **R:** functions assigned with `<<-`, `setGeneric` generics, `setRefClass` classes, and R6 classes (`Account <- R6Class(...)`) index.

  Text inside strings, heredocs, and comments still stays out of the index. Changed in [src/languages/elixir.ts](src/languages/elixir.ts), [src/languages/zig.ts](src/languages/zig.ts), [src/languages/terraform_idx.ts](src/languages/terraform_idx.ts), [src/languages/sql_idx.ts](src/languages/sql_idx.ts), [src/languages/powershell_idx.ts](src/languages/powershell_idx.ts), and [src/languages/r.ts](src/languages/r.ts). Upgrading reindexes: the parser fingerprint moved, so already-indexed files in these languages pick up the new symbols on next touch. No `install` re-run needed.

- **Kotlin type aliases now index.** `typealias NodeSet = Set<Node>` now indexes as a `type` symbol, so `token-goat symbol NodeSet` and `token-goat read "Graph.kt::NodeSet"` find it. It used to produce no symbol at all. Kotlin only allows a type alias at the top level, so that is the only place [src/languages/kotlin.ts](src/languages/kotlin.ts) looks for one. Upgrading reindexes: this changes what the Kotlin extractor extracts, so the parser fingerprint moved and already-indexed files self-heal on next touch. No `install` re-run needed.

- **Swift type aliases, associated types, and macro declarations now index.** `typealias Callback = (Int) -> Void`, a protocol's `associatedtype Item`, and a Swift 5.9 `macro stringify<T>(...)` declaration now index, the first two as `type` and the macro as `macro`. None of them uses `func`, `var`, or `let`, so [src/languages/swift.ts](src/languages/swift.ts) used to skip all three. A type alias inside a struct or protocol is recorded under that type. One declared inside a function body is a local and stays out, and so does anything written inside a `"""` string. Upgrading reindexes: this changes what the Swift extractor extracts, so the parser fingerprint moved and already-indexed files self-heal on next touch. No `install` re-run needed.

- **Dart typedefs now index, and a function typedef no longer creates a symbol named `Function`.** `typedef IntList = List<int>;` produced no symbol. `typedef Compare<T> = int Function(T a, T b);` produced one symbol, and it was the wrong one: a top-level function called `Function`. Both forms now index under the alias name as `type`, and so does the older `typedef int Compare(Object a, Object b);`. Changed in [src/languages/dart.ts](src/languages/dart.ts). Upgrading reindexes: this changes what the Dart extractor extracts, so the parser fingerprint moved and already-indexed files self-heal on next touch. No `install` re-run needed.

- **A Scala 3 type written without braces now indexes its members.** Scala 3 lets a declaration open its body with a trailing colon and indentation instead of `{`, and that is how current Scala is written. The extractor tracked a body only by counting braces, so `object Greeter:` produced the object name and nothing else: every `val`, `var`, `def`, and nested type under it was dropped, and `token-goat outline` on such a file came back nearly empty. A declaration that ends in a colon and opens no brace on its own line now carries its own indentation extent in [src/languages/scala.ts](src/languages/scala.ts), ending at the first line indented no further than the declaration itself, and its direct members are the lines sitting at the body column. Deeper lines stay what they are: the expression under `def greet(name: String) =` is part of that method, not a member beside it. Text inside a `"""` literal is still data, so a declaration written out inside one is not indexed. Brace-delimited Scala is unchanged, including a type declared right after an indented body. Upgrading reindexes: this changes what the Scala extractor extracts, so the parser fingerprint moved and already-indexed files self-heal on next touch. No `install` re-run needed.

- **PHP enum cases now index.** `enum Suit: string { case Hearts = 'H'; }` used to yield the type `Suit` alone, so `token-goat symbol Hearts` found nothing and `token-goat read "Suit.php::Hearts"` had no handle to return. Each case is now indexed as a constant of its enum, for both the pure form (`case Hearts;`) and the backed form (`case Hearts = 'H';`), in [src/languages/php.ts](src/languages/php.ts). A `switch` arm is not a case: the matcher requires the terminator an enum case takes, and it fires only directly inside an `enum` body, so `case Suit::Hearts:` inside one of the enum's own methods stays what it is. Upgrading reindexes: this changes what the PHP extractor extracts, so the parser fingerprint moved and already-indexed files self-heal on next touch. No `install` re-run needed.

- **The Nx and Meson filters now report every line they drop.** Nx task headers (`> nx run app:build`) are dropped on a passing run and past the first five on a failing one, and the `[token-goat: ...]` summary now gives that count. Meson's `Found ninja`, `Found cmake`, and `Found pkg-config` discovery lines are counted the same way. Both were removed without any note before, so a reader could not tell how much of the run was hidden. Changed in [src/tool_filters/build.ts](src/tool_filters/build.ts). This takes effect on the next command; no reindex is needed.

- **A shortened Gradle stack trace now says so, and the Gradle filter counts what it drops.** The filter keeps the first ten frames of a stack trace and used to discard the rest with no marker, so a cut trace read as one that ended on its tenth frame. It also dropped task-progress lines, downloads, daemon messages, build scan notices, deprecation warnings, and per-test result lines without leaving any trace of the count, which made it the only build filter in [src/tool_filters/build.ts](src/tool_filters/build.ts) that reported nothing about its own work. Frames past the cap are now replaced by a line naming how many were suppressed, at the point they were cut, and each drop category ends up in the usual `[token-goat: ...]` summary. Passed and skipped test lines are counted apart, so a run with skipped tests cannot read as a run where they all passed. `BUILD SUCCESSFUL`, `BUILD FAILED`, the failure block, error lines, and failing task lines are kept as before.

- **A clean `psalm` run came back with its "No errors found!" line removed, and INFO-level findings never arrived at all.** The psalm branch of [src/tool_filters/linters.ts](src/tool_filters/linters.ts) sorted lines with two regexes that disagreed. One of them listed `No errors` and `Found N errors` as progress chatter, so the single line a reader runs psalm to see was swapped for an anonymous "dropped N progress/info lines" note, leaving a clean run with nothing at all to show and a failing run with no count. The same regex matched a leading `INFO:` before the diagnostic branch could look at it, so every INFO finding was discarded and that branch's own INFO severity was unreachable. Verdict lines now pass through untouched and INFO findings are kept and grouped by issue type alongside ERROR and FATAL. This takes effect on the next command; no reindex is needed.

- **`redis-cli` SCAN replies now compress, keeping the cursor and every key.** The SCAN detector looked for `1) (integer) 0`. Redis returns the cursor as a string, so redis-cli prints it `1) "17"`. The detector never matched. A large scan fell through to the generic list path instead, which took the cursor for a key and printed it as one. The detector now reads the real shape. The cursor is kept verbatim, which matters because a non-zero value is what a caller passes back to continue the walk. The first key, which redis-cli prints on the same line as the outer `2)` index, is no longer skipped. Lines the branch does not recognize are reported with a count instead of vanishing. This takes effect on the next command; no reindex is needed.

- **`helm list` keeps a release whose status is not healthy.** The helm table kept the first ten rows and dropped the rest, and helm sorts by name, so which releases survived had nothing to do with which ones were worth looking at: a `failed` or `pending-upgrade` release sorting past the tenth row disappeared. `helm list` now shares the row budget that `kubectl get` and `aws ... --output table` already use, which takes rows carrying a not-ready status first and fills the remainder from the top. Helm 3 writes its statuses in lower case, so those spellings joined the shared not-ready vocabulary in [src/tool_filters/helpers.ts](src/tool_filters/helpers.ts). This takes effect on the next command; no reindex is needed. Changed in [src/tool_filters/containers.ts](src/tool_filters/containers.ts).

- **`apt-get update` output no longer keeps every `Hit:` line verbatim.** `Get:` lines collapsed to a count. `Hit:` lines printed in full, though they are the same shape and say only that an index is already current. Against an up-to-date sources list nearly every line is a `Hit:`, so the filter was collapsing the few and keeping the many. Both now collapse to their own counts. `Ign:` and `Err:` are apt's diagnostic outcomes and still arrive whole, along with the `Fetched` summary. This takes effect on the next command; no reindex is needed. Changed in [src/tool_filters/misc.ts](src/tool_filters/misc.ts).

- **A long `sqlite3` result came back without its last rows, and `sqlite3 -json` output came back without its closing bracket.** The compressor for `sqlite3` keeps a sample of a result set longer than 20 rows, and it took that sample from the front only. An `order by` puts the row the query was written to find at the end, so the answer was the part that got cut, and `-json` closes its array on the last line, so what came back no longer parsed as JSON. The sample now comes from both ends: the first five rows, a line naming the full row count, then the last five. A real 200-row result is still 95% smaller, and the final row now survives. This takes effect on the next command; no reindex is needed. Changed in [src/tool_filters/misc.ts](src/tool_filters/misc.ts).

- **A skipped `R CMD check` was reported as a passing one.** The compressor for `R` collapses the checks that report OK into a single count. It also treated a check reporting SKIPPED as an OK one, which dropped the line and inflated the count, so a report saying the package's tests never ran read as a report saying they passed. A SKIPPED line is now kept in full and the count covers only the checks that actually reported OK. This takes effect on the next command; no reindex is needed. Changed in [src/tool_filters/languages.ts](src/tool_filters/languages.ts).

- **A command whose text mentioned "logs", "pipeline" or "workflow" was compressed as a build log, whatever it really was.** Token-goat picks a compressor by looking at the command. Most compressors are chosen by the program name, but the one for continuous integration logs is chosen by keyword, and it was registered ahead of three whole groups of name-matched compressors. So `ls -la logs`, `grep -rn "workflow" .github` and a code search for `pipeline(` were all taken off the compressor built for them and handed to the one for build logs, which does not know what a directory listing or a search result looks like. That compressor now sits behind every compressor chosen by program name, so each of those commands reaches the one that understands it and gets the shorter output that compressor was written to produce. A 60-entry `ls -la logs` now comes back as 656 bytes instead of 3,389, ending with a line naming how many entries were left out and of what type. Genuine build log commands are unaffected. This takes effect on the next command; no reindex is needed. Changed in [src/tool_filters/dispatch.ts](src/tool_filters/dispatch.ts).

- **`token-goat index` kept serving search results built by an older embedding model.** Token-goat records which model and inference runtime produced the vectors in an index, and discards them when that changes, because vectors from two different stacks cannot be compared. That check only ran while a file was being embedded. The per-file freshness check came first, and it looks at content alone, so on an unchanged project every file was read as current and the check was never reached. Upgrade the `onnxruntime-node` runtime and `semantic` kept answering from the previous stack's vectors, while the message that reports the change told people to run `token-goat index`, which did nothing. Both the `index` command and the background indexer now compare the stack before they look at any file, so the vectors are rebuilt on the next run. After upgrading, the first `token-goat index` re-embeds each affected project once and prints a line saying so. Existing installs do not need to re-run `install`. Changed in [src/cli.ts](src/cli.ts) and [src/worker.ts](src/worker.ts).

- **Raising `indexing.large_file_symbol_only_kb` did not bring the files it admitted into `semantic`.** A file over that size keeps its symbols but is not embedded, and [src/parser.ts](src/parser.ts) recorded that skip by stamping the file's plain content hash, which is the same value a real embedding writes. So once the threshold went up, the freshness check still read every previously skipped file as already embedded, and neither the background indexer nor `token-goat index` ever went back for them. The index reported itself current while `semantic` stayed blind to that content until someone edited the file. `token-goat doctor` recommends raising this setting when embedding coverage is low, so its advice did not work as written. The skip now records the threshold it was made under, so changing the threshold re-opens the decision. That matches the markers already used for embeddings being switched off and for the optional model not being installed. Raise the value and the next index run embeds the newly admitted files, with no `--force` needed. Upgrading reindexes every project. Existing installs do not need to re-run `install`. Changed in [src/parser.ts](src/parser.ts), [src/worker.ts](src/worker.ts), and [src/cli.ts](src/cli.ts).

- **A `"""` in the middle of a line ended an Elixir heredoc or a Swift multi-line string early, and the example code inside it was indexed as real declarations.** Both languages close such a literal only on a delimiter that begins its own line, with nothing but the indentation in front of it. The closing-delimiter search in [src/languages/common.ts](src/languages/common.ts) accepted one anywhere on the line, so a body line that happened to contain three quotes ended the literal there and everything below it was parsed as ordinary source. Doc bodies are where this showed up: an Elixir `@moduledoc` or a Swift doc string that quotes a snippet produced a function name the file never declares, while the real function under the literal disappeared and the enclosing module or struct came back as its signature line alone. Each language now records where its closer may sit, next to the existing run-length rule, so a new language cannot be added until someone decides. Elixir and Swift require the delimiter to begin the line, indented or not, and the Swift rule covers the extended form (`#"""`) too. PHP heredocs and PowerShell here-strings keep the placement rules they already had, and Kotlin, Scala, Dart, C#, and R still close on the next delimiter wherever it falls, which is what their specifications say. Upgrading reindexes every project, so fabricated symbols disappear and the real ones come back on the next index. Existing installs do not need to re-run `install`.

- **A run of quotes longer than the delimiter mis-paired a Kotlin, Scala, or Dart multi-line string, and the declarations after it were cut short or lost.** Kotlin and Scala end a `"""` literal at the last three quotes of a trailing run, so `"""a""""` is the string `a"`. Both walks that decide where a symbol ends, and the line-by-line pass that blanks multi-line strings before extraction, looked for the closing delimiter with a plain text search. That search finds the first three quotes of a four-quote run and leaves the fourth behind as code, and the stray quote then opens a string that runs to the end of the file. A Kotlin or Scala class came back as its signature line alone, and where the literal shared a line with a declaration, that declaration was blanked out and never indexed. Dart reads the same run the other way around: its grammar ends the literal at the first three quotes, and adjacent string literals join, so `'''a''''''b'''` is two literals spelling `ab`. The line-by-line pass gave Dart the Kotlin reading, swallowed the second literal's opening delimiter, and left the rest of the file inside a string, so a Dart file with a class on either side of one indexed nothing at all. The rule now lives in one place, [src/languages/common.ts](src/languages/common.ts), and each language names which quotes of a longer run close its literal. C# raw strings, whose delimiter length is set by the opening run, read the same as before. Kotlin, Scala, and Dart files already in the index reindex on next touch. No `install` re-run needed.

- **The SQL adapter indexed a table whose name escapes a quote under a name the file never declares.** Every SQL dialect escapes the closing delimiter of a delimited identifier by doubling it: `""` inside a `"..."` name, a doubled backtick inside a `` `...` `` name in MySQL, and `]]` inside a `[...]` name in SQL Server. [src/languages/sql_idx.ts](src/languages/sql_idx.ts) read the first half of that escape as the closing delimiter, so `CREATE TABLE "a.""b".c` stopped at `"a."` and was indexed as `a.`, while `token-goat symbol` found nothing under the real name. The same gap truncated a backtick-quoted name to its first part and reduced a bracketed one to a single letter. A fabricated symbol is worse than a missing one, since the index looks populated while `symbol` answers with a name that is not in the file. The identifier pattern now accepts a doubled delimiter, the walk that splits a qualified name on its dots stays inside the identifier across a doubled one, and the step that removes the delimiters collapses the doubled pair back to the one character it stands for, so the name above is stored as `a."b.c`. Ordinary unquoted and singly quoted names are unchanged. Upgrading reindexes every project, so a fabricated name disappears on the next index. Changed in [src/languages/sql_idx.ts](src/languages/sql_idx.ts). Existing installs do not need to re-run `install`.

- **A bash here document or a PowerShell here-string holding a brace cut the enclosing function short, and a here-document body could be indexed as real code.** Both are the same shape as the R, PHP, and Swift forms described below: the opening text names the word that closes them, and everything in between is verbatim. Bash accepts any shell word as that delimiter, quoted in whole or in part, so `cat <<\EOF` and `cat <<'END-OF-FILE'` close on a line reading `EOF` and `END-OF-FILE` respectively. The indexer matched only a bare or wholly quoted delimiter made of letters, digits, and underscores, so neither of those forms registered as opening a body at all. Every line of the body was then read as shell code: a `}` in it closed the enclosing function early, a `NAME=value` line in it was indexed as a real variable, and the functions declared below it went missing. The delimiter word is now read the way the shell reads it, as one shared rule in [src/languages/common.ts](src/languages/common.ts), and the word after quote removal is carried along so a body can only be ended by its own closer. The left shift `<<` and the here string `<<<` still open nothing. PowerShell's `@"` to `"@` and `@'` to `'@` here-strings were already blanked while the adapter hunted for declarations, but not by the later pass that widens a declaration to its whole body, so a `}` or an unpaired `"` inside one collapsed the function around it to its header line. That pass now blanks here-strings first, the same way it already did for PHP and Swift. An array `@(`, a hash `@{`, a splat `@args`, and a string ending in `@` still open nothing. Changed in [src/languages/bash_idx.ts](src/languages/bash_idx.ts) and [src/parser.ts](src/parser.ts). Existing installs need no re-run of `token-goat install`, but the index has to be rebuilt for the corrected spans to appear: run `token-goat index --force`.

- **An R raw character constant, a PHP heredoc, or a Swift extended string holding a bracket cut the enclosing symbol short.** R, PHP, and Swift write three more of the literal forms whose closing text is chosen by the opening text rather than fixed in advance: R writes `r"(...)"` (with optional dash padding, as in `r"---(...)---"`), PHP writes `<<<EOT`, and Swift writes `#"..."#` with any number of `#` on each side, plus `#"""` to `"""#` for the multi-line form. All three hold verbatim text, which is the reason to reach for them, and verbatim text is free to contain an unpaired `"`. The walks that decide where a symbol ends read raw source one character at a time and knew only the fixed-delimiter forms, so they re-paired the quotes inside such a literal and then counted the next `)` or `}` in its text as a real delimiter. An R function whose default argument held one came out as its signature line alone, so `token-goat read "file.R::f"` returned one line of a function that is three, and a `setClass` call shrank the same way. A PHP method whose heredoc body contained a `}` ended on that line, and the class around it ended there too, taking every later member out of its scope. Both walks now read the literal through the same shared rule the adapters already use to find declarations: R's raw constant is recognized once, in [src/languages/common.ts](src/languages/common.ts), and both R's parameter-list scan in [src/languages/r.ts](src/languages/r.ts) and the brace scan behind it call it, while PHP's span walk in [src/parser.ts](src/parser.ts) now blanks every multi-line-capable literal before walking braces, which is the same text the PHP adapter sees when it hunts for declarations. Swift's extended delimiters join that same shared rule. It carries the opening hash run along, so a two-hash string can only be ended by a two-hash closer, and it reads a backslash inside one as plain text rather than an escape, which is what Swift specifies. A Swift method holding `#"a"b } c"#` used to stop on that line and take the class around it down to its own header line; it now spans its whole body, and `#available`, `#selector`, and `#file` still read as the compiler directives they are. Existing installs need no re-run of `token-goat install`, but the index has to be rebuilt for the corrected spans to appear: run `token-goat index --force`.

- **The R adapter read the inside of a multi-line character constant as real code.** R strings run across lines in both of their spellings: the ordinary `"..."` and `'...'` form, and the raw `r"(...)"` form R 4.0 added for text that is full of quotes and backslashes. Embedded SQL, Shiny templates, and code samples are the usual reason to write one. [src/languages/r.ts](src/languages/r.ts) tracked no string state at all, so every line of a string body was parsed as ordinary source, and a body line such as `helper <- function(x) {` produced a symbol for a function the file never defines. A fabricated symbol is worse than a missing one, since the index looks populated while `symbol` answers with a name that is not there. The adapter now carries multi-line string state across lines through the same shared machinery the PHP, Kotlin, Swift, C#, Scala, and Dart adapters use, extended with R's two opener kinds: an ordinary quote left open at end of line, and the raw form with its `(`, `[`, and `{` delimiters and its dash-padded variants such as `r"---(...)---"`. A constant that opens and closes on one line is deliberately left visible, because that is where `setClass("Point", ...)` and `setMethod("show", ...)` carry their own names. A `#` comment and a backtick-quoted name such as `` `my var` `` no longer open a string either. R files already in the index reindex on next touch. No `install` re-run needed.

- **The Scala and Dart adapters read the inside of a multi-line string as real code.** Both languages write long text in a triple-quoted string (`"""..."""` in Scala, `'''...'''` or `"""..."""` in Dart), and embedded SQL, templates, and code samples are the normal reason to reach for one. Neither [src/languages/scala.ts](src/languages/scala.ts) nor [src/languages/dart.ts](src/languages/dart.ts) tracked that a string was open, so every line of its body was parsed as ordinary source. A body line such as `class Job {` produced a symbol named `Job` that exists nowhere in the file, and a stray brace inside the string shifted the brace depth, which reparented or dropped the real declarations that followed. A fabricated symbol is worse than a missing one, since the index looks populated while `symbol` answers with a name the file never declares. Both adapters now carry multi-line string state across lines, the same way the PHP, Kotlin, Swift, C#, and Elixir adapters already did, so string content is blanked before any matcher sees it. A string that opens and closes on one line still leaves the rest of that line as code, and an ordinary single-quoted Dart string is still an ordinary string. Upgrading reindexes every project, so previously fabricated symbols disappear on the next index. Changed in [src/languages/common.ts](src/languages/common.ts), [src/languages/scala.ts](src/languages/scala.ts), and [src/languages/dart.ts](src/languages/dart.ts). Existing installs do not need to re-run `install`.

- **The Apex and PHP adapters indexed nothing in a file that spelled a keyword with a capital letter.** Both languages ignore keyword case, so `Public class AccountService` and `Class Repo` are ordinary code, and both turn up in real projects. Every matcher in [src/languages/apex.ts](src/languages/apex.ts) and [src/languages/php.ts](src/languages/php.ts) accepted lowercase only. A capitalized declaration failed to match, taking the type with it and leaving every member inside it unscoped. `Trigger AccountTrigger ON Account` came out worse than missing: the trigger vanished and a symbol named after the Salesforce object stood in its place. Keywords now match in any case. Names do not change: `Public class fooBar` still indexes `fooBar`, and a keyword sitting in a comment, a string, or a file path is still just text. The keyword that names a symbol's kind is lowercased before use, so an `Interface` declaration stays an interface instead of landing under a kind nothing queries for. Upgrading reindexes: this changes what the Apex and PHP extractors extract, so the parser fingerprint moved and already-indexed files self-heal on next touch. No `install` re-run needed.

- **The PHP adapter invented a constant named after a type and lost the real one.** A PHP 8.3 typed class constant puts the type between the `const` keyword and the name, and `CONST_RE` in [src/languages/php.ts](src/languages/php.ts) had no slot for it, so `final public const int LIMIT = 5;` indexed a constant called `int` and dropped `LIMIT`. A fabricated symbol is worse than a missing one: `symbol LIMIT` found nothing while the index looked populated. The same missing slot hid every nullable or union typed constant (`const ?string TAG`) outright. Two matchers beside it had the same gap: `PROP_RE` accepted neither PHP 8.4's asymmetric visibility (`public private(set) string $name;`) nor the `abstract` and `final` property modifiers that arrived with property hooks, nor a namespaced or intersection type, and `METHOD_RE` missed a return-by-reference declaration (`public function &alpha()`). All three now accept the full declaration form and still require the name they capture to be a name. Upgrading reindexes: this changes what the PHP extractor extracts, so the parser fingerprint moved and already-indexed PHP files self-heal on next touch. No `install` re-run needed.

- **Declaration modifiers missing from the C#, Kotlin, Scala, and Swift adapters dropped or renamed whole declarations.** A modifier absent from a matcher's alternation costs more than the modifier: the header matches nothing, no scope frame opens, and every member inside the body goes unindexed too. [src/languages/csharp.ts](src/languages/csharp.ts) left `new` out of its type-header list, so a nested `public new class Inner` and its methods vanished. [src/languages/kotlin.ts](src/languages/kotlin.ts) left `external` and `inline` out of its `val` list, losing a Kotlin/JS `external val` and an `inline val` with a custom getter, while the function matcher beside it accepted both words. [src/languages/scala.ts](src/languages/scala.ts) accepted neither the qualified access forms `private[this]`, `private[pkg]`, and `protected[pkg]` nor the Scala 3 soft modifiers `open`, `inline`, `transparent`, and `infix`, so an `open class` and every `inline def` in a file indexed as nothing. [src/languages/swift.ts](src/languages/swift.ts) left `nonisolated` out of its type-header list, and, separately, read `class func alpha()` as a type declaration named `func`: that invented a symbol, opened a frame for it that mis-scoped everything after it, and hid the real method. `class var`, `class let`, and `class subscript` had the same shape. A new per-keyword guard feeds one legal declaration through the real extractor for every modifier these four adapters list, so the next missing alternative cannot hide behind the siblings that still match. Kotlin, Scala, Swift, and C# files already in the index reindex on next touch. No `install` re-run needed.

- **The Kotlin adapter dropped any class, interface, or object introduced by `external`, `final`, or `inline`, along with every member declared inside it.** `CLASS_HEADER_RE` in [src/languages/kotlin.ts](src/languages/kotlin.ts) left those three modifiers out of its alternation, while `FUN_RE` in the same file already accepted `external` and `final`. An unmatched header pushes no class frame, so `external interface Console { fun log(message: String) }` yielded neither the interface nor the function, and the same went for a Kotlin/JS `external object`, an explicitly `final class`, and the pre-1.5 `inline class` spelling of `value class`. A file of nine declarations indexed three. All three modifiers now match, and members resolve to the right parent. Kotlin files already in the index reindex on next touch. No `install` re-run needed.

- **The Scala adapter lost every declaration that followed a brace-free `sealed trait` or `sealed abstract class`, the two halves of the standard Scala algebraic-data-type pairing.** A type declaration with no `{` on its line never gets a brace to mark its body as open, so its scope frame stayed on the stack for the rest of the file. Once that happened, the nesting check rejected every later class, object, trait, enum, `def`, `val`, and `var`, and the first brace that did arrive was charged to the stale frame, so that sibling's members were filed under the wrong parent. A file opening with `sealed trait Op` followed by its case classes indexed the trait and nothing else. The same leak inside an `object` body wiped out the rest of that object and everything after it. The pop that clears such a frame was spelled for the `case class` and `case object` forms only; it is now a general sweep that clears a finished brace-free frame whenever the next declaration starts, with an open-parenthesis count so a constructor parameter list spread over several lines is not mistaken for a finished declaration. Changed in [src/languages/scala.ts](src/languages/scala.ts). Upgrading reindexes: this changes what `extractScala` returns for any already-indexed Scala file, so the parser fingerprint moved and those files self-heal on next touch. No re-run of `token-goat install` is needed.

- **A fresh `token-goat install`, or a re-run of an existing one, wrote a hook matcher covering only Read and Grep for PreToolUse and only Read for PostToolUse, silently dropping every other tool's hooks: Bash, Write, Edit, Glob, WebFetch, WebSearch, Agent, Skill, BashOutput, TaskOutput, ExitPlanMode, and MCP tool calls.** The narrowed matcher is derived from the live hook-handler registry, which is only populated once every hook handler module has been imported. `install`'s own command handler never imports that module set: it only pulls in the two modules a couple of unrelated CLI commands happen to need, so the registry it read from was almost empty at install time. This affected every fresh install and, because the installer re-narrows an already-installed entry on every subsequent run, silently downgraded a working existing install's matcher on the next `token-goat install`. Fixed by importing the full hook-handler module set before computing the matcher, the same way the `hook` command already does for its own reasons. Changed in [src/cli.ts](src/cli.ts). Existing installs need a re-run of `token-goat install` to restore full hook coverage; no reindex needed.

- **Gemini CLI's `install --gemini` never wired a hook for its own `write_file` tool on `BeforeTool`, or its `grep_search`/`search_file_content` tools on `AfterTool`.** The installer's per-event tool list was a hand-maintained set that had already gone stale twice before (see the `Glob` and `WebSearch` entries below); this is the same failure a third time, on Write's pre-hook and Grep's post-hook (which also does secret redaction on Grep output). The gate is now derived directly from the live hook-handler registry instead of a hand-maintained list, so a future handler addition can no longer be missed the same way. Changed in [src/bridges/gemini_install.ts](src/bridges/gemini_install.ts). Existing installs need a re-run of `token-goat install --gemini` to pick up the new matchers; no reindex needed.

- **Codex's image-viewing tool never triggered image shrinking, session read tracking, or any other Read-scoped hook.** The Codex-to-internal tool name table had no entry for `view_image`, the only tool name the `view_image|Bash` hook matcher actually wires for image reads, so it reached every Read-only handler under its raw, unmapped name and matched none of them. An oversized image opened through Codex passed through completely unshrunk. Added the missing `view_image` -> `Read` entry. Changed in [src/hooks_cli.ts](src/hooks_cli.ts).

- **Codex's shell tool, which its own hook payloads name `shell`, was never mapped to token-goat's internal `Bash` tool name.** The Codex-to-internal tool name table only had a `bash` key, which no real Codex payload ever sends (confirmed against this repo's own Codex user-facing guidance text, which already calls the tool `shell`); every real Codex shell call fell through unmapped, so bash-output compression, bash-output recall caching, and Bash-scoped hint tracking never ran on Codex shell calls. Added a `shell` -> `Bash` entry alongside the existing `bash` entry. Changed in [src/hooks_cli.ts](src/hooks_cli.ts). Existing installs do not need a re-run of `token-goat install`: this is a payload-mapping fix, not a hook-wiring change.

- **The Codex `config.toml` matcher that decides whether Codex even calls token-goat's hook script for a shell command named its internal `Bash` tool name instead of Codex's own native `shell` tool name.** The matcher string is written verbatim into `config.toml` and matched by Codex against its own native tool names before it ever invokes the hook command, the same way `apply_patch` and `web_search` (the other two entries in the same list) already are; `view_image|Bash` meant the shell half of that alternation matched nothing a real Codex install ever sends, so every Codex shell call skipped the hook entirely regardless of the tool-name-mapping fix above, which only affects payloads that already reached the hook. Changed the matcher to `view_image|shell|bash`, keeping `bash` as a harmless fallback alternative. Changed in [src/bridges/codex_install.ts](src/bridges/codex_install.ts). Existing installs need a re-run of `token-goat install --codex`: the matcher string also feeds the `trusted_hash` Codex stores in `[hooks.state]`, so an old install's stored hash no longer matches and Codex would otherwise treat the stale entry as untrusted; no reindex needed.

- **Following the re-run instruction directly above, on a real existing Codex install, left `token-goat` reporting the Codex integration as not installed, permanently.** The old `view_image|Bash` hook group was never removed on re-install, since the entry-stripping step only ever compares against the matcher currently being written, so it survived at its old array position while the three current matchers appended after it; the installed-check then read the `[hooks.state]` trust key from a fixed array position instead of the entry's real one, so it looked up the wrong key from that point on and reported the integration missing no matter how many more times `install --codex` was run. Re-running `install --codex` now also strips a dead group whose matcher no longer appears in the current matcher list and rewrites every `[hooks.state]` key to the entry's real position, so a re-run correctly reports as installed again. Changed in [src/bridges/codex_install.ts](src/bridges/codex_install.ts) and [src/bridges/matcher_group.ts](src/bridges/matcher_group.ts); no reindex needed.

- **A Codex `PreCompact`, `UserPromptSubmit`, or `SubagentStop` hook entry sitting inside a config group that carries a `matcher` key was reported as not installed, permanently.** These three events are wired without a matcher, but a hand edit or another tool can leave token-goat's entry inside a group that has one. Both halves of `install --codex` look at every group regardless of `matcher`, so such an entry was written, trusted, and then treated as already present on every later run, while the installed-check demanded a group with no `matcher` at all and so never saw it. So `token-goat uninstall` stayed quiet about the Codex integration instead of pointing at `uninstall --codex`, and the hooks stayed behind. The installed-check now scans every group for these three events, matching what the writer already does. A re-run of `install --codex` is not needed: the check now reads the existing config correctly. No reindex needed. Changed in [src/bridges/codex_install.ts](src/bridges/codex_install.ts) and [src/bridges/matcher_group.ts](src/bridges/matcher_group.ts).

- **Re-installing over a Codex config holding two or more groups under retired matchers left a stale trust hash behind in `[hooks.state]`.** The cleanup step removes such groups from the array and records each stripped entry's state key for deletion, but it read that key from the group's current position while its own removals were shifting positions down, so every group after the first removal recorded a key one slot short. The first key was recorded twice and the last one not at all. Keys are now recorded against each group's original position, so every stripped entry's hash is deleted. Changed in [src/bridges/codex_install.ts](src/bridges/codex_install.ts). Run `install --codex` once to clear an already-orphaned hash. No reindex needed.

- **Gemini CLI's `install --gemini` never wired a hook for its own `glob` tool, so the repeated-glob dedup hint was completely dead there.** The installer derives which tool names get a hook matcher from a fixed list of internal tools known to have a registered handler, and that list was written before `Glob` had one; when `hooks_glob.ts` added `Glob`'s pre/post handlers later, nobody updated the installer's list to match, so `settings.json` never got a matcher for Gemini's `glob` tool at all and a real Gemini glob call never even reached the hook subprocess. Added `Glob` to both lists. Changed in [src/bridges/gemini_install.ts](src/bridges/gemini_install.ts). Existing installs need a re-run of `token-goat install --gemini` to pick up the new matcher; no reindex needed.

- **The pre-compaction session manifest could silently drop a file's whole entry when two sibling subagents both surgically read it.** `mergeManifestFiles` in `src/hooks_compact.ts` built the merged file entry from an explicit field list that never carried `symbols_read` forward, so a file read via `token-goat read "file::symbol"` by two different subagents lost its symbol list on the second merge and matched none of the manifest's read, edited, or surgical-read filters, vanishing from the pre-compaction summary entirely even though the manifest budget still credited it as narrowly read. Now the merge unions and deduplicates `symbols_read` across colliding entries. Changed in [src/hooks_compact.ts](src/hooks_compact.ts). No reindex or install re-run needed.

- **Gemini CLI's search tool, `google_web_search`, was routed to token-goat's WebFetch handler instead of its dedicated WebSearch handler, so search-result dedup and the search-side of URL policy never ran on Gemini.** WebFetch's handler only acts on a call carrying a `url` key; a search call carries `query` instead, so every `google_web_search` call silently passed through untouched no matter what it searched for. The WebFetch mapping traced back to the same installer gate gap described above: the gate never included `WebSearch` as a wireable tool, so mapping the search tool to WebFetch was the only way it got a hook matcher at all, at the cost of the hook always doing nothing once it fired. Remapped `google_web_search` to `WebSearch` and added `WebSearch` to the installer's wireable-tool lists. Changed in [src/hooks_cli.ts](src/hooks_cli.ts) and [src/bridges/gemini_install.ts](src/bridges/gemini_install.ts). Existing installs need a re-run of `token-goat install --gemini` to pick up the new matcher; no reindex needed.
